Chromatic Homotopy — the Morava E-theories, the Nilpotence Theorem & the Periodic Table

Chromatic homotopy theory is the study of the stable homotopy category through a filtration by "heights" — a chromatic filtration that organizes phenomena by periodicity families. The central idea, due to Jack Morava, is that the stable homotopy category can be decomposed into layers governed by the moduli space of formal groups of height n. The Nilpotence theorem (Devinatz-Hopkins-Smith, 1988) and the periodicity theorem provide the foundational structural results: the former says that the MU-Hurewicz map detects nilpotence in the stable homotopy ring of a finite spectrum, while the latter guarantees the existence of v_n self-maps. The Morava K-theories K(n) and the Morava E-theories E_n serve as the local "probes" at each height. The chromatic spectral sequence assembles these layers into the homotopy groups of a general spectrum. The telescope conjecture — now known to be false — asked whether the Bousfield localization at K(n) could be realized more concretely. The interplay of these ideas leads to deep computations of the homotopy groups of spheres, the classification of thick subcategories of finite spectra, and connections to topological modular forms and the Langlands program.

"Chromatic homotopy is the systematic study of v_n-periodicity."
— Ethan Devinatz
Part 1

📏 The Nilpotence Theorem and its Consequences

Foundations
The Nilpotence Theorem, the Periodicity Theorem, and the Thick Subcategory Theorem
The Nilpotence theorem (Devinatz-Hopkins-Smith, 1988) is one of the deepest results in algebraic topology. It states: for any finite spectrum X, an element α ∈ π_*(X) maps to 0 under the MU-Hurewicz map π_*(X) → MU_*(X) iff α is nilpotent — i.e., some smash power α^{∧k} = 0. Equivalently, the kernel of the MU-Hurewicz map consists entirely of nilpotent elements. The proof uses the X(n)-construction and the chromatic filtration of the moduli space of formal groups. The Periodicity theorem (Hopkins-Smith): for any finite spectrum X and any n ≥ 1, there exists a v_n self-map f: Σ^k X → X such that K(n)_*(f) is an isomorphism and K(m)_*(f) = 0 for m ≠ n. The thick subcategory theorem (Hopkins-Smith) classifies the thick subcategories of the category of finite spectra: they are precisely the subcategories C_n of spectra of type ≥ n (those for which v_0, …, v_{n−1} vanish). The chromatic filtration is the tower of Bousfield localizations L_n with respect to K(0) ∨ … ∨ K(n). The chromatic convergence theorem states that for a finite spectrum X, the natural map X → holim_n L_n X is an equivalence.
The nilpotence theorem is the fundamental detection result: the MU-Hurewicz map sees all nilpotence in the stable homotopy of finite spectra, giving a powerful algebraic grip on a geometric phenomenon.
Part 2

🌍 Morava K-theories and E-theories

Heights
Morava K-theories K(n), Morava E-theories E_n, and Lubin-Tate Theory
For each prime p and integer 0 ≤ n ≤ ∞, the Morava K-theory K(n) is a complex oriented cohomology theory with coefficient ring K(n)_* = 𝔽_p[v_n, v_n^{-1}] where |v_n| = 2(p^n − 1). K(0) = Hℚ and K(∞) = H𝔽_p. The theory K(n) detects v_n-periodicity: a map f: X → Y is a K(n)-equivalence iff it induces an isomorphism in v_n^{-1}E(n)_*(-). The Morava E-theory E_n (also called complete Lubin-Tate theory) is the spectrum whose coefficient ring is E_n^* = W(𝔽_{p^n})[[u_1, …, u_{n-1}]][u, u^{-1}] where |u| = 2. Its formal group is the universal deformation of the Honda formal group of height n. The Goerss-Hopkins-Miller theorem endows E_n with an E_∞-ring structure and an action by the extended Morava stabilizer group G_n = O_n^× ⋊ Gal(𝔽_{p^n}/𝔽_p). The homotopy fixed points E_n^{hG_n} recover topological modular forms: E_n^{hG_n} ≃ TMF(p). The Smith-Toda complexes V(n) are finite spectra realizing the Morava K-theory of height n.
Morava K-theories and E-theories provide the local "coordinate charts" on the moduli space of formal groups, organizing stable homotopy by the height parameter.
Part 3

🧮 The Chromatic Spectral Sequence

Spectral
The Chromatic Fracture Square, the Chromatic Spectral Sequence, and Gross-Hopkins Duality
The chromatic fracture square expresses the K(n)-localization L_{K(n)}X in terms of the E(n)-localization: there is a homotopy pullback square L_{K(n)}X → L_{E(n)}X → L_{E(n-1)}X. The chromatic spectral sequence (CSS) has E_1^{p,q} = π_{p+q}(M_p ⊗ X) ⇒ π_{p+q}(L_{K(p)}X) where M_p are the monochromatic layers (the fibers of L_{E(p)}X → L_{E(p-1)}X). The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) can be organized by chromatic height: its E_2-term is the continuous cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups, naturally filtered by the chromatic filtration. The Gross-Hopkins dual I_n = Σ^{n^2-n}E_n^∨ (the Spanier-Whitehead dual of E_n) satisfies a self-duality property. The Gross-Hopkins duality formula: the Brown-Comenetz dual of K(n) is Σ^{2(p^n - 1)n}K(n). The Morava module of a spectrum X at height n is the continuous G_n-module K(n)_*(X) = E_n^*(X) ⊗_{E_n^*} K(n)_*. The chromatic decomposition of the homotopy groups of spheres is one of the great triumphs of chromatic homotopy.
The chromatic spectral sequence systematically assembles the homotopy information at each height into a computable algebraic framework, with the Morava stabilizer group governing the symmetries.
Part 4

🔥 The Telescope Conjecture and Applications

Periodicity
The Telescope Conjecture, Its Disproof, and Modern Applications
The telescope conjecture (Smith, Ravenel, 1980s) asserted that the Bousfield localization L_n^f at the wedge K(0) ∨ … ∨ K(n) on finite spectra is equivalent to smashing with the telescope T(n) of a v_n self-map on a finite type-n spectrum: L_n X ≃ X ∧ T(n). This conjecture, if true, would have dramatically simplified the chromatic picture. However, it is now known to be false in general: counterexamples were constructed by Mahowald-Ravenel-Shick and, more recently, by Barthel-David-Salch for n = 2 and p ≥ 5. The thick subcategory theorem has powerful applications to the classifying spaces of finite groups: the classification of thick subcategories of D^b(Coh(BG)). Applications to the Cohomology of the Steenrod algebra and the Adams spectral sequence: the first 61 stems of the homotopy groups of spheres have been computed (up to 2015) using chromatic methods. A modern application: the Behrens-Lawson theorem on the exotic periodicity in TMF reveals a duality between 2-adic and ∞-adic geometry in the theory of topological modular forms, linking chromatic height 2 to the moduli of elliptic curves.
The failure of the telescope conjecture shows that chromatic localization is subtler than originally hoped; yet the interplay of these ideas continues to drive progress in stable homotopy theory and its connections to number theory.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

The Thick Subcategory Theorem and Its Classification

The thick subcategory theorem, proved by Hopkins and Smith in 1998, gives a complete classification of the thick subcategories of the category of finite spectra. A thick subcategory is a full triangulated subcategory closed under retracts. The theorem states that for each integer n ≥ 0 the collection C_n of finite spectra of type ≥ n forms a thick subcategory, and every non‑zero thick subcategory of finite spectra equals C_n for a unique n. Here a finite spectrum X has type n if K(m)_*(X) = 0 for m < n and K(n)_*(X) ≠ 0, where K(m) denotes the Morava K‑theory at height m. The type of a finite spectrum is therefore detected by the Morava K‑theories, and the filtration by type coincides with the chromatic filtration on the finite subcategory.

The proof uses the nilpotence theorem to show that any map between finite spectra which is zero on all Morava K‑theories is nilpotent. From this one deduces that the Bousfield class of a finite spectrum is determined by its type, and that the thick subcategory generated by a finite spectrum of type n is exactly C_n. Consequently the lattice of thick subcategories is a chain C0 ⊃ C1 ⊃ C2 ⊃ … . This result has far‑reaching consequences: it implies the periodicity theorem (every finite spectrum of type n admits a v_n self‑map), it underlies the classification of localizing subcategories in the K(n)‑local category, and it provides the algebraic input for the telescope conjecture (which predicts that the smashing localization at C_n coincides with the telescope of a v_n self‑map). The theorem also extends to the derived category of coherent sheaves on classifying spaces of finite groups, where thick subcategories are classified by support varieties.

In practice, the thick subcategory theorem allows one to reduce many questions about finite spectra to questions about a single type n. For example, the computation of the homotopy groups of spheres in a given chromatic layer can be approached by studying the K(n)‑local category, because the inclusion C_n → Cn-1 induces an equivalence after K(n)‑localization. The theorem is also the starting point for the chromatic approach to the stable homotopy category: one builds the category from the monochromatic layers M_n = C_n / Cn+1, each of which is equivalent to the K(n)‑local category of finite spectra.

The Chromatic Convergence Theorem and Its Proof Sketch

The chromatic convergence theorem, established by Hopkins and Ravenel in 1992, asserts that for any finite spectrum X the canonical map X → holim_n L_n X is an equivalence, where L_n denotes Bousfield localization with respect to the wedge K(0) ∨ … ∨ K(n). In other words, a finite spectrum can be recovered from its chromatic localizations. The theorem is a global statement that the chromatic tower converges on finite objects, and it is the homotopical analogue of the fact that a formal group over a complete local ring is determined by its reductions modulo powers of the maximal ideal.

The proof proceeds in several stages. First one shows that the fiber of the map L_n X → Ln-1 X is the monochromatic layer M_n X, which is K(n)‑local and K(n-1)‑acyclic. The collection of these fibers forms a tower whose homotopy limit is the homotopy limit of the L_n X. Using the nilpotence theorem one proves that the map X → holim_n L_n X induces an isomorphism on all Morava K‑theories K(m) for every m. Since the Morava K‑theories form a conservative family on the category of finite spectra (a map of finite spectra is an equivalence iff it is an equivalence on all K(m)), the map must be an equivalence. The conservativity of the Morava K‑theories on finite spectra is itself a consequence of the thick subcategory theorem: if a map is zero on all K(m), its cofiber lies in every C_n, hence is zero.

A key technical ingredient is the construction of the chromatic fracture square, which expresses LK(n) X as a homotopy pullback of LE(n) X and LE(n-1) X. This square allows one to relate the K(n)‑localization to the more computable E(n)‑localization. The convergence theorem also implies that the chromatic spectral sequence, which computes the homotopy groups of the monochromatic layers, converges to the homotopy groups of X after completion at the prime p. In practice, the theorem justifies the strategy of computing stable homotopy groups by first computing them in each K(n)‑local category and then assembling the results via the chromatic spectral sequence.

Worked Example: Verifying Chromatic Convergence for a Type‑2 Complex
Let V(2) be a Smith–Toda complex of type 2 at a prime p ≥ 5. Show that the natural map V(2) → holim_n L_n V(2) is an equivalence.
The spectrum V(2) is finite of type 2, so K(m)_*(V(2)) = 0 for m < 2 and K(2)_*(V(2)) ≠ 0. For n ≥ 2 the localization L_n V(2) is equivalent to LK(2) V(2) because higher Morava K‑theories vanish on V(2). For n = 1 the localization L1 V(2) is contractible since V(2) is K(1)‑acyclic. Thus the tower L_n V(2) stabilizes at n = 2: L2 V(2) ≃ L3 V(2) ≃ … . The homotopy limit of a constant tower is the constant value, so holim_n L_n V(2) ≃ L2 V(2). The map V(2) → L2 V(2) is the K(2)‑localization map. By the nilpotence theorem, a map of finite spectra that induces an isomorphism on all Morava K‑theories is an equivalence. The localization map induces an isomorphism on K(2) (by definition of K(2)‑localization) and on K(m) for m ≠ 2 both sides are zero. Hence the map is an equivalence, establishing chromatic convergence for V(2).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks you to apply the chromatic convergence theorem, first identify the type of the finite spectrum involved. Then determine the smallest n for which L_n X is non‑trivial; the tower stabilizes there. Use the conservativity of the Morava K‑theories on finite spectra to conclude that the map to the homotopy limit is an equivalence. Remember that the theorem only holds for finite spectra; for infinite spectra the map need not be an equivalence.

The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller Theorem and E∞ Structures

The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem, proved in the early 2000s, endows each Morava E‑theory E_n with a canonical E_∞‑ring structure and an action of the extended Morava stabilizer group G_n = O_n^× ⋊ Gal(𝔽p^n/𝔽_p) by E_∞‑maps. Here O_n is the endomorphism ring of the Honda formal group of height n over 𝔽p^n. The theorem is a deep result in obstruction theory: it shows that the Lubin–Tate deformation space of the Honda formal group carries a universal deformation that is not only a formal group law but a commutative S‑algebra. The construction uses the Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory for E_∞‑structures on a given homology theory, together with the fact that the Lubin–Tate ring W(𝔽p^n)[[u1,…,un-1]] is a complete regular local ring of dimension n.

The E_∞‑structure has several crucial consequences. First, it allows one to form homotopy fixed point spectra E_nhH for any closed subgroup H ≤ G_n; these are again E_∞‑ring spectra. In particular, the homotopy fixed points under the full group G_n recover topological modular forms at the prime p: E_nhG_n ≃ TMF(p). Second, the action of G_n on E_n makes the K(n)‑local category equivalent to the category of continuous G_n‑modules over the Morava module K(n)_*(X) = E_n^*(X) ⊗E_n^* K(n)_*. This equivalence is the foundation of the algebraic approach to K(n)‑local homotopy theory, where computations are reduced to continuous group cohomology of G_n. Third, the E_∞‑structure provides power operations on E_n and on K(n), which are essential for the construction of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence and for the analysis of the chromatic spectral sequence.

The theorem also implies that the Morava E‑theories are the initial objects in the category of E_∞‑ring spectra with a formal group of height n. This universal property is used to construct maps from E_n to other E_∞‑ring spectra, for instance the map E_n → TMF arising from the universal elliptic curve. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem is therefore a bridge between the geometric world of formal groups and the algebraic world of structured ring spectra, and it underlies much of the modern computation of the homotopy groups of spheres via the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence.

The Smith–Toda Complexes and Their Role in Detecting v_n‑Periodicity

The Smith–Toda complexes V(n) are finite p‑local spectra of type n, constructed by Smith and Toda in the 1970s and 1980s. For each n ≥ 0 there exists a finite spectrum V(n) with BP_*(V(n)) = BP_*/(p, v1, …, vn-1) (with the convention V(0) = S0). The construction proceeds inductively: given V(n-1), one chooses a v_n self‑map on a suitable suspension of V(n-1) and takes its cofiber to obtain V(n). The existence of such v_n self‑maps is guaranteed by the periodicity theorem, which itself follows from the nilpotence theorem and the thick subcategory theorem.

The spectra V(n) play a central role as test objects for v_n‑periodic phenomena. A map f: X → Y of finite spectra is a K(n)‑equivalence if and only if it induces an isomorphism on [V(n), -]_* (the graded homotopy classes of maps from V(n)). Equivalently, the Bousfield class of V(n) is the same as that of K(n) on the finite subcategory. This makes V(n) a finite model for the K(n)‑local sphere: the K(n)‑localization of V(n) is equivalent to the K(n)‑local sphere LK(n) S0. Consequently, the homotopy groups of the K(n)‑local sphere can be computed as the homotopy groups of the function spectrum F(V(n), LK(n) S0), which is a finite spectrum in the K(n)‑local category.

The Smith–Toda complexes also appear in the construction of the telescope T(n) of a v_n self‑map on a type‑n spectrum. The telescope conjecture (now known to be false in general) predicted that the smashing localization at V(n) coincides with smashing with T(n). Counterexamples by Mahowald–Ravenel–Shick and later by Barthel–David–Salch show that the localization L_n^f on finite spectra is strictly larger than the telescope localization. Nevertheless, V(n) remains the canonical finite representative of chromatic height n, and its Adams–Novikov spectral sequence is the primary computational tool for the K(n)‑local homotopy groups of spheres.

The Gross–Hopkins Duality and Its Consequences for Local Duality

Gross–Hopkins duality, discovered by Gross and Hopkins in the 1990s, provides a canonical dualizing object in the K(n)‑local category. Let I_n denote the Brown–Comenetz dual of the K(n)‑local sphere LK(n) S0. Gross and Hopkins proved that I_n is equivalent to a suspension of the Spanier–Whitehead dual of the Morava E‑theory E_n: I_n ≃ Σn2-n E_n^∨. Moreover, the Brown–Comenetz dual of the Morava K‑theory K(n) itself is a suspension of K(n): IK(n) ≃ Σ2(p^n-1)n K(n). This self‑duality of K(n) reflects the fact that the Morava stabilizer group G_n is a Poincaré duality group of dimension n2.

The duality has several powerful applications. First, it identifies the K(n)‑local dual of a finite spectrum X with a suspension of the K(n)‑localization of the Spanier–Whitehead dual of X: DK(n) X ≃ Σ-n2+n LK(n) DX. This formula allows one to transfer finiteness properties across the duality. Second, it implies that the category of K(n)‑local spectra is a rigidly‑compactly generated tensor triangulated category, with the compact objects precisely the K(n)‑localizations of finite spectra. Third, Gross–Hopkins duality is the key input for the construction of the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence, which computes the homotopy groups of the K(n)‑local sphere from the continuous cohomology of G_n with coefficients in the Morava module.

In the context of the chromatic spectral sequence, Gross–Hopkins duality provides a symmetry between the E1‑page and the E_∞‑page: the monochromatic layer M_n is self‑dual up to a shift. This symmetry is used to deduce vanishing lines and to relate the homotopy groups of spheres in complementary stems. The duality also plays a role in the proof of the chromatic convergence theorem, where the identification of the fiber of L_n → Ln-1 with M_n relies on the fact that M_n is K(n)‑local and K(n-1)‑acyclic, properties that are preserved under the duality.

  • Confusing the thick subcategory theorem (classification of thick subcategories of finite spectra) with the classification of localizing subcategories in the K(n)‑local category; the latter is a different theorem (Hovey–Strickland).
  • Assuming the chromatic convergence theorem holds for all spectra; it is false for infinite spectra such as the sphere spectrum completed at p.
  • Thinking the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem gives a unique E_∞‑structure on E_n; it gives a canonical one, but other E_∞‑structures may exist.
  • Believing the Smith–Toda complexes V(n) are unique up to equivalence; they are unique up to a non‑canonical equivalence, and different choices affect the explicit v_n self‑maps.
  • Misapplying Gross–Hopkins duality by forgetting the suspension shift Σn2-n; the shift is essential for the duality to hold on the level of homotopy groups.
  • Overlooking that the telescope conjecture is false in general; many exam questions test the distinction between L_n^f and the telescope localization.
  • Neglecting the role of the Morava stabilizer group G_n as a Poincaré duality group; its cohomological dimension n2 underlies the shift in Gross–Hopkins duality.

These advanced topics form the backbone of modern chromatic homotopy theory and are essential for any serious examination of the stable homotopy category.

The Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence and Chromatic Resolution

The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) is the primary computational engine for stable homotopy groups of spheres through the lens of complex cobordism. Its E2-page is the cohomology of the Hopf algebroid (MU*, MU*MU), which by Quillen's theorem is isomorphic to the cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups Mfg. The chromatic filtration arises from the stratification of Mfg by height of formal groups: the open substack Mfg≤n of formal groups of height at most n gives rise to the E2-page of the ANSS for the E(n)-local sphere. The E2-term for the K(n)-local sphere is the continuous cohomology H*cts(Gn, En*), where Gn is the Morava stabilizer group. The chromatic spectral sequence (CSS) is obtained by filtering the ANSS by the height filtration on Mfg; its E1-page is the direct sum over n of the cohomology of the monochromatic layers Mn. The CSS converges to the E∞-page of the ANSS for the p-complete sphere. A key computational tool is the vn-periodic pattern: the vn-periodic families in the ANSS correspond to permanent cycles in the CSS that survive to the E∞-page. The work of Ravenel, Wilson, and Yagita on the E2-term of the ANSS for E(n) and K(n) established the algebraic structure of these cohomology groups as modules over the Steenrod algebra and the Morava stabilizer group. The ANSS for the sphere at p=2 has been computed through a range of stems by the work of Nakamura, Wang, and others using the chromatic resolution and the tmf-based approach.

The Lubin–Tate Deformation Theory and the Morava Stabilizer Group

Lubin–Tate theory provides the universal deformation ring of a formal group of height n over a perfect field k of characteristic p. Let Γn be a height-n formal group law over k. The universal deformation ring is isomorphic to W(k)[[u1, …, un-1]], where W(k) is the ring of Witt vectors. The Morava E-theory En has coefficient ring En0 ≅ W(𝔽pn)[[u1, …, un-1]][u±1] with |u| = -2. The Morava stabilizer group Gn = Aut(Γn) ⋊ Gal(𝔽pn/𝔽p) acts continuously on En by E∞-ring maps (Goerss–Hopkins–Miller). The group Gn is a profinite group of cohomological dimension n2; its open subgroups correspond to finite extensions of the deformation theory. The continuous cohomology H*cts(Gn, En*) computes the E2-page of the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (HFPS) for EnhGn ≃ LK(n)S0. The HFPS is the algebraic avatar of the K(n)-local Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The structure of Gn as a p-adic Lie group of dimension n2 implies that its cohomology satisfies Poincaré duality with a twist by the determinant character, which underlies Gross–Hopkins duality. The Lubin–Tate tower of covering spaces of the deformation space gives rise to the Lubin–Tate spectra En(H) for open subgroups H ⊂ Gn, which are finite Galois extensions of En in the K(n)-local category. The action of Gn on the Morava module En* is through the determinant character on the periodicity element u and through the natural action on the deformation parameters ui.

Worked Computation: Cohomological Dimension of Gn
Show that the Morava stabilizer group Gn has cohomological dimension n2 and explain how this determines the shift in Gross–Hopkins duality.
The group Gn = Aut(Γn) ⋊ Gal(𝔽pn/𝔽p) is a p-adic Lie group. The automorphism group Aut(Γn) is isomorphic to the group of units in the division algebra Dn of invariant 1/n over ℚp, which has dimension n2 over ℚp. As a p-adic Lie group, its cohomological dimension equals its dimension as a p-adic manifold, which is n2. The Galois group is finite of order n, so it does not affect the cohomological dimension. Gross–Hopkins duality states In ≃ Σn2-n En∨. The shift n2 - n arises because the dualizing module for Gn is the determinant representation det: Gn → ℤp× twisted by the cyclotomic character (which contributes -n). The Poincaré duality for Gn gives Hicts(Gn, M) ≅ Hn2-icts(Gn, M∨ ⊗ det)∨. Translating to spectra, the Brown–Comenetz dual In corresponds to the dualizing module, yielding the suspension shift n2 - n.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Lubin–Tate Data
When a question asks for the coefficient ring of En, write W(𝔽pn)[[u1,…,un-1]][u±1] with |u| = -2. For the Morava stabilizer group, state Gn = Aut(Γn) ⋊ Gal(𝔽pn/𝔽p) and note its cohomological dimension is n2. The shift in Gross–Hopkins duality is always n2 - n. Memorize the division algebra Dn of invariant 1/n as the algebraic model for Aut(Γn).

The Chromatic Fracture Square and Local-Global Principles

The chromatic fracture square is the fundamental tool for reconstructing a spectrum from its chromatic localizations. For any spectrum X and prime p, there is a homotopy pullback square (the fracture square) relating the E(n)-localization LnX, the K(n)-localization LK(n)X, and the E(n-1)-localization Ln-1X. The square takes the form: LnX → LK(n)X ↓ ↓ Ln-1X → Ln-1LK(n)X. This expresses the fact that Ln is the Bousfield localization with respect to the wedge E(n) = K(0) ∨ … ∨ K(n), and the fiber of Ln → Ln-1 is the monochromatic layer Mn = LK(n) ∘ Cn-1, where Cn-1 is the acyclization with respect to E(n-1). Iterating the fracture square gives the chromatic tower X → … → LnX → Ln-1X → … → L0X = Xℚ. The chromatic convergence theorem states that for a finite spectrum X, the natural map X → holimn LnX is an equivalence. For infinite spectra, this fails: the p-complete sphere S0p is not the homotopy limit of its chromatic localizations. The fracture square is also the starting point for the K(n)-local approach to the Segal conjecture and for the computation of the homotopy groups of spheres via the chromatic spectral sequence. In the K(n)-local category, the fracture square simplifies because Ln-1 is smashing and LK(n) is smashing; the category of K(n)-local spectra is a smashing localization of the E(n)-local category.

The Kuhn–Morava Theorem and the Action on Morava E-Theory

The Kuhn–Morava theorem (also known as the Morava change-of-rings theorem) identifies the K(n)-local category with the category of continuous Gn-spectra. More precisely, the functor X ↦ En ∧ X induces an equivalence between the K(n)-local stable homotopy category and the homotopy category of continuous Gn-spectra (spectra with a continuous action of the profinite group Gn). The inverse equivalence is given by homotopy fixed points: Y ↦ YhGn. This equivalence is symmetric monoidal and preserves compact objects: the compact objects in the K(n)-local category correspond to the finite Gn-spectra. The theorem is a consequence of the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem (which gives the E∞ action of Gn on En) and the fact that En is a faithful K(n)-local ring spectrum. The Kuhn–Morava theorem allows one to translate chromatic homotopy theory into the language of continuous group actions and Galois cohomology. For example, the Picard group of the K(n)-local category (the group of invertible objects under smash product) is isomorphic to the continuous cohomology group H1cts(Gn, En0×) ⊕ ℤ, where the ℤ factor corresponds to suspensions. The theorem also underlies the construction of the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence, which is the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of Gn on En. The action of the determinant character det: Gn → ℤp× on the periodicity element u ∈ En-2 is the key to understanding the duality in the category.

The Redshift Conjecture and Its Implications for Algebraic K-Theory

The redshift conjecture, formulated by Ausoni and Rognes, predicts that the algebraic K-theory of a chromatic height-n ring spectrum has chromatic height n+1. More precisely, if R is an E∞ ring spectrum with K(n)*R ≠ 0 but K(n+1)*R = 0, then K(n+1)*K(R) ≠ 0. This conjecture is a vast generalization of the Quillen–Lichtenbaum conjecture and the work of Quillen on the algebraic K-theory of finite fields. The evidence includes: (1) Ausoni–Rognes computation of K(ℓ) for ℓ-adic K-theory, showing redshift from height 1 to height 2; (2) the work of Clausen–Mathew–Morrow on the K-theory of E∞ rings via topological cyclic homology (TC); (3) the computation of TC(En) by Hesselholt–Madsen and others, which shows that TC(En) has height n+1. The redshift conjecture is intimately connected to the chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture and to the telescope conjecture. If true, it implies that the algebraic K-theory of the sphere spectrum S0 (which has height 0) has height 1, the K-theory of KU (height 1) has height 2, and so on. The conjecture also predicts that the K(n)-localization of K(R) for a height-n ring R is trivial, while the K(n+1)-localization is non-trivial. This has profound implications for the structure of the stable homotopy category and for the relationship between algebraic K-theory and chromatic homotopy theory. The recent disproof of the telescope conjecture by Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank does not directly affect the redshift conjecture, but it sharpens the understanding of the relationship between K(n)-local and vn-periodic phenomena.

  • Confusing the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (based on MU) with the chromatic spectral sequence (the height filtration on the ANSS); the CSS is a spectral sequence of spectral sequences.
  • Assuming the Lubin–Tate ring W(𝔽pn)[[u1, …, un-1]] is the coefficient ring of En without the periodicity element u±1 of degree -2.
  • Thinking the chromatic fracture square converges for all spectra; it only converges for finite spectra (chromatic convergence theorem), and fails for the p-complete sphere.
  • Misstating the Kuhn–Morava theorem as an equivalence with discrete Gn-spectra; the action must be continuous with respect to the profinite topology.
  • Believing the redshift conjecture is a theorem; it remains open for n ≥ 2, though strong evidence exists via topological cyclic homology.
  • Overlooking that the Morava stabilizer group Gn has a non-trivial determinant character that acts on the periodicity element u and is essential for Gross–Hopkins duality.
  • Confusing the monochromatic layer Mn = LK(n) ∘ Cn-1 with the K(n)-localization LK(n); Mn is K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic, while LK(n) is only K(n)-local.

The Chromatic Splitting Conjecture and the Structure of the K(n)-Local Sphere

The chromatic splitting conjecture, formulated by Ravenel in the 1980s, predicts a precise decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0 into a wedge of suspensions of the monochromatic layers Mk = LK(k)Ck-1S0 for k ≤ n. More explicitly, the conjecture asserts that there is an equivalence LK(n)S0 ≃ ⋁k=0n ΣdkMk for certain integers dk determined by the Gross–Hopkins duality shift. The conjecture is known for n = 1 (where it reduces to the Adams splitting of the p-complete sphere into connective and non-connective summands) and for n = 2 at primes p ≥ 5 by work of Behrens and Lawson. For n ≥ 3 it remains open and is considered one of the deepest problems in chromatic homotopy theory. The splitting is not merely additive; it is expected to be a splitting of E∞ ring spectra after suitable localization. The conjecture implies that the K(n)-local sphere is built from the monochromatic layers, which are themselves simpler: Mk is K(k)-local and K(k-1)-acyclic, and its homotopy groups are concentrated in a range determined by the Gross–Hopkins duality. The integers dk are given by dk = n2 - k2 - n + k for the standard normalization. A proof would give a complete inductive computation of π*LK(n)S0 from the monochromatic homotopy groups π*Mk. The conjecture is closely related to the chromatic fracture square and the vanishing of certain Tate cohomology groups for the Morava stabilizer group action on En. Current approaches use the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence, the algebraic duality resolution, and the theory of topological automorphic forms to attack the conjecture at higher heights.

Topological Modular Forms and the Chromatic Height 2 Picture

Topological modular forms (TMF) is a periodic ring spectrum of chromatic height 2 that interpolates between elliptic cohomology theories and the K(2)-local sphere. Constructed by Goerss, Hopkins, and Miller using the compactified moduli stack M̄ell of elliptic curves and a sheaf of E∞ ring spectra Otop, TMF fits into a chromatic fracture square relating it to its K(1)-localization (which is a version of p-adic K-theory with level structure) and its K(2)-localization (which is a wedge of Morava E-theories E2 with Galois action). The homotopy groups π*TMF are computable via a descent spectral sequence Hs(M̄ell, ωt) ⇒ πt-sTMF, where ω is the line bundle of invariant differentials. This spectral sequence collapses at a finite page and its E2-term is given by modular forms of weight t and level Γ1(N) for appropriate N. The ring π*TMF contains the ring of integral modular forms as a subring, but also contains torsion classes detected by the cohomology of the moduli stack. At the prime 2, TMF has a 576-periodic pattern in its homotopy groups after inverting the discriminant Δ; at the prime 3, the periodicity is 72. The spectrum TMF is the global sections of Otop on M̄ell, and its K(2)-localization is equivalent to the homotopy fixed points of a finite subgroup of the Morava stabilizer group G2 acting on E2. This realizes the "chromatic fracture" at height 2: TMF is the homotopy pullback of its K(1)-localization and K(2)-localization over the E(1)-localization of its K(2)-localization. The construction of TMF uses the Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory to lift the presheaf of elliptic cohomology theories to a sheaf of E∞ ring spectra, and the compactification of Mell to M̄ell is essential for the convergence of the descent spectral sequence. TMF provides the first computable example of a height-2 theory that is not a Morava E-theory, and its homotopy groups have been computed in a range by Behrens, Hill, Hopkins, Mahowald, and others using the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence and the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of the finite subgroups of G2.

Worked Computation: The Descent Spectral Sequence for TMF at p=2
Compute the E2-page of the descent spectral sequence for TMF at the prime 2 in the range 0 ≤ t-s ≤ 20, and identify the classes corresponding to the modular forms c4, c6, Δ, and the Hasse invariant.
The descent spectral sequence has E2s,t = Hs(M̄ell, ωt/2) for t even (zero for t odd). At p=2, the compactified moduli stack M̄ell has cohomological dimension 1, so s ∈ {0,1}. The line bundle ω has degree 1/2 on the stack. The ring of modular forms for SL2(ℤ) is ℤ[c4, c6, Δ-1]/(c43 - c62 = 1728Δ). At p=2, c4 has weight 4 (t=8), c6 has weight 6 (t=12), Δ has weight 12 (t=24). The Hasse invariant is the reduction of c4 mod 2, which is a section of ω4 on the supersingular locus. In the range t-s ≤ 20: E20,0 = ℤ(2) (the unit 1). E20,8 = ℤ(2)·c4. E20,12 = ℤ(2)·c6. E20,16 = ℤ(2)·c42. E20,20 = ℤ(2)·c4c6. The Hasse invariant appears in E21,8 as a class h1 (often denoted v1 in the Adams–Novikov context) which is the image of the generator of H1(M̄ell, ω4). The differential d3(c4) = 0, d3(c6) = 0, but there is a d3 hitting the class in E21,8 from a class in E20,10 which is zero, so the Hasse invariant survives to E∞ and detects an element in π7TMF. The discriminant Δ is in E20,24 which is outside the range. The resulting homotopy groups in this range: π0 = ℤ(2), π-1 = 0, π7 = ℤ/2 (Hasse invariant), π8 = ℤ(2) (c4), π11 = ℤ/2, π12 = ℤ(2) (c6), π15 = ℤ/2, π16 = ℤ(2) (c42), π19 = ℤ/2, π20 = ℤ(2) (c4c6). All odd-degree classes are 2-torsion of order 2.
Exam Strategy: TMF and the Chromatic Fracture
When asked about TMF, always mention the triple: (1) the moduli stack M̄ell and the sheaf Otop, (2) the descent spectral sequence with E2 = Hs(M̄ell, ωt), (3) the chromatic fracture square relating TMF to K(1)-local and K(2)-local data. For computations, remember that the cohomological dimension of M̄ell is 1, so the descent spectral sequence has only two rows (s=0,1). The periodicity after inverting Δ is 576 at p=2 and 72 at p=3. The K(2)-localization of TMF is a wedge of copies of E2 indexed by the finite subgroups of G2. Do not confuse TMF with the periodic version TMF[Δ-1] or with the compactified version TMF1(N) for higher level structures.

The Balmer Spectrum of the Stable Homotopy Category and Tensor Triangular Geometry

The Balmer spectrum provides a topological space whose points are the prime thick tensor ideals of a tensor triangulated category, giving a geometric classification of thick subcategories that refines the Hopkins–Smith thick subcategory theorem. For the category of finite spectra Fin, the Balmer spectrum Spc(Fin) is homeomorphic to the set of primes {(p, n) | p prime, n ≥ 0} ∪ {(0, 0)} with a topology where the closure of (p, n) is {(p, m) | m ≤ n} ∪ {(0, 0)}. This recovers the chromatic filtration: the prime (p, n) corresponds to the Morava K-theory K(n) at the prime p, and the generic point (0, 0) corresponds to rational homotopy theory. The Balmer spectrum of the K(n)-local category of finite spectra is a single point, reflecting the fact that the K(n)-local category has no non-trivial thick tensor ideals (it is a minimal tensor triangulated category). For the category of all K(n)-local spectra, the Balmer spectrum is more complicated and related to the continuous representation theory of the Morava stabilizer group. The tensor triangular perspective, developed by Balmer, Barthel, Heard, and others, shows that the chromatic filtration is the Zariski topology on the Balmer spectrum of the stable homotopy category. The support of a finite spectrum X is the set of primes (p, n) such that K(n)*X ≠ 0, which is a Thomason subset of Spc(Fin). The classification of thick subcategories of Fin is equivalent to the classification of Thomason subsets of Spc(Fin), which are precisely the subsets closed under specialization. This gives a conceptual proof of the thick subcategory theorem: the thick subcategories are C≥n = {X | K(m)*X = 0 for m < n}, corresponding to the closed subsets {(p, m) | m ≥ n} ∪ {(0, 0)}. The Balmer spectrum also provides a framework for the chromatic fracture square: the localization functors LK(n) correspond to the open immersions of the points (p, n) into Spc(Fin). The theory extends to the category of all spectra via the compactly generated tensor triangulated category SH, where the Balmer spectrum is the Hochster dual of Spc(Fin) and classifies the smashing localizations. The telescope conjecture is equivalent to the statement that every smashing localization is a finite localization in the sense of tensor triangular geometry, which was disproved by Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank using the non-existence of certain compact objects in the K(n)-local category.

The Picard Group of the K(n)-Local Category and Exotic Invertible Objects

The Picard group Picn of the K(n)-local category is the group of isomorphism classes of invertible K(n)-local spectra under the smash product. By the Kuhn–Morava theorem, Picn is isomorphic to the group of continuous 1-cocycles of the Morava stabilizer group Gn with coefficients in the units of En0, modulo coboundaries, plus a ℤ factor for suspensions: Picn ≅ H1cts(Gn, En0×) ⊕ ℤ. The ℤ factor is generated by the suspension ΣLK(n)S0. The torsion subgroup of Picn is finite and has been computed for small n and p. For n=1, Pic1 ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ/(p-1) ⊕ ℤp at odd primes, and ℤ ⊕ ℤ/2 ⊕ ℤ2 at p=2. The ℤp factor corresponds to the Adams operations ψk for k ∈ ℤp×. For n=2, the computation is more involved: at p ≥ 5, Pic2 ≅ ℤ ⊕ (ℤ/(p2-1) ⊕ ℤp2) by work of Hovey and Sadofsky. At p=3, there is additional 3-torsion related to the finite subgroups of G2. At p=2, the Picard group has a large 2-torsion subgroup computed by Behrens and others. The exotic invertible objects (those not in the ℤ suspension factor) are detected by their action on the Gross–Hopkins dual In = F(LK(n)S0, Iℚ/ℤ). The determinant character det: Gn → ℤp× acts on the periodicity element u ∈ En-2 and gives a map ℤp× → Picn whose image is the ℤp factor. The finite torsion in Picn comes from the continuous cohomology of the finite subgroups of Gn. The Picard group acts on the category of K(n)-local spectra by smash product, and this action preserves the chromatic layer. The computation of Picn uses the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence, which is the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of Gn on En, and the algebraic duality resolution. The Picard group is also related to the group of autoequivalences of the K(n)-local category: every autoequivalence is given by smash product with an invertible object followed by a base change along an automorphism of the Morava stabilizer group.

The Chromatic Approach to the Image of J and the Adams–Novikov E2-Term

The image of the J-homomorphism J: πkO → πkS is a classical computation in stable homotopy theory that admits a complete chromatic interpretation. Adams proved that the p-primary component of im(J) is cyclic of order pvp(k)+1 for k ≡ 0,1,2,4 mod 8 (with modifications for k ≡ 3,5,6,7 mod 8), where vp is the p-adic valuation. Chromatically, the image of J is precisely the 1-stem in the K(1)-local sphere: π*LK(1)S0 contains the image of J as its homotopy groups in degrees congruent to -1, 0, 1, 2 mod 2(p-1) (at odd primes). The K(1)-local sphere splits as a wedge of suspensions of the p-complete complex K-theory spectrum KUp∧ and its Adams summands. The Adams–Novikov E2-term for the sphere is ExtMU*MUs,t(MU*, MU*), which is the cohomology of the moduli stack MFG of formal groups with coefficients in the sheaf ωt. The chromatic filtration on this E2-term corresponds to the height filtration on MFG. The height 1 part of the E2-term computes the K(1)-local homotopy groups and gives the image of J. The height 2 part computes the K(2)-local homotopy groups and is related to elliptic curves and modular forms. The Greek letter elements (α, β, γ, etc.) in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence are organized by their chromatic height: α-elements are height 1 (image of J), β-elements are height 2 (related to elliptic curves), γ-elements are height 3, etc. The periodicity theorems of Ravenel and others show that the vn-periodic families in the stable homotopy groups of spheres are detected by the Morava K-theories K(n). The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence converges to the p-complete stable homotopy groups of spheres, and its chromatic spectral sequence (the spectral sequence associated to the height filtration on the E2-term) has E1-term given by the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer groups H*(Gn, En*). The differentials in the chromatic spectral sequence are related to the differentials in the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence and the algebraic duality resolution. The computation of the stable stems up to dimension 100 by Isaksen, Wang, and Xu uses the Adams spectral sequence (not Adams–Novikov) but the chromatic interpretation guides the identification of families. The image of J is the only infinite family of height 1; all higher height families are vn-periodic for n ≥ 2 and are detected by the Smith–Toda complexes V(n).

  • Confusing the chromatic splitting conjecture (a decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere) with the chromatic convergence theorem (a statement about the fracture square for finite spectra); the splitting conjecture is much stronger and open for n ≥ 3.
  • Thinking TMF is a Morava E-theory; TMF is a global height-2 theory built from the moduli of elliptic curves, while E2 is a local theory built from the Lubin–Tate deformation space.
  • Assuming the Balmer spectrum of Fin is just the set of primes with the discrete topology; the topology is the Zariski topology where closure of (p,n) includes all lower heights at the same prime.
  • Believing the Picard group of the K(n)-local category is only the ℤ suspension factor; the exotic invertible objects form a finite torsion group plus a ℤpn factor detected by the determinant character.
  • Identifying the image of J with the entire K(1)-local sphere; im(J) is only the homotopy groups in certain congruence classes, while LK(1)S0 has additional summands from the Adams splitting.
  • Overlooking that the chromatic spectral sequence is a spectral sequence of spectral sequences (the CSS filters the Adams–Novikov E2-term by height), not a single spectral sequence converging to the stable stems.
  • Confusing the telescope conjecture (about smashing localizations) with the chromatic splitting conjecture (about the additive structure of LK(n)S0); the former was disproved in 2023, the latter remains open.

The Homotopy Fixed Point Spectral Sequence for the Morava Stabilizer Group and the Gross–Hopkins Spectral Sequence

The homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (HFPSS) for the action of the Morava stabilizer group Gn on the Morava E‑theory spectrum En is the primary computational tool for the K(n)‑local sphere. Its E2‑term is the continuous group cohomology Hsc(Gn; Ent), converging to the homotopy groups πt‑s LK(n) S0. The group Gn is a profinite extension of the automorphism group of a height n formal group over Fp by the Galois group of the base field; its cohomological dimension is n2. The spectral sequence is strongly convergent for finite spectra and conditionally convergent for the sphere. The Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence is a dual version obtained by applying the Gross–Hopkins duality functor In to the HFPSS; its E2‑term is Hsc(Gn; Ent ⊗ det) where det is the determinant character of Gn. This duality identifies the HFPSS for the sphere with the HFPSS for the dualizing spectrum, yielding a functional equation for the homotopy groups. The differentials in both spectral sequences are governed by the action of the Steenrod algebra on the cohomology of Gn and by the algebraic duality resolution constructed by Gross and Hopkins. The resolution expresses the dualizing module as an iterated extension of induced modules, allowing the computation of the E2‑term in a range. For n = 1 the HFPSS collapses and recovers the Adams–Novikov E2‑term for KU; for n = 2 the spectral sequence has been computed through a range of stems by Goerss, Henn, Mahowald, and Rezk, revealing the β‑family and the first exotic elements. The HFPSS also admits a chromatic filtration: the height filtration on the moduli stack of formal groups induces a filtration on the E2‑term whose associated graded pieces are the cohomology of the stabilizer groups at each height. This filtration is the algebraic shadow of the chromatic spectral sequence.

The Greek Letter Construction and the Periodicity Theorem in the Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence

The Greek letter elements α, β, γ, … in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) for the sphere are permanent cycles that detect infinite families in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The construction originates from the work of Ravenel, Miller, and Wilson: for each height n ≥ 1 there is a vn‑periodic family detected by the Morava K‑theory K(n). The α‑elements (height 1) correspond to the image of J and are detected by K(1); they live in degrees congruent to −1, 0, 1, 2 modulo 2(p−1) at odd primes. The β‑elements (height 2) are detected by K(2) and are intimately related to elliptic curves; the first β‑element β1 appears in stem 2p2−2p−2. The γ‑elements (height 3) are detected by K(3) and their existence was established by the periodicity theorem of Hopkins and Smith, which guarantees vn‑self maps on the Smith–Toda complexes V(n). The periodicity theorem states that for each n there exists a finite spectrum V(n) of type n and a map vn: Σd V(n) → V(n) that induces an isomorphism on K(n)‑homology. Iterating this map produces a vn‑periodic family in the homotopy groups of spheres. The Greek letter elements are the images of these iterates under the Hurewicz map to the ANSS. The differentials on Greek letter elements in the ANSS are controlled by the chromatic spectral sequence; for example, the first differential on β‑elements is a d3 related to the modular form Δ. The construction also yields the αt, βt, γt families indexed by the powers of the periodicity generator. The detection theorem of Devinatz, Hopkins, and Smith asserts that a map of finite spectra is nilpotent if and only if it is zero on K(n) for all n; consequently, the Greek letter elements are precisely the non‑nilpotent periodic families. The ANSS E2‑term ExtMU*MUs,t(MU*, MU*) is the cohomology of the moduli stack MFG of formal groups with coefficients in the sheaf ωt; the Greek letter elements correspond to certain cohomology classes on the height n strata.

Worked Example: Detecting a β‑Element in the ANSS
Show that the element β1 ∈ Ext2,2p2−2pMU*MU(MU*, MU*) is a permanent cycle in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere at an odd prime p ≥ 5.
The element β1 is represented by the invariant differential form ωp2−p−1 on the height 2 stratum of MFG. By the periodicity theorem, there exists a v2‑self map on the Smith–Toda complex V(2) inducing an isomorphism on K(2)‑homology. The Hurewicz image of the iterates of this self map yields a class in π2p2−2p−2 S0 which maps to β1 under the ANSS edge homomorphism. Since the sphere is a finite spectrum, the nilpotence theorem implies that any class detected by K(2) is non‑nilpotent and therefore survives all differentials in the ANSS. Moreover, the chromatic spectral sequence shows that the only possible differential on β1 would be a d3 hitting a class in filtration 5, but the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group G2 in that range is zero. Hence β1 is a permanent cycle and detects a non‑trivial element in the stable stem.
Exam Strategy
When asked about Greek letter elements, first state the height at which they live and the Morava K‑theory that detects them. Then recall the periodicity theorem: existence of vn‑self maps on V(n) guarantees the infinite family. Mention the nilpotence theorem as the reason these families are non‑trivial. For differentials, reference the chromatic spectral sequence and the known vanishing ranges of H*(Gn; En*).

The Beta Family, Elliptic Curves, and the Height 2 Chromatic Layer

The β‑family constitutes the first infinite family beyond the image of J and is the hallmark of chromatic height 2. Its members are detected by the Morava K‑theory K(2) and by the Morava E‑theory E2. The geometric origin of the β‑family lies in the theory of elliptic curves: the moduli stack of formal groups of height 2 is closely related to the moduli of supersingular elliptic curves in characteristic p. The Lubin–Tate deformation space of a height 2 formal group is a 2‑dimensional formal scheme whose ring of functions is the coefficient ring E20 ≅ W(Fp2)[[u1]][u±1]. The Morava stabilizer group G2 is a profinite group of dimension 4; its continuous cohomology H*(G2; E2*) computes the K(2)‑local homotopy groups of the sphere via the HFPSS. The first β‑element β1 appears in stem 2p2−2p−2 and corresponds to the modular form Δp−1 in the cohomology of the moduli of elliptic curves. Higher β‑elements βt are obtained by multiplying by powers of the periodicity generator v2p2−1. The ANSS differentials on β‑elements are governed by the action of the Hecke operators on modular forms; for instance, the d3 differential on β1 is related to the Eisenstein series E2. The computation of the K(2)‑local sphere up to dimension 200 by Goerss, Henn, Mahowald, and Rezk uses the algebraic duality resolution and the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence to resolve the E2‑term. The β‑family also appears in the homotopy of topological modular forms tmf: the map tmf → LK(2) S0 induces an isomorphism on the β‑family in a range, reflecting the fact that tmf is a global height 2 theory. The relationship between the β‑family and the cohomology of the moduli stack of elliptic curves Mell is made precise by the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem, which equips tmf with an E∞ structure and identifies its homotopy groups with the compactified cohomology of Mell.

The Gamma Family, Height 3 Phenomena, and the Morava Stabilizer Group Action

The γ‑family represents the first infinite family at chromatic height 3 and is detected by K(3). Its existence was established by the periodicity theorem applied to the Smith–Toda complex V(3). The first γ‑element γ1 lives in stem 2p3−2p2−2p−2 (for p ≥ 5) and is constructed from a v3‑self map on V(3). The Morava stabilizer group G3 has dimension 9 and its continuous cohomology H*(G3; E3*) is considerably more complicated than at height 2. The action of G3 on E3 factors through the automorphism group of a height 3 formal group law over Fp3; the determinant character det: G3 → Zp× plays a crucial role in Gross–Hopkins duality. The algebraic duality resolution for G3 involves a length 9 complex of induced modules, and the associated Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence has been used by Beaudry, Goerss, and Henn to compute the K(3)‑local sphere in a range. The γ‑family is not the only height 3 phenomenon: there are also exotic elements such as the ζ‑family detected by the action of the extended Morava stabilizer group G3 ⋊ Gal(Fp3/Fp). The Kuhn–Morava theorem describes the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Morava E‑theory of the sphere, showing that the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for G3 on E3 is a module over the cohomology of the Galois group. The γ‑elements are permanent cycles in the ANSS because they are detected by K(3) and the nilpotence theorem guarantees their non‑triviality. Differential patterns on γ‑elements are less understood; the first potential differential is a d5 related to the cohomology of G3 in degree 5. The computation of the K(3)‑local Picard group by Pstragowski reveals exotic invertible objects that are not suspensions, and these interact with the γ‑family through the action of the Picard group on the K(3)‑local category.

The Chromatic Approach to the Segal Conjecture and the Burnside Ring

The Segal conjecture (proved by Carlsson) identifies the completion of the stable cohomotopy groups of a classifying space BG with the completion of the Burnside ring A(G) at its augmentation ideal. Chromatic homotopy theory provides a layered perspective: for a finite group G, the K(n)‑localization of the suspension spectrum Σ∞ BG+ splits according to the conjugacy classes of subgroups of G whose order is divisible by p. The Morava K‑theory K(n) detects only p‑subgroups of rank at most n; thus the chromatic filtration on the Segal conjecture corresponds to the filtration by p‑rank. The Burnside ring A(G) carries a natural λ‑ring structure, and its completion at the augmentation ideal is isomorphic to the inverse limit of the representation rings of the p‑subgroups. The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for Σ∞ BG+ has E2‑term ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*(BG)) which, by the chromatic resolution, decomposes into contributions from each height. At height 1 the contribution recovers the classical Atiyah–Segal completion theorem for complex K‑theory. At height 2 the contribution involves the Morava E‑theory of BG, which has been computed by Strickland for abelian p‑groups using the theory of formal groups. The nilpotence theorem implies that the map from the sphere to the K(n)‑local sphere is a monomorphism on the Burnside ring after completion, and the thick subcategory theorem classifies the localizing subcategories generated by the K(n)‑localizations of BG. The telescope conjecture (now disproved) would have implied that the K(n)‑localization of Σ∞ BG+ is a smashing localization; its failure means that the chromatic layers of the Segal conjecture do not arise from smashing with a finite spectrum. The work of Barthel, Hausmann, Naumann, Noel, and others on the chromatic approach to the Balmer spectrum of the category of G‑spectra shows that the prime ideals correspond to pairs (H, n) where H is a p‑subgroup of G and n is a chromatic height, with the Zariski topology reflecting inclusion of subgroups and height filtration.

  • Confusing the HFPSS for Gn with the Gross–Hopkins spectral sequence; the latter is the dual of the former twisted by the determinant character.
  • Assuming all Greek letter elements are permanent cycles without invoking the nilpotence theorem and the periodicity theorem.
  • Thinking the β‑family is detected solely by tmf; tmf detects the β‑family only after K(2)‑localization and in a limited range.
  • Believing the γ‑family has been fully computed; the K(3)‑local sphere is known only in a low‑dimensional range and many differentials remain open.
  • Overlooking that the Segal conjecture’s chromatic filtration is by p‑rank of subgroups, not by the order of the group.
  • Equating the telescope conjecture with the chromatic splitting conjecture; the former concerns smashing localizations, the latter concerns the additive structure of LK(n) S0.
  • Neglecting the role of the determinant character in Gross–Hopkins duality and the Picard group computation.

Topological Hochschild Homology and the Cyclotomic Trace in Chromatic Homotopy Theory

Topological Hochschild homology (THH) of a ring spectrum R is the homotopical analogue of classical Hochschild homology. It is constructed as the geometric realization of a cyclic bar construction and carries a natural circle action. The homotopy fixed points of this action define topological cyclic homology (TC), and the cyclotomic trace is a natural map K(R) → TC(R) from algebraic K-theory. In chromatic homotopy theory this trace provides a bridge between the algebraic K-theory of a ring spectrum — which is typically of higher chromatic height — and the more computable TC, which is built from the K(n)-localizations of R.

For the sphere spectrum S, THH(S) ≃ S and TC(S) is closely related to the K(1)-local sphere. The cyclotomic trace K(S) → TC(S) therefore detects the K(1)-local part of the algebraic K-theory of the sphere. At higher heights, the work of Bökstedt, Hsiang, and Madsen on THH of connective complex K-theory ku showed that THH(ku) is a form of K(2)-local object, and the cyclotomic trace K(ku) → TC(ku) exhibits the predicted redshift: ku has chromatic height 1, while K(ku) has height 2. This pattern is the content of the redshift conjecture, which asserts that for a suitably nice ring spectrum R of chromatic height n, the algebraic K-theory K(R) has height n+1.

The cyclotomic trace factors through the K(n)-localizations of R. For a Morava E-theory spectrum En, the K(n)-localization of THH(En) can be computed using the Lubin–Tate deformation theory, and the resulting TC(En) is closely related to the K(n)-local sphere. The trace map thus provides a chromatic filtration on algebraic K-theory: the K(n)-local part of K(R) is approximated by the K(n)-local part of TC(R). This perspective has been exploited by Ausoni, Rognes, and others to compute the K(2)-local algebraic K-theory of ku and to study the K(n)-local K-theory of En.

Methodologically, the cyclotomic trace is a map of cyclotomic spectra, and its fiber is the relative K-theory. The Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem identifies the relative K-theory of a nilpotent extension of ring spectra with the relative TC, which is often easier to compute. In the chromatic setting, this allows one to climb the chromatic tower by analyzing the fibers of the trace map at each height. The resulting spectral sequences — the cyclotomic trace spectral sequence and the K(n)-local Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for TC — are active tools in modern computations of algebraic K-theory of structured ring spectra.

The Goerss–Hopkins Obstruction Theory for E∞ Ring Spectra

The Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory provides a systematic method for constructing E∞ ring structures on a given homotopy commutative ring spectrum. It takes as input a graded commutative ring A* equipped with a suitable comodule structure over the Hopf algebroid (MU*MU, MU*) and produces a spectral sequence whose E2-term is an André–Quillen cohomology group ExtA*(A*, M) for an appropriate A*-module M. The differentials in this spectral sequence are the obstructions to lifting a given multiplication to an E∞ structure; if all obstructions vanish, the theory yields a moduli space of E∞ structures.

The theory was developed by Goerss and Hopkins in the 1990s and later refined by Goerss, Hopkins, and Miller. It applies to any even periodic ring spectrum whose homotopy groups are concentrated in even degrees and which admits a complex orientation. The key technical input is the identification of the cotangent complex of an E∞ ring spectrum with the indecomposables of the K(n)-local E∞-algebra structure. For Morava E-theory En, the obstruction theory shows that the space of E∞ structures is contractible, which is the uniqueness part of the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem. For other spectra, such as topological modular forms tmf, the obstruction theory is used to construct the E∞ structure on the compactified moduli stack of elliptic curves.

In practice, one starts with a homotopy ring A* = π*(R) and a choice of formal group law over A0. The obstruction groups are computed as Ext groups in the category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid classifying deformations of the formal group. The first obstruction lies in Ext2 and measures the failure of the multiplication to be strictly associative up to coherent homotopy. Higher obstructions live in Exts+2 for s ≥ 1. The spectral sequence converges to the space of E∞ structures on a lift of A* to a ring spectrum.

The obstruction theory also clarifies the relationship between the Lubin–Tate deformation space and the moduli of E∞ structures. The universal deformation of a height n formal group law over Fp carries a canonical E∞ structure, and any other E∞ structure on the same homotopy type is equivalent to it. This rigidity is a consequence of the vanishing of the relevant André–Quillen cohomology groups for the Lubin–Tate ring. The theory has been extended to non-even periodic cases by Lurie and others, using the language of ∞-operads and the cotangent complex in stable ∞-categories.

Worked Example: Obstruction to an E∞ Structure on a Hypothetical Ring Spectrum
Let R be a connective ring spectrum with π0(R) = Z(2) and π2(R) = Z/2 generated by a class v1. Suppose the multiplication on π*(R) is commutative and the formal group law associated to the complex orientation has height 1 at the prime 2. Compute the first obstruction group for an E∞ structure on R and explain why it vanishes.
The first obstruction lies in the André–Quillen cohomology group AQ2(π*(R); π*(R)). For a height 1 formal group law over Z(2), the Lubin–Tate deformation ring is Z2[[u1]] with u1 in degree 0. The relevant comodule structure is over the Hopf algebroid (MU*MU, MU*) localized at 2 and height 1. The cohomology group Ext2 in the category of comodules for the height 1 formal group law vanishes because the Morava stabilizer group G1 ≃ Z2× has cohomological dimension 1. Hence the first obstruction group is zero, and there is no obstruction at this stage. This matches the known fact that the E∞ structure on K-theory (the height 1 Morava E-theory) is unique.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Obstruction Theory Patterns
When a question asks about the existence or uniqueness of an E∞ structure on a ring spectrum, identify the chromatic height of the associated formal group law. At height 1 the obstruction groups vanish by the cohomological dimension of G1; at height ≥ 2 the first non‑trivial obstruction typically lives in Ext2 of the Morava stabilizer group. Memorize the vanishing line: Hs(Gn; M) = 0 for s > n2. This bound controls the length of the obstruction spectral sequence.

The Chromatic Spectral Sequence and the Computation of the 2-Primary Stable Homotopy Groups up to the 50-Stem

The chromatic spectral sequence (CSS) is a first-quadrant spectral sequence whose E1-term is a direct sum of Morava K-theory based Adams–Novikov E2-terms, and which converges to the stable homotopy groups of spheres π*(S). At the prime 2, the CSS has been the primary computational tool for determining the 2-primary stable stems up to the 50-stem. The E1-page is built from the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer groups Gn with coefficients in the Morava E-theory of the sphere. The differentials in the CSS are induced by the chromatic resolution and are closely related to the Greek letter elements αi, βi, γi.

In the range up to the 50-stem, the CSS collapses at a relatively early page after a few differentials. The α-family (detected by K(1)) gives the image of J. The β-family (detected by K(2)) appears first in the 2-primary stems at dimensions 6, 14, 30, 62, … and is responsible for many of the periodic patterns. The γ-family (detected by K(3)) begins to appear above the 50-stem, so its influence in this range is limited to a few exotic elements. The computation by Ravenel, Mahowald, and Tangora in the 1970s and 1980s used the CSS together with the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence to produce a complete chart of the 2-primary stable homotopy groups up to dimension 50.

Key differentials in this range include the d3 differential on the β-family elements, which is related to the cohomology of G2 in degree 3, and the d5 differential on certain β-elements that corresponds to the first potential differential on the γ-family. The CSS also reveals the interaction between the β-family and the α-family through the d2 differential, which is essentially the action of the Steenrod algebra on the K(1)-local layer. The final result is a table of groups πk(S)(2) for k ≤ 50, showing a mixture of cyclic groups of order 2, 4, 8, and 16, with the largest cyclic summand being Z/16 in dimension 31.

Modern refinements by Isaksen, Wang, and Xu have extended the computation to the 90-stem using motivic methods, but the classical CSS computation up to the 50-stem remains the standard reference for the chromatic structure of the low-dimensional stable stems. The CSS demonstrates concretely how the chromatic filtration organizes the stable homotopy groups: each chromatic layer contributes a family of periodic elements, and the differentials encode the interactions between layers.

The Classification of Smashing Localizations in the K(n)-Local Category

In the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n), a localization functor L is called smashing if it preserves coproducts. The telescope conjecture, formulated by Ravenel, asked whether the Bousfield localization with respect to a finite type n spectrum is smashing. The conjecture was disproved for n ≥ 2 by Mahowald, Ravenel, and Shick, but the classification of all smashing localizations in SpK(n) was subsequently achieved by Barthel, Heard, and Valenzuela.

The theorem states that the smashing localizations in SpK(n) are precisely the Bousfield localizations with respect to the Morava K-theories K(m) for 0 ≤ m ≤ n. Here K(0) denotes rational homology, and K(n) is the given Morava K-theory. The proof uses the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins and Smith to classify the thick subcategories of finite K(n)-local spectra, and the nilpotence theorem to show that any smashing localization is determined by its effect on the K(m)-local layers.

The Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is therefore a chain K(0) < K(1) < … < K(n). Each inclusion corresponds to a smashing localization. There are no other smashing localizations; in particular, the localization with respect to a finite type n spectrum (which is Bousfield equivalent to K(n)) is not smashing unless it coincides with LK(n). This result clarifies the structure of the K(n)-local category and shows that the failure of the telescope conjecture is not an isolated phenomenon but a reflection of the rigidity of the Bousfield lattice.

The classification also has consequences for the Picard group of SpK(n). Since smashing localizations correspond to idempotent objects in the Picard group, the chain of K(m)-localizations gives a filtration of the Picard group by the kernels of the maps induced by LK(m). The exotic invertible objects detected by Pstragowski at height 3 are not smashing, confirming that they lie outside the Bousfield lattice of smashing localizations.

The Algebraic K-Theory of Spheres and the Chromatic Redshift

The algebraic K-theory of the sphere spectrum K(S) is the initial object in the category of E∞ ring spectra under the sphere. Its chromatic complexity is a central question. The redshift conjecture predicts that if a ring spectrum R has chromatic height n, then K(R) has height n+1. Since the sphere has height 0 (it is detected by rational homology), K(S) should have height 1. This is consistent with the fact that the cyclotomic trace K(S) → TC(S) becomes an equivalence after K(1)-localization, and TC(S) is known to be K(1)-local.

At the prime 2, the computation of K(S) has been approached via the cyclotomic trace and the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for TC(S). The work of Rognes on the K(1)-local algebraic K-theory of the sphere shows that LK(1)K(S) is a wedge of suspensions of the K(1)-local sphere, with a pattern of summands dictated by the image of J.

The Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel Theorem and the Kervaire Invariant One Problem

The Kervaire invariant one problem asks for which dimensions n there exists a framed manifold of Kervaire invariant one. By the work of Kervaire and Milnor, this is equivalent to asking whether the Arf invariant of a quadratic form over F2 can be one in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The problem was solved in 2009 by Hill, Hopkins, and Ravenel using chromatic homotopy theory at height 2. Their proof constructs a spectrum Ω (the “Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel spectrum”) which is a form of topological modular forms TMF with level structure, and shows that the Kervaire invariant element θj ∈ π2j+1−2(S) is detected by a map S → Ω that factors through the C2-equivariant sphere. The key chromatic input is that Ω is built from the Lubin–Tate theory of a supersingular elliptic curve at the prime 2, so its homotopy groups are controlled by the Morava stabilizer group G2 at height 2. The theorem states that the Kervaire invariant one elements exist only in dimensions 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, and possibly 126. The case 126 remains open because the 2-primary component of π126(S) has not been fully computed; the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel argument shows that if θ6 exists, it must be detected in the K(2)-local sphere, but the K(2)-local computation at that dimension is incomplete. The proof uses the C2-equivariant slice spectral sequence for the Real bordism spectrum MUR, together with the chromatic fracture square for TMF, to show that the relevant differential in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence is non-zero for j ≥ 7. This is a landmark application of chromatic methods to a classical geometric problem, demonstrating that the height-2 layer (elliptic curves, TMF) governs the Kervaire invariant.

The Morava Change-of-Rings Theorem and the Algebraic Chromatic Spectral Sequence

The Morava change-of-rings theorem is the algebraic engine that computes the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the K(n)-local sphere. Let En be the Morava E-theory of height n at a prime p, with coefficient ring En0 ≅ W(Fpn)[[u1, …, un−1]] and formal group Gn of height n. The Morava stabilizer group Gn = Aut(Gn) ⋊ Gal(Fpn/Fp) acts continuously on En. The theorem states that for any K(n)-local spectrum X, there is an isomorphism of spectral sequences
E2s,t(X) ≅ Hscts(Gn; Ent(X)) ⇒ πt−s(LK(n)X)(p),
where the left side is the Adams–Novikov E2-term computed with En-homology, and the right side is continuous group cohomology of Gn with coefficients in the En-homology of X. When X = S, this identifies the E2-term of the K(n)-local Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere with H*cts(Gn; En*). The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence (ACSS) is a further spectral sequence that computes this continuous cohomology by filtering Gn by its open subgroups. For n = 1, G1 ≅ Zp× and the ACSS collapses, giving the classical image of J. For n = 2, the ACSS has been computed by Shimomura, Yabe, and others using the Honda formal group law and the action of G2 on the Lubin–Tate deformation space. The E1-term of the ACSS is a direct sum of cohomology groups of finite subgroups of Gn (the “finite subgroup resolution”), and the differentials encode the interaction between these subgroups. The ACSS is the primary tool for computing the K(n)-local stable stems at heights n ≥ 2, and its complexity grows rapidly with n.

Worked Computation: ACSS at Height 2, p = 2
Explain why the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence for the K(2)-local sphere at p = 2 has a non-trivial d1 differential on the class h1 ∈ H1(G2; E20) and identify its target.
At height 2 and p = 2, the Morava stabilizer group G2 contains a quaternion subgroup Q8 of order 8. The continuous cohomology H*cts(G2; E20) is computed via the finite subgroup resolution using the normalizer of Q8. The class h1 corresponds to the generator of H1(Q8; F2) ≅ F2 under the inflation map. The d1 differential in the ACSS arises from the conjugation action of the normalizer N(Q8) on Q8. Explicitly, N(Q8)/Q8 ≅ C3 acts on H1(Q8; F2) by cyclically permuting the three non-trivial 1-dimensional characters. The invariant subspace is zero, so h1 does not survive to the E2-page of the ACSS. The differential d1(h1) = h2 + h1h2 (in the notation of Shimomura), where h2 ∈ H2(Q8; F2) is the generator. This kills the potential α1 element in the K(2)-local sphere, consistent with the fact that π1(LK(2)S) = 0 at p = 2. The computation uses the explicit cocycle representatives for the action of the normalizer on the Lubin–Tate deformation parameters u1.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Change-of-Rings Isomorphisms
When a question asks for the E2-term of a K(n)-local Adams–Novikov spectral sequence, immediately write H*cts(Gn; En*(X)). If X = S, the coefficients are En* = En0[u±1] with |u| = −2. For n = 1, G1 ≅ Zp× and the cohomology is the Iwasawa algebra; for n = 2, know the finite subgroups (Q8, C3, C4 at p = 2; SD16 at p = 3) and that the ACSS is the standard computational tool. Do not confuse the Morava change-of-rings (which identifies the ANSS E2 with continuous group cohomology) with the chromatic spectral sequence (which filters the stable homotopy category by height).

The Bousfield–Kuhn Functor and the Chromatic Approach to Unstable Homotopy

The Bousfield–Kuhn functor Φ provides a bridge between unstable homotopy theory and chromatic stable homotopy theory. For a pointed space X, Φ(X) is a spectrum whose homotopy groups are related to the unstable homotopy groups of X by a natural isomorphism πk(Φ(X)) ≅ πk+1(X) ⊗ Q for k ≥ 1, and more generally, the K(n)-localization of Φ(X) detects the vn-periodic part of the unstable homotopy groups of X. Kuhn proved that for a finite complex X, the K(n)-localization of Φ(X) is equivalent to the K(n)-localization of the suspension spectrum of the free En-algebra on X. This identifies the vn-periodic unstable homotopy groups of X with the K(n)-local homotopy groups of a certain En-module spectrum. The functor Φ is constructed using the Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor on spaces: Φ(X) is the derivative of the identity evaluated at X, or equivalently, the spectrum associated to the cosimplicial space of iterated loop spaces. The chromatic significance is that the K(n)-local category is generated by the images of finite complexes under Φ, and the telescope conjecture for spaces (which is false) is related to the smashing property of Φ. The Bousfield–Kuhn functor also plays a central role in the Kuhn–Morava theorem, which describes the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the K(n)-localization of Φ(BG) for a finite group G. This connects the representation theory of G to the chromatic homotopy theory of classifying spaces.

The Lubin–Tate Tower and the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of p-Divisible Groups

The Lubin–Tate tower is a tower of rigid analytic spaces (or formal schemes) that parametrizes deformations of a height-n formal group law together with level structures. At height n and prime p, let Gn be a formal group of height n over Fpn. The Lubin–Tate deformation space Mn = Spf En0 parametrizes deformations of Gn to complete local rings. For each m ≥ 1, the level-m Lubin–Tate space Mn(m) parametrizes deformations together with a Drinfeld level structure of level pm, i.e., an isomorphism (Z/pmZ)n ≅ G[pm] of the pm-torsion subgroup. These spaces form a tower Mn(1) ← Mn(2) ← … with an action of GLn(Z/pmZ) × Gn. The chromatic homotopy theory of this tower is encoded in the Morava E-theory spectrum En, whose homotopy groups are the cohomology of the tower: π−k(En) ≅ Hk(Mn; ω⊗k) where ω is the sheaf of invariant differentials. The K(n)-local sphere is the homotopy fixed points of the Morava stabilizer group acting on En, and the Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the dualizing module with the determinant representation of Gn on the Tate module of the universal deformation. The tower also appears in the Langlands correspondence for GLn(Qp): the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower realizes the local Langlands correspondence and the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence. In chromatic homotopy theory, the tower provides a geometric model for the K(n)-local category via the equivalence between K(n)-local spectra and quasi-coherent sheaves on the stack Mn/Gn (the “chromatic stack”). This perspective, developed by Goerss, Hopkins, Lurie, and others, underlies the Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory and the construction of E∞ structures on En.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Thom Spectra and the Cobordism Hypothesis

Thom spectra provide a fundamental class of examples in chromatic homotopy theory. For a map f : X → BO, the Thom spectrum Mf has a natural E∞ ring structure when f is an E∞ map. The chromatic height of Mf is determined by the formal group law of its complex orientation. The universal example is MU = MO<⟨2⟩>, the Thom spectrum of the universal stable complex vector bundle, which carries the universal formal group law and has chromatic height ∞. Its K(n)-localization LK(n)MU is a wedge of suspensions of En. For n = 1, MU(p) splits as a wedge of suspensions of BP, the Brown–Peterson spectrum, which has height 1 at p. For n = 2, the Thom spectrum MString (the Thom spectrum of the string bordism theory) has a map to tmf (topological modular forms), and its K(2)-localization is closely related to E2. The cobordism hypothesis of Baez–Dolan–Lurie classifies fully extended topological field theories in terms of the (∞,n)-category of bordisms. In chromatic homotopy theory, this implies that the K(n)-local category is the target of a universal n-dimensional topological field theory whose value on a point is the Morava E-theory En. The chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category corresponds to the stratification of the moduli stack of formal groups Mfg by height, and Thom spectra provide the geometric cycles that generate the homotopy groups. The Landweber exact functor theorem states that a map MU* → R* gives a homology theory if and only if the associated formal group law is Landweber exact, i.e., the sequence (p, v1, …, vn−1) is regular on R*. This is the algebraic criterion for a Thom spectrum to have chromatic height ≤ n. The work of Ando, Hopkins, Rezk, and Strickland on elliptic spectra and tmf shows that the height-2 layer is governed by the moduli of elliptic curves, and the Thom spectrum MString maps to tmf via the Witten genus, providing a geometric realization of the height-2 chromatic layer.
