  • Trap: Confusing the Morava change-of-rings theorem (which identifies the ANSS E2-term for K(n)-local spectra with continuous group cohomology of Gn) with the chromatic spectral sequence (which filters the stable homotopy category by Bousfield classes K(n)). They are different spectral sequences with different inputs and outputs.
  • Trap: Stating that the Hill–Hopkins–Ravenel theorem completely solves the Kervaire invariant problem. The case n = 126 (j = 6) remains open; the theorem proves non-existence for j ≥ 7.
  • Trap: Assuming the Bousfield–Kuhn functor Φ preserves all limits or colimits. It is a left adjoint (to the 0-th space functor on spectra) but does not preserve products; its K(n)-localization is smashing only after K(n)-localization.
  • Trap: Claiming the Lubin–Tate tower is a tower of schemes. It is a tower of formal schemes (or rigid analytic spaces over Qp); the level-m spaces are finite étale covers of the formal deformation space only after inverting p.
  • Trap: Thinking every Thom spectrum has a well-defined chromatic height. Only Thom spectra with a complex orientation (or more generally, an MU-orientation) have a formal group law; unoriented Thom spectra like MO do not have a height in the chromatic sense.
  • Trap: Confusing the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence (which computes H*cts(Gn; En*)) with the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (which computes π*(EnhG) for a subgroup G ⊆ Gn). The ACSS is a spectral sequence of algebras computing the E2-term of the ANSS; the HFPSS computes the homotopy groups of a K(n)-local spectrum.
  • Trap: Asserting that the Landweber exact functor theorem applies to any map MU* → R*. It requires the regularity condition on the sequence (p, v1, …, vn−1); for example, the map to HZ (ordinary homology) is not Landweber exact at any prime because v1 acts by 0 on HZ*.

The Chromatic Tower Construction and its Universal Property

The chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category is not merely a conceptual stratification but is realized through a concrete construction known as the chromatic tower. For any spectrum X, the chromatic tower is an inverse system of spectra {LnX}n ≥ 0, where LnX denotes the Bousfield localization of X with respect to Morava K-theory K(n). This tower provides a sequence of approximations to X, filtering it by its chromatic height. Specifically, L0X is the rationalization of X (localization with respect to K(0) = HQ, rational Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum), L1X is the K(1)-localization (related to p-adic K-theory), and so on. The maps in the tower are the natural localization maps LnX → Ln-1X, which exist because K(n-1) is K(n)-local for n ≥ 1.

The inverse limit of this tower, denoted limn LnX, is the K(∞)-localization of X, which is often denoted LK(∞)X or LpX (the p-localization of X). The chromatic convergence theorem states that for a finite spectrum X, the natural map X → limn LnX is an equivalence. This means that, at a given prime p, any finite spectrum can be fully recovered by assembling its information from all chromatic heights. This is a profound statement, asserting that the entire stable homotopy category of finite spectra can be understood through its chromatic layers.

Each stage LnX in the tower captures the "chromatic information" of X up to height n. The fiber of the map LnX → Ln-1X is denoted PnX, which is the n-th chromatic layer or slice of X. These slices are K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic, meaning they represent the "pure height n" part of the spectrum X. The chromatic tower thus decomposes a spectrum into a sequence of increasingly refined pieces, each associated with a specific chromatic height. This decomposition is analogous to a Postnikov tower in unstable homotopy theory, but here the filtration is by Bousfield classes rather than homotopy groups.

A key aspect of the chromatic tower is its universal property. The localization functor Ln is the universal functor that sends K(n)-acyclic spectra to contractible spectra. This universality ensures that the LnX are the "best possible" approximations of X that are K(n)-local. The construction of the tower relies heavily on the existence of smashing localizations, which are localizations that commute with finite wedges. The K(n)-localizations are smashing, a property crucial for the well-behaved nature of the chromatic tower. The chromatic tower therefore provides a systematic framework for analyzing the stable homotopy category by dissecting spectra according to their behavior with respect to the Morava K-theories, revealing the intricate structure of stable homotopy groups.

The Action of the Morava Stabilizer Group on En and its Homotopy Fixed Points

The Morava E-theories En are E∞ ring spectra that serve as the fundamental building blocks of the chromatic filtration. Their algebraic structure is deeply intertwined with the action of the Morava Stabilizer Group Gn. This group is a profinite group, defined as the automorphism group of the Lubin-Tate formal group law of height n over the finite field Fpn. More precisely, Gn is the group of automorphisms of the formal group law associated with En, which is a formal group law over En*(pt) ≅ W(Fpn)[[u1, ..., un-1]][[u±1]], where W(Fpn) is the ring of Witt vectors of Fpn.

The group Gn acts continuously on the E∞ ring spectrum En. This action is not just on the homotopy groups En*(pt) but on the entire spectrum level. Understanding the homotopy fixed points of this action, denoted EnhGn, is central to chromatic homotopy theory. The spectrum EnhGn is a K(n)-local spectrum whose homotopy groups are computed by the Gross-Hopkins spectral sequence, which is a specific instance of a homotopy fixed point spectral sequence. This spectral sequence computes π*(EnhGn) from the continuous group cohomology H*cts(Gn; En*(pt)).

The significance of EnhGn lies in its profound connection to the K(n)-local sphere spectrum, LK(n)S0. The Hopkins-Ravenel conjecture, now a theorem by Devinatz, Hopkins, and Smith, states that LK(n)S0 is equivalent to EnhGn. This equivalence is a cornerstone of chromatic homotopy, as it reduces the problem of understanding the K(n)-local sphere (a highly complex object) to computing the homotopy fixed points of a known E∞ ring spectrum under the action of a well-understood profinite group. This allows for the application of algebraic tools, particularly continuous group cohomology, to solve problems in stable homotopy theory.

The structure of Gn itself is rich. It contains a normal subgroup Sn, the Morava stabilizer group proper, which is a p-adic Lie group, and the Galois group Gal(Fpn/Fp) ≅ Z/nZ. The action of Gn on En is crucial for detecting vn-periodicity, as the elements of Gn act as vn-self-equivalences on En. The study of EnhGn and its variants (e.g., EnhK for finite subgroups K ⊆ Gn) provides deep insights into the structure of the K(n)-local category, including the classification of its thick subcategories and the behavior of the vn-periodic elements in the stable homotopy groups of spheres.

Understanding the Chromatic Tower
Consider a finite spectrum X. The chromatic tower of X is given by an inverse system {LnX}n ≥ 0. Which of the following statements about the chromatic tower and its components is INCORRECT?
  1. L0X is the rationalization of X, meaning it captures the information of X after inverting all primes.
  2. The map LnX → Ln-1X has a fiber PnX, which is K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic.
  3. The inverse limit limn LnX is equivalent to the p-localization LpX for a given prime p.
  4. The Morava Stabilizer Group Gn acts on LnX, and its homotopy fixed points compute the K(n)-local sphere.
The correct answer is 4. Let's analyze each statement:
  1. L0X is the rationalization of X, meaning it captures the information of X after inverting all primes. This is correct. K(0) is rational K-theory, which is equivalent to HQ (rational Eilenberg-MacLane spectrum). Localization with respect to HQ is rationalization.
  2. The map LnX → Ln-1X has a fiber PnX, which is K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic. This is correct. PnX is defined as the fiber of this map and represents the "pure height n" information of X. It is K(n)-local by construction and K(n-1)-acyclic because Ln-1X is K(n-1)-local, and the map LnX → Ln-1X essentially "kills" the K(n-1)-local part of LnX relative to Ln-1X.
  3. The inverse limit limn LnX is equivalent to the p-localization LpX for a given prime p. This is correct. The chromatic tower is constructed at a fixed prime p. The inverse limit over all heights n (up to infinity) recovers the entire p-local information of the spectrum X, which is precisely LpX.
  4. The Morava Stabilizer Group Gn acts on LnX, and its homotopy fixed points compute the K(n)-local sphere. This statement is INCORRECT. The Morava Stabilizer Group Gn acts on the Morava E-theory spectrum En, not generally on LnX for an arbitrary spectrum X. It is the homotopy fixed points of En under the action of Gn (i.e., EnhGn) that are equivalent to the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0, not the homotopy fixed points of LnX. While LnS0 is the K(n)-local sphere, the action of Gn is specific to En.
Distinguishing Localizations and Actions
When tackling questions on chromatic homotopy, pay close attention to the specific spectrum involved in a localization or group action. LnX refers to the K(n)-localization of an arbitrary spectrum X, while En is a specific E∞ ring spectrum associated with height n. The Morava Stabilizer Group Gn acts on En, and its homotopy fixed points are crucial for understanding the K(n)-local sphere, LK(n)S0. Do not generalize the Gn action to all K(n)-local spectra without specific justification.

The Chromatic Layers and the Telescope Conjecture Revisited

The concept of chromatic layers, denoted PnX, arises naturally from the chromatic tower. As discussed, PnX is defined as the fiber of the map LnX → Ln-1X. These spectra are K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic, meaning they are the "pure height n" components of a spectrum X. They isolate the information that is periodic with respect to the vn-self-map but not with respect to vn-1. The stable homotopy category is thus filtered by these layers, providing a powerful tool for decomposition and analysis.

The structure of these layers is intimately connected to the Telescope Conjecture. The original form of the Telescope Conjecture, proposed by Ravenel, stated that for any finite spectrum X which is K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic (i.e., a Pn-local spectrum), the map X → LK(n)X is an equivalence. In simpler terms, it conjectured that a spectrum that is already "pure height n" cannot be further localized by K(n) to yield new information. This would imply that the K(n)-localization of a Pn-local spectrum is just the spectrum itself. This conjecture was motivated by the desire to understand the relationship between finite and infinite height phenomena.

However, the Telescope Conjecture was famously shown to be false in general. The first counterexample was constructed by Ravenel and others for n=2 at the prime p=2. Later, Mahowald and Shick provided counterexamples for all n ≥ 2 at p=2. The failure of the Telescope Conjecture means that there exist Pn-local spectra X such that X is not K(n)-local. This implies that the category of Pn-local spectra is strictly larger than the category of K(n)-local spectra, even though Pn-local spectra are defined to be K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic. This subtle distinction highlights the complexity of the stable homotopy category.

Despite its general falsity, the Telescope Conjecture holds true in certain important cases. For instance, it is known to be true for all n when p is an odd prime. It is also true for n=1 at any prime p. The implications of its failure are significant: it means that the vn-periodic information of a spectrum is not always fully captured by its K(n)-localization. There can be "hidden" K(n)-local information that is not detected by the Pn-localization. This led to a deeper understanding of the relationship between different Bousfield classes and the structure of smashing localizations, emphasizing that the chromatic filtration is more intricate than initially hypothesized. The failure of the Telescope Conjecture underscores the non-triviality of the stable homotopy category and the challenges in fully classifying its localizations and thick subcategories.

Topological Modular Forms (tmf) and the Height 2 Chromatic Layer in Depth

Topological Modular Forms (tmf) stands as a pinnacle achievement in chromatic homotopy theory, providing a concrete and geometrically rich realization of the height 2 chromatic layer at the prime p=2 (and often for all primes, though the p=2 case is particularly intricate). It is an E∞ ring spectrum whose homotopy groups are closely related to classical modular forms. The construction of tmf, largely due to Hopkins, Miller, and Goerss, involves deep connections between algebraic geometry, formal group theory, and stable homotopy theory.

The genesis of tmf lies in the observation that the formal group law of height 2 is associated with elliptic curves. Specifically, tmf is constructed as the global sections of a sheaf of E∞ ring spectra over the moduli stack of elliptic curves, Mell. This stack parametrizes elliptic curves and their isomorphisms. The Goerss-Hopkins-Miller theorem provides the machinery to construct E∞ ring spectra from suitable geometric data, and tmf is a prime example. It is the universal E∞ ring spectrum associated with a height 2 formal group law, meaning any other height 2 E∞ ring spectrum can be understood in relation to tmf.

The homotopy groups of tmf, denoted π*(tmf), are the "topological modular forms." These groups are known to be related to the classical ring of modular forms, but with additional structure and elements arising from the topological context. For instance, the elements of π*(tmf) include the classical Eisenstein series and cusp forms, but also new elements that reflect the higher homotopical information. The connection to the moduli stack Mell means that tmf captures the "geometry" of elliptic curves in a homotopical sense, extending the classical theory of modular forms to a more general, structured setting.

Furthermore, tmf plays a crucial role in understanding string bordism theory. The Thom spectrum MString, which classifies string manifolds, has a canonical map to tmf. This map is induced by the Witten genus, a characteristic class valued in modular forms. The K(2)-localization of MString is closely related to tmf, illustrating how tmf serves as a universal object for height 2 phenomena in geometric contexts. The height 2 chromatic layer is often considered the most accessible "higher" layer beyond height 1 (which is related to K-theory), and tmf provides a concrete and extensively studied model for this layer, offering insights into the structure of the stable homotopy category at this specific chromatic height.

The study of tmf has led to significant advances, including the development of tools for computing its homotopy groups, which are notoriously difficult. It also provides a rich source of examples for E∞ ring spectra and their module categories, pushing the boundaries of our understanding of structured ring spectra and their connections to number theory and algebraic geometry.

The Chromatic Splitting of the K(n)-Local Sphere and its Implications

The K(n)-local sphere, denoted LK(n)S0, is the unit object in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category. Understanding its structure is paramount to understanding the entire K(n)-local category. A deep and intricate aspect of its structure is described by the Chromatic Splitting Conjecture, which postulates a decomposition of LK(n)S0 into simpler pieces. While originally a conjecture, significant parts of it have been proven, particularly for n=1 and n=2, and for all n at odd primes.

The conjecture, in its strongest form, suggests that LK(n)S0 splits as a wedge sum of suspensions of certain "atomic" K(n)-local spectra. More precisely, for a given prime p and height n, it suggests that LK(n)S0 is equivalent to a wedge sum of spectra of the form ΣkI(n), where I(n) is a specific K(n)-local spectrum. This splitting is not arbitrary; it is governed by the action of the Morava Stabilizer Group Gn and its subgroups. The components of the splitting are related to the homotopy fixed points of En under the action of finite subgroups of Gn.

For n=1, LK(1)S0 is equivalent to the p-adic K-theory spectrum K̂p. The splitting in this case is relatively straightforward, reflecting the simpler structure of G1. For n=2, at an odd prime p, the splitting of LK(2)S0 involves components related to the homotopy fixed points of E2 under the action of cyclic subgroups of G2. This provides a more complex, yet still manageable, decomposition.

The implications of the chromatic splitting are profound. If LK(n)S0 can be decomposed into simpler, more fundamental building blocks, it significantly simplifies the study of the entire K(n)-local category. Any K(n)-local spectrum X can be understood as a module over LK(n)S0. Therefore, a splitting of LK(n)S0 provides a corresponding decomposition of the K(n)-local category itself, potentially reducing its study to the study of module categories over simpler ring spectra. This is analogous to how the category of modules over a ring R splits if R is a direct sum of simpler rings.

The proof of aspects of the Chromatic Splitting Conjecture involves sophisticated techniques, including the use of the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence, the Gross-Hopkins spectral sequence, and detailed analysis of the representation theory of the Morava Stabilizer Group. While the full conjecture remains open in some cases (particularly for n ≥ 3 at p=2), the progress made has already reshaped our understanding of the K(n)-local stable homotopy categories and their role in the overall chromatic picture. It provides a structural decomposition that reveals the underlying symmetries and periodicities inherent in the stable homotopy groups of spheres.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the Chromatic Tower with the Postnikov Tower: While both are filtration constructions, the chromatic tower filters by Bousfield classes (chromatic height) using localizations, whereas the Postnikov tower filters by homotopy groups, using Eilenberg-MacLane spaces.
  • Assuming the Telescope Conjecture is Universally True: Remember that the Telescope Conjecture is false in general (e.g., for n ≥ 2 at p=2), though it holds for n=1 and for all n at odd primes. Do not apply it indiscriminately.
  • Misattributing the Action of Gn: The Morava Stabilizer Group Gn acts on the Morava E-theory spectrum En, not on an arbitrary K(n)-local spectrum LnX. The equivalence LK(n)S0 ≅ EnhGn is specific to the sphere spectrum.
  • Overgeneralizing the Chromatic Convergence Theorem: The theorem states that for a finite spectrum X, X → limn LnX is an equivalence. This convergence does not necessarily hold for all infinite spectra.
  • Simplifying the Relationship between tmf and Modular Forms: While tmf is deeply connected to classical modular forms, its homotopy groups are not identical to the classical ring of modular forms; they contain additional topological information and structure.
  • Assuming the Chromatic Splitting Conjecture is Fully Proven: While significant progress has been made, especially for n=1, n=2 (odd primes), and all n (odd primes), the full conjecture for all n and all primes (especially p=2, n ≥ 3) remains an active area of research.

The Transchromatic Character Map and Generalized Character Theory

The transchromatic character map provides a bridge between Morava E-theory at different heights, generalizing the classical character theory of finite groups to the chromatic setting. For a finite group G, the classical character map relates the representation ring R(G) to class functions on conjugacy classes. In chromatic homotopy theory, the transchromatic character map, developed by Ando, Hopkins, Rezk, Strickland, and others, relates the Morava E-theory of BG to functions on the scheme of homomorphisms from a formal group of height n to the Lubin-Tate formal group. Specifically, for a finite group G and a prime p, there is a map E_n0(BG) → Mapcts(Homcts(G, G_n), E_n0) where G_n is the Morava stabilizer group. This map becomes an isomorphism after inverting certain Euler classes. The domain E_n0(BG) is a completed tensor product of copies of E_n0 indexed by conjugacy classes of commuting p-tuples in G. The target involves the scheme Hom(ℤ_p^n, G) of continuous homomorphisms from the p-adic integers to G. The transchromatic character map is compatible with the chromatic fracture square and respects the action of the Morava stabilizer group. It plays a crucial role in the computation of E_n0(BG) for specific groups, in the study of power operations in Morava E-theory, and in the formulation of the chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture for p-groups. The generalized character theory extends this to compact Lie groups and p-compact groups, where the target becomes a scheme of homomorphisms from a p-adic torus to the p-compact group. This theory provides a powerful computational tool and a conceptual framework for understanding the relationship between chromatic layers.

The K(n)-Local Category as a Derived Category of Sheaves on the Lubin-Tate Stack

The geometric perspective on the K(n)-local stable homotopy category, pioneered by Lurie and building on work of Goerss-Hopkins-Miller, identifies the K(n)-local category with the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the Lubin-Tate stack MLT^n. The Lubin-Tate stack classifies deformations of a height n formal group over a perfect field of characteristic p, together with a trivialization of its p-divisible group. The Morava E-theory spectrum E_n represents the global sections of the structure sheaf on this stack. The action of the Morava stabilizer group G_n on E_n corresponds to the automorphisms of the universal deformation. The K(n)-localization of a spectrum X corresponds to the derived global sections of a sheaf on MLT^n associated to X. This equivalence is not merely abstract; it provides a concrete dictionary: K(n)-local spectra correspond to quasi-coherent sheaves, smash product corresponds to derived tensor product, and the K(n)-local sphere corresponds to the structure sheaf. The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence for the K(n)-local sphere becomes the descent spectral sequence for the structure sheaf on the stack. The Gross-Hopkins duality corresponds to Serre duality on the stack. The Picard group of the K(n)-local category corresponds to the group of invertible sheaves. This geometric viewpoint has been instrumental in proving the chromatic splitting conjecture for n=2 at odd primes, in understanding the classification of smashing localizations, and in the development of the theory of topological modular forms. It also provides a framework for the chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis, where fully dualizable objects in the K(n)-local category correspond to certain perfect complexes on the Lubin-Tate stack.

Worked Computation: Transchromatic Character Map for Cyclic Groups
Let G = Cp^k be a cyclic p-group of order p^k. Compute the transchromatic character map E_n0(BCp^k) → Map(Hom(Cp^k, G_n), E_n0) and identify its image.
For G = Cp^k, the continuous homomorphisms Homcts(Cp^k, G_n) correspond to elements g ∈ G_n such that gp^k = 1. Since G_n is a pro-p-group of dimension n2, its p^k-torsion subgroup is finite. The Morava E-theory E_n0(BCp^k) is isomorphic to E_n0[[x]]/([p^k](x)) where [p^k](x) is the p^k-series of the Lubin-Tate formal group law. The transchromatic character map sends the coordinate x to the function g ↦ logLT(g) where logLT is the Lubin-Tate logarithm. The map becomes an isomorphism after inverting the Euler classes e(g) = [p^k]'(logLT(g)) for g ≠ 1. The image consists of functions f: Hom(Cp^k, G_n) → E_n0 satisfying the congruence f(g) ≡ f(1) mod (logLT(g)) for all g. This reflects the fact that E_n0(BCp^k) is the ring of functions on the scheme of p^k-torsion points of the Lubin-Tate formal group.
Exam Strategy: Geometric vs. Algebraic Perspectives
When a question asks about the K(n)-local category, decide whether the algebraic (spectra, Bousfield localization) or geometric (stacks, sheaves) perspective is more efficient. For computational questions (homotopy groups, spectral sequences), the algebraic Adams-Novikov approach is usually more direct. For structural questions (duality, Picard group, classification of localizations), the geometric stack perspective often gives cleaner answers. Know the dictionary: K(n)-local sphere structure sheaf, Gross-Hopkins duality Serre duality, Picard group invertible sheaves, smashing localizations support conditions on the stack.

The Generalized Tate Construction, Norm Maps, and Chromatic Equivariant Homotopy Theory

The generalized Tate construction and norm maps provide the chromatic approach to equivariant homotopy theory, extending the classical Tate construction from finite groups to compact Lie groups and p-compact groups. For a finite group G and a G-spectrum X, the Tate construction XtG is the cofiber of the norm map XhG → XhG from homotopy orbits to homotopy fixed points. In chromatic homotopy theory, the K(n)-local Tate construction for a finite group G is closely related to the transchromatic character map. The K(n)-localization of the Tate construction (XtG)K(n) vanishes for many spectra X, a phenomenon known as the Tate vanishing theorem. This vanishing is equivalent to the statement that the norm map becomes an equivalence after K(n)-localization. For compact Lie groups, the generalized Tate construction involves the geometric fixed points and the Tate diagonal. The chromatic approach to equivariant homotopy theory, developed by Greenlees, May, Hovey-Strickland, and others, uses the family of subgroups of a compact Lie group to filter the category of G-spectra. The K(n)-localization of the Burnside ring A(G) and the representation ring R(G) are computed using the transchromatic character map. The norm maps for the inclusion of subgroups H ⊂ G induce maps on K(n)-local categories that are analyzed using the geometry of the Lubin-Tate stack. This theory has applications to the chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture for p-groups, the computation of the K(n)-local homotopy groups of classifying spaces BG, and the study of power operations in Morava E-theory. The generalized Tate construction also plays a role in the cyclotomic trace map from algebraic K-theory to topological cyclic homology, where the Tate construction for the circle group appears.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of p-Compact Groups and the Classification of K(n)-Local Spectra with Group Actions

p-Compact groups, introduced by Dwyer and Wilkerson, are the p-complete analogues of compact Lie groups. They are classified by their Weyl groups and root data, generalizing the classification of compact Lie groups. In chromatic homotopy theory, p-compact groups arise naturally as the automorphism groups of certain K(n)-local spectra. The Morava stabilizer group G_n is a profinite group that contains finite subgroups corresponding to the automorphism groups of the Lubin-Tate formal group. These finite subgroups are related to p-compact groups. The K(n)-local category admits an action of the Morava stabilizer group, and the homotopy fixed points under finite subgroups yield K(n)-local spectra with interesting properties. The classification of K(n)-local spectra with an action of a finite group G is related to the transchromatic character map and the geometry of the Lubin-Tate stack. For a finite subgroup G ⊂ G_n, the homotopy fixed points E_nhG is a K(n)-local E_∞ ring spectrum. The classification of such homotopy fixed points, and more generally of K(n)-local G-spectra, is a major open problem. The chromatic approach to p-compact groups uses the theory of topological modular forms and the geometry of the moduli stack of formal groups. The Weyl group of a p-compact group acts on its maximal torus, and this action is reflected in the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin-Tate formal group. The generalized character theory for p-compact groups, developed by Ando, Hopkins, Rezk, and others, provides a transchromatic character map for the classifying space of a p-compact group. This map relates the Morava E-theory of the classifying space to functions on the scheme of homomorphisms from the p-adic torus to the p-compact group. The classification of K(n)-local spectra with group actions has implications for the chromatic splitting conjecture, the Picard group, and the theory of topological modular forms.

The Chromatic Approach to the Cobordism Hypothesis and Fully Dualizable Objects in K(n)-Local Categories

The cobordism hypothesis, proved by Lurie, classifies fully extended topological quantum field theories in terms of fully dualizable objects in symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-categories. In chromatic homotopy theory, the K(n)-local stable homotopy category is a symmetric monoidal ∞-category, and its fully dualizable objects can be analyzed using the geometric perspective. An object in the K(n)-local category is fully dualizable if and only if it is a compact object, i.e., a finite K(n)-local spectrum. The geometric interpretation identifies the K(n)-local category with the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the Lubin-Tate stack MLT^n. Under this equivalence, the compact objects correspond to perfect complexes on the stack. The duality structure corresponds to the derived Hom and tensor product of perfect complexes. The cobordism hypothesis then implies that fully extended TQFTs valued in the K(n)-local category are classified by perfect complexes on MLT^n equipped with certain additional structure (an action of the Morava stabilizer group, or equivalently, a descent datum). This provides a chromatic approach to the classification of TQFTs. The Morava E-theory spectrum E_n, as the global sections of the structure sheaf, corresponds to the trivial TQFT. The K(n)-local sphere corresponds to the structure sheaf. The Gross-Hopkins dualizing object corresponds to the dualizing sheaf on the stack. The Picard group of invertible objects corresponds to the group of line bundles. This perspective connects chromatic homotopy theory to the broader program of geometric representation theory and the Langlands program, where the Lubin-Tate stack plays a role analogous to the moduli stack of local systems.

  • Confusing the transchromatic character map (which relates different heights) with the ordinary character map (which relates representation theory to class functions at a fixed height).
  • Assuming the K(n)-local category is equivalent to the derived category of sheaves on the Lubin-Tate stack as abelian categories; the equivalence is of stable ∞-categories, and the t-structures do not match.
  • Overlooking that the Tate vanishing theorem for finite groups requires K(n)-localization; the non-localized Tate construction rarely vanishes.
  • Treating p-compact groups as simply p-completions of compact Lie groups; exotic p-compact groups exist that do not come from Lie groups.
  • Assuming all K(n)-local spectra with group actions arise as homotopy fixed points of E_n; this is false for non-finite groups and for actions not factoring through G_n.
  • Confusing the compact objects in the K(n)-local category (finite K(n)-local spectra) with the compact objects in the global stable homotopy category (finite spectra); K(n)-localization does not preserve compactness.
  • Neglecting that the chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis requires the symmetric monoidal structure to be compatible with the geometric equivalence; the smash product must correspond to derived tensor product of sheaves.

Power Operations in Morava E-Theory and the Dyer-Lashof Algebra at Height n

The Morava E-theory spectrum En carries a rich structure of power operations that generalize the classical Dyer-Lashof operations in mod p homology and the Steenrod operations in mod p cohomology. For a prime p and height n, the extended Morava stabilizer group Gn = Sn ⋊ Gal(Fpn/Fp) acts on En by E∞ ring maps. The homotopy fixed points EnhGn recover the K(n)-local sphere. The power operations on En are encoded by the action of the E∞ operad on the Lubin-Tate formal group law. Concretely, for each k ≥ 0 there is a power operation Pk : En0(X) → En0(X) satisfying the Cartan formula and the Adem relations. These operations are determined by the p-typical formal group law F over En0 and the p-series [p]F(x) = vnxpn + ⋯. The operation P0 is the Frobenius lift φ on En0, and the higher operations Pk for k > 0 are determined by the coefficients of the p-series. The algebra of power operations is isomorphic to the completed group ring Zp[[Gn]] under a suitable completion. This isomorphism, due to Ando, Hopkins, and Strickland, identifies the power operation Pk with the action of a specific element in the Morava stabilizer group. The Dyer-Lashof algebra at height n is the algebra of stable operations on the homology theory En*(−) that commute with the action of the Morava stabilizer group. It is generated by operations Qk of degree k(p−1)pn subject to the Adem relations and the relation Qk2 = 0 for p = 2. The action of the Dyer-Lashof algebra on En*(X) is compatible with the power operations on En*(X) via the Kronecker pairing. This structure is essential for the computation of the K(n)-local Adams-Novikov spectral sequence and for the construction of the chromatic spectral sequence. The power operations also control the E∞ structure on En and its homotopy fixed points. For a finite subgroup H ⊂ Gn, the homotopy fixed points EnhH inherit power operations from En, and the fixed point spectral sequence for H is a spectral sequence of algebras over the Dyer-Lashof algebra. The Goerss-Hopkins obstruction theory for E∞ structures on K(n)-local spectra is formulated in terms of power operations and their higher coherences. The space of E∞ structures on a K(n)-local spectrum X is controlled by the André-Quillen cohomology of the algebra of power operations acting on En*(X). This perspective connects the chromatic homotopy theory of power operations to the deformation theory of formal groups and the geometry of the Lubin-Tate stack.

The Adams-Novikov E2-Term as Continuous Cohomology of the Morava Stabilizer Group

The E2-term of the K(n)-local Adams-Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere is canonically isomorphic to the continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group Gn with coefficients in the Morava module En*. Specifically, E2s,t ≅ Hcs(Gn; Ent) where En* = W(Fpn)[[u1, …, un-1]][u±1] with |u| = −2 and |ui| = 0. The action of Gn on En* is through its action on the Lubin-Tate formal group law: the strict automorphisms Sn act by changing coordinates, and the Galois group acts on the coefficients W(Fpn). This isomorphism is the Morava change-of-rings theorem, which identifies the E2-term of the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence based on En with the continuous group cohomology of Gn. The continuous cohomology Hc*(Gn; M) for a profinite group Gn and a discrete Gn-module M is computed as the direct limit of the cohomology of the finite quotients Gn/U where U ranges over open normal subgroups. For the Morava module En*, the action is not discrete but continuous; one uses the completed group ring Zp[[Gn]] and the category of continuous modules. The E2-term is concentrated in even topological degrees when p is odd, but for p = 2 there are odd-degree classes. The cohomological dimension of Gn is n2 + 1 for p > 2 and n2 + n + 1 for p = 2, which determines the vanishing line of the spectral sequence. The E2-term carries a weight grading coming from the action of the multiplicative group Gm on the Lubin-Tate formal group, corresponding to the Adams grading. The differentials in the spectral sequence are derivations with respect to the Yoneda product on cohomology. The dr differentials for r < n2 + 1 are determined by the action of the Steenrod algebra on the cohomology of Gn. The Gross-Hopkins duality provides a perfect pairing Hcs(Gn; Ent) ⊗ Hcn2+1−s(Gn; En−t−n) → Qp/Zp which is compatible with the Poincaré duality in the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence. The computation of Hc*(Gn; En*) for small n and p is a major computational project. For n = 1, G1 ≅ Zp× and the cohomology is the p-adic completion of the cohomology of the cyclic group of order p−1, giving the image of J. For n = 2, the cohomology of G2 was computed by Shimomura, Wang, and others using the spectral sequence for the extension 1 → S2 → G2 → Gal → 1. The E2-term for n = 2, p ≥ 5 is known to be generated by classes h1, h2, v2 with relations reflecting the structure of the elliptic curve. For n = 3, the cohomology of G3 is largely unknown beyond low degrees. The chromatic spectral sequence converges to this E2-term, providing a filtration by height.

Worked Computation: Hc1(G1; E10) at p = 2
Compute the first continuous cohomology group Hc1(G1; E10) where G1 = Z2× ≅ Z/2 × Z2 acts on E10 = Z2 via the determinant character (the action of Z2× on the Lubin-Tate formal group of height 1).
The group G1 = Z2× ≅ {±1} × (1 + 2Z2) ≅ Z/2 × Z2. The action on E10 = Z2 is via the determinant: the generator −1 acts by −1, and the pro-2 part 1+2Z2 acts trivially. Continuous cohomology Hc1(G1; Z2(1)) where Z2(1) denotes the Tate twist (action by the cyclotomic character, which equals the determinant here). By the Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence for 1 → Z2 → G1 → Z/2 → 1, we have E2p,q = Hp(Z/2; Hcq(Z2; Z2(1))). Since Z2 acts trivially on Z2(1) (the pro-2 part acts trivially), Hc0(Z2; Z2(1)) = Z2(1) and Hc1(Z2; Z2(1)) = Homcont(Z2, Z2(1)) ≅ Z2(1). The Z/2 action on these is by −1. Then H1(Z/2; Z2(1)) = Z2(1)/2Z2(1) ≅ Z/2 and H0(Z/2; Z2(1)) = 0. The spectral sequence gives Hc1(G1; Z2(1)) ≅ Z/2. This class corresponds to the element η in the 1-stem of the 2-primary stable homotopy groups of spheres, detected in the Adams-Novikov spectral sequence by h1 ∈ E21,2.
Exam Strategy: Morava Change-of-Rings
When asked to identify the E2-term of the K(n)-local ANSS, write Hc*(Gn; En*) immediately. For n=1, recall G1 ≅ Zp× and the cohomology computes the image of J. For n=2, p≥5, the E2-term is generated by h1, h2, v2 with v2 permanent. The Gross-Hopkins duality pairing Hcs ⊗ Hcn2+1−s → Qp/Zp is a favorite source of true/false questions: it shifts topological degree by −n, not 0.

The Honda Formal Group Law and the Explicit Construction of Morava K-Theories

The Honda formal group law provides an explicit p-typical formal group law of height n over Fp that is used to construct the Morava K-theory spectrum K(n). For a prime p and integer n ≥ 1, the Honda formal group law Hn is defined by the p-series [p]Hn(x) = xpn. This is the unique (up to strict isomorphism) p-typical formal group law of height n over Fp with this p-series. The coefficients of Hn lie in Fp and are determined recursively by the condition that the logarithm logHn(x) = ∑k≥0 xpnk/pk converges in the p-adic completion. The Morava K-theory K(n) is the complex-oriented ring spectrum whose coefficient ring is K(n)* = Fp[vn±1] with |vn| = 2(pn−1), and whose formal group law is the Honda formal group law Hn. The periodicity generator vn corresponds to the coefficient of xpn in the p-series. The construction of K(n) as a spectrum uses the Baas-Sullivan theory of bordism with singularities or the Landweber exact functor theorem. The Landweber exact functor theorem applies because the sequence (p, v1, …, vn-1) is regular on MU* and vn acts invertibly on the resulting module. The Honda formal group law is the reduction mod p of the Lubin-Tate formal group law over W(Fpn)[[u1, …, un-1]]. The Morava stabilizer group Sn is the group of strict automorphisms of Hn over Fpn. It is a profinite group of dimension n2 over Zp. The endomorphism ring of Hn is the non-commutative ring En = W(Fpn)⟨S⟩/(S a = apS, Sn = p) where S is the Frobenius operator. This is the Morava stabilizer group ring. The Honda formal group law is also the formal group law of the Lubin-Tate formal group at the closed point of the Lubin-Tate space where all deformation parameters ui = 0. The Morava K-theory K(n) is a field object in the stable homotopy category: every module over K(n) is free, and the Künneth spectral sequence collapses. The K(n)-local category is equivalent to the derived category of modules over the E∞ ring spectrum K(n). The Honda formal group law admits a universal deformation over the Lubin-Tate ring W(Fpn)[[u1, …, un-1]], and the Morava E-theory En represents this deformation. The map En → K(n) is the quotient by the maximal ideal (p, u1, …, un-1). The chromatic spectral sequence for K(n) is trivial because K(n) is already of height exactly n. The Adams-Novikov spectral sequence based on K(n) has E2-term ExtK(n)*K(n)(K(n)*, K(n)*) which is the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group with coefficients in K(n)*. The Honda formal group law also appears in the construction of the Johnson-Wilson theories E(n) and the truncated Brown-Peterson spectra BP⟨n⟩. The p-typical formal group law of BP⟨n⟩ reduces to the Honda formal group law mod (p, v1, …, vn-1). The Landweber exact functor theorem for K(n) requires checking that the sequence (p, v1, …, vn-1) is regular on MU* and that vn is a unit in the quotient. This is verified by the explicit formula for the p-series of the Honda formal group law.

The Chromatic Approach to the Adams Conjecture and the Image of J

The Adams conjecture, proved by Quillen and Sullivan, describes the image of the stable J-homomorphism J : πk(O) → πkS in terms of the action of the Adams operations ψk on the topological K-theory of the sphere. Chromatic homotopy theory provides a conceptual framework for this result by identifying the image of J with the K(1)-local sphere. At height 1 and prime p, the Morava K-theory K(1) is the p-completion of the Adams summand of KU, and the Morava E-theory E(1) is the p-complete complex K-theory KUp∧. The Morava stabilizer group G1 = Zp× acts on E(1) by the Adams operations: a ∈ Zp× acts as ψa. The homotopy fixed points E(1)hG1 is the K(1)-local sphere LK(1)S0. The K(1)-local Adams-Novikov spectral sequence has E2-term Hc*(Zp×; Zp[u±1]) which computes the p-primary image of J. The E2-term is concentrated in cohomological degrees 0 and 1. The degree 0 part is the invariants Zp[u±1]Zp× = Zp[u±1] corresponding to the stable stems that are in the image of J. The degree 1 part is the coinvariants, which correspond to the cokernel of J. The differential d1 in the spectral sequence is the boundary map in the long exact sequence of the fiber sequence LK(1)S0 → E(1) → E(1) where the second map is ψa − 1 for a topological generator a of Zp×. The image of J at the prime p is the subgroup of π*S(p) detected by K(1)-local homotopy. The chromatic fracture square for the sphere at height 1 is S0(p) → LK(1)S0 × LK(0)S0 → LK(0)LK(1)S0. Since LK(0)S0 = HQ and LK(0)LK(1)S0 = HQ, the fracture square shows that the p-local sphere is built from the K(1)-local sphere (the image of J) and rational homotopy. The Adams operations ψk on KU correspond to the action of k ∈ Zp× on the Lubin-Tate formal group of height 1. The Adams conjecture states that the map ψk − 1 : BU → BU becomes null after applying the J-homomorphism. Chromatically, this is the statement that the map ψk − 1 on E(1) induces the zero map on homotopy fixed points E(1)hG1 = LK(1)S0. The proof uses the fact that the K(1)-local sphere is the homotopy fixed points of the action of G1 on E(1), and the map ψk − 1 is homotopic to the action of k − 1, which is in the augmentation ideal of the group ring. The image of J is also described by the K(1)-local spherical Witt vectors. The K(1)-local sphere is equivalent to the K(1)-localization of the spherical Witt vectors WS(Fp). The Adams operations act on WS(Fp) by the usual Witt vector Frobenius and Verschiebung. The chromatic approach to the Adams conjecture generalizes to higher heights: the K(n)-local sphere is the homotopy fixed points of Gn on En, and the "higher Adams operations" are the action of the Morava stabilizer group. The image of the higher J-homomorphisms (from the homotopy groups of the units of En) is detected by the K(n)-local sphere. The redshift conjecture predicts that the algebraic K-theory of K(n)-local spectra has chromatic height n+1, which is a higher analog of the fact that K(KU) has height 2.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Real-Oriented Spectra and the Anderson Dual of the Sphere

Real-oriented spectra are ring spectra equipped with a map from the real K-theory spectrum KO or, more generally, a complex orientation that factors through the real orientation. The chromatic homotopy theory of real-oriented spectra reveals phenomena at heights 1 and 2 that are invisible in the complex-oriented setting. The Anderson dual of the sphere IZ is the spectrum representing the functor X ↦ Hom(π−*(X), Z) ⊕ Ext(π−*−1(X), Z). Its K(n)-localization LK(n)IZ is closely related to the Gross-Hopkins dual of the K(n)-local sphere. For n = 1, LK(1)IZ ≅ Σ−1LK(1)S0 at odd primes, reflecting the self-duality of the K(1)-local sphere up to a shift. For n = 2, the Anderson dual of the K(2)-local sphere at p = 3 is related to the spectrum tmf and the Witten genus. The real K-theory spectrum KO has chromatic height 1 at p = 2 and height 0 at odd primes. The K(1)-localization of KO at p = 2 is the connective cover of the K(1)-local sphere. The K(2)-localization of KO at p = 2 is trivial, but the K(2)-localization of the connective real K-theory ko is related to the K(2)-local sphere via the action of the Morava stabilizer group G2 at p = 2. The spectrum TMF (topological modular forms) is a real-oriented spectrum whose K(2)-localization at p = 2, 3 captures the height 2 chromatic layer. The Anderson dual IZ admits a chromatic filtration whose layers are the Gross-Hopkins duals of the K(n)-local spheres. The K(n)-local Anderson dual LK(n)IZ is equivalent to the Gross-Hopkins dual In of the K(n)-local sphere, shifted by −n2−n−1 at p = 2 and −n2−1 at odd primes. This equivalence uses the fact that the Morava module En* is a free module of rank 1 over the completed group ring Zp[[Gn]], and the Anderson dual corresponds to the Matlis dual in the category of continuous Gn-modules. The real orientation on a spectrum E gives rise to a map KO → E, which induces maps on K(n)-localizations. At p = 2, the K(1)-localization of KO is the K(1)-local sphere, so any real-oriented spectrum receives a map from the K(1)-local sphere. At p = 2, the K(2)-localization of KO is trivial, but the K(2)-localization of the Anderson dual IZ is the Gross-Hopkins dual I2. The spectrum tmf is a real-oriented E∞ ring spectrum whose K(2)-localization at p = 2 is a form of the K(2)-local sphere with an action of the finite subgroup G24 ⊂ G2 of order 24. The homotopy fixed points E2hG24 is closely related to tmf. The Anderson dual of tmf is related to the K(2)-local sphere via the Gross-Hopkins duality. The chromatic approach to real-oriented spectra uses the theory of Real E∞ ring spectra (in the sense of Atiyah) and the action of the Galois group Z/2 on complex-oriented spectra. The K(n)-local category at p = 2 admits a Real structure, and the homotopy fixed points under the Real structure give the K(n)-localization of real-oriented spectra. The Anderson dual IZ is a Real spectrum, and its K(n)-localization inherits a Real structure. The Gross-Hopkins dual In is the K(n)-localization of the Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere. The Picard group of the K(n)-local category at p = 2 contains exotic invertible objects that are detected by real-oriented spectra. The chromatic fracture square for real-oriented spectra relates the K(n)-localizations to the K(n-1)-localizations via the fiber of the map ψk − 1 for k a topological generator of the units in the coefficient ring.

  • Confusing the Honda formal group law (defined over Fp with [p](x) = xpn) with the Lubin-Tate formal group law (defined over W(Fpn)[[u1, …, un-1]] with [p](x) = px + u1xp + ⋯ + xpn); the former is the reduction of the latter at the closed point.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Motivic Spectra and the Motivic Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence

The extension of chromatic homotopy theory to the motivic stable homotopy category SH(k) over a field k was pioneered by Morel, Voevodsky, and later developed by Hoyois, Isaksen, and others. The motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (MANSS) takes the form ExtMU_*MUs,t,w(MU_*, MU_*X) ⇒ πt-s,w(LMUX) where the weight w tracks the motivic Tate twist. Over ℂ, Betti realization induces a map from the MANSS to the classical ANSS that is an isomorphism on the E2-page after inverting the motivic Hopf map η ∈ π1,1(S0,0) and completing at a prime p. The motivic Steenrod algebra and the motivic dual Steenrod algebra govern the E2-term via the motivic version of the change-of-rings theorem. The motivic Morava K-theories K(n)mot and motivic Morava E-theories E_nmot are constructed using the motivic Lubin–Tate theory of p-divisible groups over the motivic base. The motivic chromatic filtration is indexed by height n and weight w, and the motivic thick subcategory theorem (Hoyois–Kelly–Østvær) classifies thick tensor ideals in the K(n)mot-local motivic category. The motivic Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the motivic Anderson dual of the K(n)mot-local sphere with a shift of the motivic Gross–Hopkins dual, where the shift depends on n, p, and the weight. The motivic redshift conjecture predicts that the algebraic K-theory of a motivic K(n)-local ring spectrum has motivic chromatic height n+1. Computations by Isaksen, Wang, and Xu using the motivic May spectral sequence and the motivic Adams spectral sequence have determined the 2-primary motivic stable stems over ℂ up to stem 60, revealing new η-periodic and ν-periodic families that have no classical analogues.

The Chromatic Approach to the Lichtenbaum–Quillen Conjecture and Étale K-Theory

The Lichtenbaum–Quillen conjecture, proved by Voevodsky, Rost, Suslin, and others as a consequence of the Bloch–Kato conjecture (the norm residue isomorphism theorem), states that for a ring of integers O_K in a number field, the algebraic K-theory spectrum K(O_K) satisfies K(O_K)̂_p ≃ LK(1)K(O_K) for p odd, where the p-completion agrees with the K(1)-localization. Chromatically, this identifies the K(1)-local layer of K(O_K) with the étale K-theory spectrum Ket(O_K)̂_p, which is built from Galois cohomology of the absolute Galois group G_K = Gal(K̄/K). The higher chromatic analog, the Lichtenbaum–Quillen conjecture at height n, predicts that the K(n)-localization of K(O_K) is related to the continuous cohomology of G_K with coefficients in the Morava E-theory E_n. The work of Clausen–Mathew–Morrow on the K-theory of p-adic rings shows that for a p-adic ring R with perfect residue field, the K(n)-localization of K(R) is controlled by the topological cyclic homology TC(R) and its cyclotomic trace. The chromatic fracture square for K(R) relates LK(n)K(R) to LK(n-1)K(R) and the fiber of the cyclotomic trace map K(R) → TC(R). The étale descent spectral sequence for K-theory takes the form H^scont(G_K, π_t(E_n)) ⇒ πt-s(LK(n)K(O_K)̂_p), where the Morava stabilizer group action on E_n is twisted by the Galois action on the Lubin–Tate formal group. The Quillen–Lichtenbaum constant, which measures the difference between K-theory and étale K-theory, is detected by the K(1)-local sphere and its higher analogs. The work of Rognes on the K-theory of finite fields shows that K(F_q)̂_ℓ ≃ LK(1)K(F_q) for ℓ ≠ p, and the chromatic redshift predicts that K(K(F_q)) has height 2, which is verified by the computation of TC(F_q) via the Bökstedt–Hsiang–Madsen theorem.

Worked Problem: Chromatic Detection of v_n-Periodic Families
Let p = 2, n = 2. The Greek letter element β2/2 ∈ π14(S0) is detected in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence by an element in Ext2,16BP_*BP(BP_*, BP_*). Explain why β2/2 supports a v28-periodic family in the 2-primary stable homotopy groups of spheres, and compute the stem of the first element in this family after β2/2 itself.
The element β2/2 corresponds to the invariant prime ideal I2 = (2, v1) in BP_*. By the periodicity theorem (Hopkins–Smith, Ravenel), any element in the Adams–Novikov E_∞-page that is not annihilated by v_n generates a v_n^N-periodic family for some N. For n = 2 at p = 2, the Morava stabilizer group G2 has cohomological dimension 4, and the Gross–Hopkins duality implies that the v2-periodicity exponent is 8. The element β2/2 lies in stem 14. The periodicity theorem guarantees that multiplication by v28 induces an isomorphism on the K(2)-local homotopy groups in sufficiently high stems. The first element in the v28-periodic family after β2/2 is v28 ⋅ β2/2, which lies in stem 14 + 8|v2| = 14 + 8 ⋅ 6 = 62, since |v2| = 2(p2 - 1) = 6 at p = 2. This family is detected by the beta family in the ANSS, and its existence is a consequence of the non-nilpotence of v2 in the K(2)-local category.
Exam Strategy: Chromatic Height and Periodicity
When asked about v_n-periodic families, always identify the height n, the prime p, and the periodicity exponent N = |v_n| ⋅ (p^n - 1)/(p - 1) for the beta family, or N = |v_n| for the alpha family. Remember that the periodicity theorem guarantees v_n^N-periodicity for some N, but the minimal N is determined by the action of the Morava stabilizer group on E_n. For p = 2, n = 2, the minimal periodicity is v28; for p = 3, n = 2, it is v224. The Greek letter construction produces elements βt/s that generate these families. In multiple-choice questions, check whether the stem increment matches |v_n| ⋅ N.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Cyclotomic Spectra and the Cyclotomic Trace

The cyclotomic trace map trc: K(R) → TC(R) from algebraic K-theory to topological cyclic homology is the central tool in modern chromatic approaches to K-theory. The spectrum TC(R) is the homotopy fixed points of the circle action on the topological Hochschild homology THH(R): TC(R) ≃ THH(R)hS1. Chromatically, the K(n)-localization of TC(R) is controlled by the K(n)-localization of THH(R). The work of Hesselholt–Madsen, Nikolaus–Scholze, and others shows that for a p-adic ring R, the K(n)-local cyclotomic trace induces an equivalence LK(n)K(R) ≃ LK(n)TC(R) for n ≥ 1 after p-completion. The cyclotomic Frobenius φ_p: THH(R) → THH(R)tC_p and the Verschiebung V_p: THH(R)tC_p → THH(R) induce the cyclotomic structure. At height n, the Morava E-theory E_n carries a canonical Frobenius lift φ: E_n → E_ntC_p which is an equivalence after K(n)-localization. The cyclotomic trace at height n factors as K(R) → TC(R) → LK(n)TC(R) ≃ LK(n)THH(R)hS1. The Bökstedt periodicity theorem states that THH(F_p) ≃ F_p[σ] with |σ| = 2, and the K(n)-localization of THH(F_p) is the K(n)-local sphere. For a perfectoid ring R, the Hesselholt–Madsen filtration on TC(R) has graded pieces given by the de Rham–Witt complex WΩ_R^*, and the K(n)-localization of this filtration converges to the K(n)-local K-theory. The redshift conjecture predicts that if R is K(m)-local, then K(R) is K(m+1)-local. This is verified for R = KU (height 1) where K(KU) has height 2, and for R = E_n (height n) where the work of Clausen–Mathew–Morrow shows that TC(E_n) has height n+1. The cyclotomic trace also detects the image of the J-homomorphism: the map J: π_*(SO) → π_*(S0) factors through the K(1)-local sphere, and the cyclotomic trace on K(ℤ) detects the K(1)-local summand.

The Chromatic Approach to the Baum–Connes Conjecture and Assembly Maps

The Baum–Connes conjecture predicts that the assembly map μ: K_*^G(EG) → K_*(C^*_r(G)) from the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space for proper actions to the K-theory of the reduced group C^*-algebra is an isomorphism for a discrete group G. Chromatically, the assembly map can be analyzed through the lens of chromatic homotopy theory by considering the K(n)-localization of the group ring spectrum Σ^∞_+ G and the K(n)-local category of G-spectra. The work of Davis–Lück, Bartels–Lück–Reich, and others on the Farrell–Jones conjecture for algebraic K-theory has a chromatic analog: the chromatic assembly map μ_n: LK(n)(Σ^∞_+ EG) → LK(n)(Σ^∞_+ BG) relates the K(n)-local homotopy type of the classifying space for proper actions to that of the classifying space. For a group G of finite virtual cohomological dimension, the K(n)-local assembly map is an isomorphism if and only if the K(n)-local cohomological dimension of G is finite. The chromatic approach to the Novikov conjecture (homotopy invariance of higher signatures) uses the fact that the K(n)-localization of the signature operator is detected by the K(n)-local index map. The work of Kasparov, Skandalis, and Tu on the Baum–Connes conjecture for a-T-menable groups uses the Dirac–dual Dirac method, which has a chromatic interpretation: the Dirac element D ∈ KK^G(C0(EG), ℂ) and the dual Dirac element η ∈ KK^G(ℂ, C0(EG)) become invertible after K(n)-localization for all n. The chromatic fracture square for the group C^*-algebra relates LK(n)K_*(C^*_r(G)) to LK(n-1)K_*(C^*_r(G)) and the fiber of the map induced by the chromatic tower. For G = SL3(ℤ), the Baum–Connes conjecture is known, and the K(2)-local assembly map detects the v2-periodic families in the K-theory of the group ring. The chromatic approach also applies to the isomorphism conjecture in topological K-theory: the assembly map for the family of virtually cyclic subgroups is a K(n)-local equivalence for all n if and only if the group satisfies the strong Novikov conjecture.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Factorization Homology and Topological Chiral Homology

Factorization homology ∫_M A of an E_n-algebra A over a framed n-manifold M, developed by Lurie, Francis, and Ayala–Francis, provides a higher-categorical framework for chromatic homotopy theory. When A is a K(n)-local E_∞ ring spectrum, the factorization homology ∫_M A computes the K(n)-local topological chiral homology of M with coefficients in A. For M = S1, ∫S1 A ≃ THH(A), the topological Hochschild homology. For M a Riemann surface Σ_g of genus g, ∫Σ_g A is related to the mapping class group action on the K(n)-local category. The chromatic approach to factorization homology uses the fact that the K(n)-local category is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category, and the E_n-algebras in this category are classified by maps from the Lubin–Tate moduli stack MFG≤n to the moduli of E_n-algebras. The work of Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum on the chiral homology of categories over the Ran space shows that the K(n)-local factorization homology satisfies a chromatic version of the cobordism hypothesis: the value on a closed n-manifold is the K(n)-local homotopy fixed points of the action of the framed bordism category on A. For A = E_n, the Morava E-theory, the factorization homology ∫_M E_n is equivalent to the continuous cohomology of the mapping class group of M with coefficients in the Morava module E_n^*. The chromatic redshift in factorization homology predicts that if A is K(n)-local, then ∫_M A is K(n+1)-local for M of positive dimension. This is verified for M = S1 where THH(E_n) has height n+1. The factorization homology of the Morava K-theory K(n) over a surface Σ_g computes the K(n)-local homology of the moduli stack of G_n-bundles on Σ_g, where G_n is the Morava stabilizer group. The work of Ben-Zvi–Nadler on the character theory of categorical representations shows that the K(n)-local factorization homology of a 3-manifold M computes the K(n)-local invariants of the associated 3-dimensional topological field theory, which is the chromatic analog of the Witten–Reshetikhin–Turaev invariant.

  • Confusing the motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (which has a weight grading) with the classical ANSS; the weight tracks the Tate twist and is essential for the motivic chromatic filtration.
  • Assuming the Lichtenbaum–Quillen conjecture holds at all heights; it is a theorem only at height 1 (K(1)-local), while higher heights remain conjectural and involve the Galois action on Morava E-theory.
  • Overlooking that the cyclotomic trace K(R) → TC(R) is a K(n)-local equivalence for all n ≥ 1 only after p-completion and for p-adic rings; it fails for general rings without completion.
  • Misstating the periodicity exponent for v_n-periodic families; the minimal exponent is not always |v_n| but depends on the action of the Morava stabilizer group (e.g., v28 at p=2, v224 at p=3).
  • Confusing the Baum–Connes assembly map in operator K-theory with the chromatic assembly map in stable homotopy; the former targets K_*(C^*_r(G)), the latter targets LK(n)π_*(Σ^∞_+ BG).
  • Assuming factorization homology ∫_M A preserves chromatic height; the redshift conjecture predicts ∫_M A has height n+1 when A has height n and dim M > 0.
  • Neglecting the role of the Morava stabilizer group G_n in the chromatic approach to the Novikov conjecture; the K(n)-local index map factors through the continuous cohomology of G_n.

The Role of Higher Real K‑Theories in the Chromatic Filtration

Higher real K‑theories, denoted E_nhG for a finite subgroup G ⊂ 𝔾_n (the Morava stabilizer group), interpolate between the purely algebraic Morava K‑theories K(n) and the Lubin–Tate spectra E_n. They were introduced by Hopkins, Mahowald and Miller to capture the “real” forms of height‑n phenomena that arise from Galois descent. For a prime p and height n, the maximal finite subgroup of 𝔾_n is cyclic of order (p^n−1) when p is odd and a quaternionic group when p=2 and n≥2. The homotopy fixed‑point spectrum E_nhG is K(n)‑local and often exhibits periodicities that are shorter than the generic v_n‑periodicity of E_n, reflecting the extra symmetry imposed by G.

Key properties:

  • Homotopy groups. The homotopy groups π_* E_nhG are computed by the continuous cohomology H_c^*(G; π_*E_n). Because G is finite, this reduces to ordinary group cohomology, giving a finite‑dimensional algebra over the coefficient ring (W(𝔽p^n)[[u1,…,un−1]][u±1]).
  • Real orientations. When G contains the element −1 (the “complex conjugation” in 𝔾_n), the fixed‑point spectrum admits a genuine Real orientation in the sense of Hu–Kriz. This yields a C2‑equivariant refinement E_nhG → E_nhG with a “Real” formal group law, providing a bridge to equivariant chromatic phenomena.
  • Detection of v_n‑torsion. The map K(n) → E_nhG is often a K(n)‑local equivalence on homotopy groups up to a certain stem, allowing one to detect v_n‑torsion classes in the stable homotopy groups of spheres via the homotopy fixed‑point spectral sequence (HFPSS). For example, at p=2, n=2, the spectrum EO2 = E2hC4 detects the famous v2‑periodic families that generate the 2‑primary image of J.
  • Relation to topological modular forms. The height‑2 case with G = SL2(𝔽_p) recovers the spectrum TMF(p) after K(2)‑localization. This explains why TMF carries a rich supply of v2‑periodic elements and why its homotopy groups exhibit the “beta‑family” pattern.

From an exam perspective, candidates should be able to:

  1. State the definition of a higher real K‑theory as a homotopy fixed‑point spectrum E_nhG.
  2. Identify the maximal finite subgroup of 𝔾_n for given (p,n) and explain its impact on periodicity.
  3. Explain how the HFPSS for E_nhG is used to detect v_n‑periodic families, citing the EO2 example.
  4. Relate height‑2 higher real K‑theories to TMF and describe the resulting chromatic consequences.

Transchromatic Duality and the K(n)‑Local Picard Group

Transchromatic duality is the phenomenon whereby K(n)‑local spectra at different heights are linked by a duality that exchanges height‑n information with height‑(n−1) data. The central object governing this duality is the K(n)‑local Picard group PicK(n), the group of invertible K(n)‑local spectra under smash product. Hopkins and Miller showed that PicK(n) fits into a short exact sequence

0 → ℤ → PicK(n) → H1_c(𝔾_n; π0E_n^×) → 0,

where the ℤ‑summand records suspensions and the cohomology term encodes “exotic” invertibles arising from non‑trivial 𝔾_n‑actions on the unit of E_n. The exotic part is finite and often identified with the group of continuous characters of 𝔾_n.

Transchromatic duality manifests through the Gross–Hopkins dual I_n, an invertible K(n)‑local spectrum satisfying

I_n ∧ X ≃ FK(n)(X, SK(n))

for any K(n)‑local X, where F denotes the K(n)‑local function spectrum. I_n is itself a shift of a higher real K‑theory: I_n ≃ Σ−n2E_nh𝔾_n1, with 𝔾_n1 the kernel of the determinant character det : 𝔾_n → ℤ_p^×. This description shows that dualizing at height n lowers the effective height by one, because the determinant kernel acts trivially on the associated formal group law, leaving a height‑(n−1) structure.

Consequences for calculations:

  • The Anderson dual of the K(n)‑local sphere, DK(n)S, is equivalent to I_n up to a suspension, providing a concrete model for the dualizing object.
  • When computing the K(n)‑local homotopy groups of a finite spectrum X, one may first smash with I_n to obtain a “dual” problem that often has simpler HFPSS differentials, especially when X carries a 𝔾_n‑action.
  • Exotic elements of PicK(n) give rise to “twisted” versions of Morava E‑theory, denoted E_nχ, where χ is a continuous character. These twists affect the differentials in the chromatic spectral sequence and can be used to construct counterexamples to the telescope conjecture at specific heights.

Exam‑level expectations include:

  1. Writing the exact sequence for PicK(n) and interpreting each term.
  2. Describing the construction of the Gross–Hopkins dual I_n and its relation to the determinant kernel.
  3. Explaining how transchromatic duality reduces a height‑n computation to height‑(n−1) data via I_n.
  4. Identifying an exotic invertible spectrum and outlining its effect on the chromatic spectral sequence.
Technique: Using the Gross–Hopkins Dual to Compute a K(2)‑Local Homotopy Group
Let X be the finite spectrum S0/2 (the mod‑2 Moore spectrum). Compute π_* (I2 ∧ X) in the range * = 0,…,10, assuming the known K(2)‑local homotopy groups of the sphere up to stem 10.
Step 1: Recall that I2 ≃ Σ−4E2h𝔾21. The HFPSS for E2h𝔾21 has E2‑term H^s(𝔾21; π_tE2). For low stems the only non‑zero groups occur for s=0, giving π_tE2h𝔾21 ≅ (π_tE2)𝔾21.
Step 2: The mod‑2 Moore spectrum kills the class η∈π1S, so the K(2)‑local sphere contribution in stems 0–10 reduces to the v2‑periodic families generated by β1 and β2. Their images under the 𝔾21‑invariants are the classes β1𝔾 in degree 8 and β2𝔾 in degree 16, both of which lie outside the requested range.
Step 3: Consequently, for *≤10 the only contribution comes from the suspension Σ−4. Thus π_k(I2 ∧ X) ≅ πk+4X for k+4 ≤10, i.e. π0(I2 ∧ X)=π4X=0, π1(I2 ∧ X)=π5X=0, …, π6(I2 ∧ X)=π10X=ℤ/2, and all other groups vanish.
Step 4: Summarize: the non‑zero K(2)‑local homotopy groups of I2 ∧ S0/2 in the given range are π6 ≅ ℤ/2 and π8 ≅ ℤ/2, matching the shifted Moore pattern. This illustrates how the Gross–Hopkins dual converts a K(2)‑local computation into a simple suspension when the exotic part of PicK(2) acts trivially.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a computation involving I_n, first check whether the exotic Picard component is trivial for the given spectrum. If it is, replace I_n by the appropriate suspension of a homotopy fixed‑point spectrum and read off the answer from known low‑stem data. This avoids a full HFPSS analysis and saves valuable time.

Chromatic Nilpotence Filtration via Finite Subgroup Schemes of Formal Groups

The nilpotence theorem asserts that a map f : X→Y of finite spectra is nilpotent if and only if its image under each Morava K‑theory K(n) is nilpotent. A deeper structural view comes from the language of finite subgroup schemes of the universal formal group law over the Lubin–Tate moduli stack 𝔐FG^≤n. For a height‑n formal group 𝔽 over a perfect field k of characteristic p, the p‑torsion subgroup scheme 𝔽[p] is a finite flat group scheme of rank p^n. Its closed subgroup schemes correspond to sub‑formal groups of lower height, and the inclusion hierarchy mirrors the chromatic filtration.

Key points:

  • Stratification. The stack 𝔐FG^≤n admits a stratification by the height of the formal group: the closed substack of height ≤ m (m≤n) is defined by the vanishing of the (n−m)‑th invariant factor of the Honda formal group law. This yields a decreasing sequence of substacks whose complements are the “chromatic layers” of height exactly m.
  • Detection via subgroup schemes. Given a map f, its image under K(m) can be interpreted as the induced map on the cohomology of the classifying stack B𝔽[p^∞] truncated at height m. If f vanishes on all such subgroup schemes for m≤n, the nilpotence theorem guarantees that some power f^k is null.
  • Finite subgroup schemes and the X(n)‑construction. The spectra X(n) constructed by Ravenel are built by killing the homotopy of the sphere at all heights > n. Concretely, X(n) is the cofiber of a map Σ^∞B(ℤ/p)∧(n+1) → S, which corresponds to imposing the vanishing of the (n+1)‑st finite subgroup scheme of the universal formal group. This geometric viewpoint explains why X(n) detects nilpotence up to height n.
  • Application to periodic families. The existence of a non‑trivial finite subgroup scheme of order p^k forces the presence of a v_k‑periodic element in the stable homotopy groups. For example, the existence of a subgroup scheme of order p2 in a height‑2 formal group yields the β‑family detected by K(2).

Typical exam questions ask you to:

  1. Explain how the stratification of 𝔐FG^≤n encodes the chromatic filtration.
  2. Relate the vanishing of K(m)‑homology of a map to the triviality of its effect on the corresponding finite subgroup scheme.
  3. Describe the role of the X(n)‑construction in proving the nilpotence theorem, emphasizing the subgroup‑scheme perspective.
  4. Give an example of a finite subgroup scheme that produces a known v_n‑periodic family.

Derived Algebraic Geometry Meets Chromatic Homotopy: The Lubin–Tate Stack and Spectral Algebraic Stacks

Derived algebraic geometry (DAG) provides a language in which the entire chromatic hierarchy can be viewed as a sheaf of spectra over a spectral stack. The central object is the Lubin–Tate stack 𝔐LT^n, the derived moduli of deformations of a height‑n formal group 𝔽 over 𝔽p^n. It is presented as the quotient [Spf W(𝔽p^n)[[u1,…,un−1]] / 𝔾_n], where 𝔾_n acts by change of coordinates. The sheaf of E_∞‑ring spectra 𝔈_n on 𝔐LT^n assigns to each derived test algebra A the Morava E‑theory of the associated formal group over A.

Important features:

  • Spectral descent. The global sections Γ(𝔐LT^n, 𝔈_n) recover the K(n)‑local sphere SK(n). The descent spectral sequence associated to the cover by the affine chart Spf W[[u_i]] has E2‑term H^s_c(𝔾_n; π_tE_n), which is precisely the HFPSS used throughout chromatic homotopy.
  • Derived deformation theory. The cotangent complex L𝔐LT^n is concentrated in degree 0 and identified with the module of invariant differentials of the universal formal group. This yields a perfect obstruction theory that explains why the height‑n locus is smooth of dimension n−1 in the derived sense.
  • Interaction with topological modular forms. For n=2, the Lubin–Tate stack maps to the derived stack of elliptic curves 𝔐ell. Pullback of 𝔈2 along this map produces the sheaf of TMF, establishing a precise geometric bridge between height‑2 chromatic phenomena and modular forms.
  • Chromatic fracture via stacks. The chromatic fracture square can be reformulated as a pullback of spectral stacks:

𝔐LT≤n ≃ 𝔐LT≤n−1 ⨿𝔐LT≤n−1∩𝔐LT=n 𝔐LT=n,

where the intersection is the derived fiber product encoding the “gluing” of height‑(n−1) and height‑n data. This viewpoint clarifies why the fracture square is homotopy‑cartesian after applying global sections.

Exam expectations:

  1. State the presentation of 𝔐LT^n as a quotient stack and identify the role of 𝔾_n.
  2. Derive the descent spectral sequence from the affine cover and relate its E2‑term to continuous cohomology.
  3. Explain how the cotangent complex of 𝔐LT^n reflects the smoothness of the height‑n locus.
  4. Connect the height‑2 Lubin–Tate stack to TMF via the map to 𝔐ell and describe the resulting effect on homotopy groups.

Recent Progress on the Telescope Conjecture: Counterexamples, Localization, and the Role of Chromatic Fracture

The telescope conjecture posits that for each n≥0, the Bousfield localization LT(n) with respect to the telescope of a vn‑self map coincides with the K(n)‑localization LK(n). While true for n=0 and n=1, the conjecture fails for n≥2 in general. The first counterexample was produced by Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith using the existence of exotic elements in PicK(n) that are not detected by any finite vn‑periodic telescope.

Key developments:

  • Mahowald–Ravenel–Wickelgren construction. They exhibited a type‑2 spectrum V(1) whose telescope T(2) does not detect the exotic invertible I2. Consequently, LT(2)S ≠ LK(2)S, providing an explicit failure at height 2 for p≥5.
  • Chromatic fracture approach. By analyzing the chromatic fracture square for the sphere, one sees that the fiber of the map LK(n)S → LT(n)S is precisely the “chromatic residual” R_n, which is non‑trivial when exotic Picard elements exist. This residual can be described as the homotopy limit of the tower of localizations LK(m)S for m<n, twisted by the action of 𝔾_n.</li>
  • Localization via higher real K‑theories. The spectra EO_n = E_nhG for suitable finite G provide intermediate localizations LEO_n. Recent work shows that LEO_nS sits strictly between LT(n)S and LK(n)S, giving a refined hierarchy of telescopic localizations.
  • Impact on the thick subcategory theorem. The failure of the telescope conjecture implies that the lattice of smashing localizations of the stable homotopy category is richer than the chromatic tower alone predicts. In particular, there exist smashing localizations that are not generated by any finite type‑n spectrum.

For examinations, students should be prepared to:

  1. State the telescope conjecture and its known status for n=0,1 and n≥2.
  2. Describe the construction of a counterexample using an exotic element of PicK(2) and explain why it evades detection by T(2).
  3. Explain how the chromatic fracture square reveals the existence of the residual R_n and its role in distinguishing LK(n) from LT(n).
  4. Discuss the intermediate localization LEO_n and its significance in the refined hierarchy of chromatic localizations.

Chromatic Redshift in Algebraic K‑Theory and Its Computational Framework

Redshift is the phenomenon whereby algebraic K‑theory raises the chromatic height of a ring spectrum by one. The conjecture, originally formulated by Ausoni and Rognes, predicts that for a K(n)‑local E_∞‑ring R, the algebraic K‑theory spectrum K(R) is K(n+1)‑local after p‑completion. Substantial evidence comes from calculations for R = E_n, K(𝔽_p), and the connective K‑theory spectrum ku.

Computational framework:

  • Topological cyclic homology (TC). The cyclotomic trace map tr : K(R) → TC(R) is a p‑adic equivalence after K(n+1)‑localization. TC admits a filtration by the “Tate tower” whose layers are expressed in terms of THH(R)Cp^k and the Tate construction. The associated spectral sequence has E2‑term H^s(Cp^k; π_tTHH(R)), which can be identified with continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group when R = E_n.
  • Redshift for Morava E‑theory. For R = E_n, the THH‑spectral sequence collapses at a finite stage, and the resulting TC‑homotopy groups exhibit a new periodicity generator vn+1 of degree 2pn−2. This directly demonstrates that K(E_n) is K(n+1)‑local.
  • Ausoni–Rognes calculation for ku. They computed π_*K(ku) at the prime 2 up to a large stem, revealing a v2‑periodic family that does not appear in π_*ku itself. The computation uses the Bokstedt spectral sequence for THH(ku) and the Tate spectral sequence for TC(ku), together with careful analysis of differentials induced by the cyclotomic structure.
  • Redshift for finite fields. For R = 𝔽_p, K(𝔽_p) ≃ Hℤ_p, which is K(0)‑local. The conjecture predicts that K(K(𝔽_p)) should be K(1)‑local; indeed, the algebraic K‑theory of the sphere spectrum (the “K‑theory of the sphere”) exhibits v1‑periodic behavior, confirming the first step of redshift.

Exam‑relevant points:

  1. State the redshift conjecture and give the precise formulation in terms of K(n)‑localization.
  2. Explain why the cyclotomic trace is the primary computational tool, describing the role of THH and TC.
  3. Summarize the Ausoni–Rognes calculation for K(ku) and identify the new v2‑periodic class.
  4. Describe how the Tate spectral sequence reveals the emergence of a vn+1‑periodic generator in K(E_n).
  5. Provide a brief example illustrating the first step of redshift from K(𝔽_p) to K(K(𝔽_p)).
  • Confusing the exotic part of PicK(n) with ordinary suspensions; the exotic component is finite and often invisible in low‑stem calculations.
  • Assuming that every finite subgroup of the Morava stabilizer group yields a Real orientation; only those containing the element −1 give rise to genuine C2‑equivariant structures.
  • Claiming that the chromatic fracture square always splits after taking global sections; it is only homotopy‑cartesian, not a product.
  • Neglecting the Tate construction in TC computations; without it the redshift phenomenon cannot be observed.
  • Treating the telescope conjecture as true for all heights; counterexamples exist already at height 2 for many primes.
  • Believing that the Lubin–Tate stack is an ordinary algebraic stack; it is a derived spectral stack and its cotangent complex lives in degree 0.
  • Mixing up the HFPSS for E_nhG with the ANSS; the former computes homotopy fixed points, while the latter computes stable homotopy groups via formal group law data.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Elliptic Cohomology and the String Orientation of tmf

The spectrum of topological modular forms tmf occupies the chromatic height 2 layer as a global avatar of elliptic cohomology. Its construction via the compactified moduli stack M̄ell of generalized elliptic curves and a sheaf of E∞ ring spectra Otop (the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem) yields a connective spectrum whose homotopy groups encode modular forms. The string orientation is a map of E∞ ring spectra σ : MString → tmf constructed by Ando–Hopkins–Strickland, refining the Witten genus. Mechanistically, the orientation arises because the universal elliptic curve over Mell carries a canonical string structure on its vertical tangent bundle; the associated sigma-orientation of loop spaces lifts to a map of Thom spectra. The effect on homotopy groups is detected by the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for tmf: the image of σ in π*tmf contains the modular forms of level 1, and the kernel is precisely the ideal generated by the Witten genus of string manifolds that bound string manifolds. At the prime 2, the homotopy of tmf is computed via the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾2 on E2, with the chromatic fracture square relating tmf to its K(2)-localization LK(2)tmf ≃ E2h𝔾2 and its rationalization. The cuspidal and Eisenstein components of π*tmf correspond to the cuspidal and Eisenstein parts of the cohomology of 𝔾2.
