The string orientation is the unique map making the diagram commute with the Witten genus MString → MF*(SL2(ℤ)) and the q-expansion map tmf → KU[[q]]. For examiners, the key distinction is that tmf is connective and periodic only after inverting the discriminant Δ, yielding tmf[Δ−1] ≃ TMF, the periodic version. The compactified moduli stack M̄ell includes nodal curves, and the fiber of Otop over the cusp is KU, explaining the q-expansion map.

The Mahowald Invariant and the Chromatic Detection of vn-Periodic Families in the Stable Homotopy Groups of Spheres

The Mahowald invariant is a homomorphism M : ExtAs,t(𝔽2, 𝔽2) → πt−sS from the Adams E2-term at the prime 2 to the stable stems, defined by Mahowald in the 1970s and later interpreted chromatically by Ravenel. It detects v1-periodic families (the image of J) and v2-periodic families (the Greek letter elements β, β1, β2, …) by mapping certain permanent cycles in the Adams spectral sequence to actual homotopy classes. The mechanism: for an element x ∈ ExtAs,t that is a permanent cycle, one constructs a finite spectrum X with a vn-self map such that the Adams–Novikov filtration of the resulting element in π*S matches the Adams filtration of x. The Mahowald invariant of the h0 tower gives the image of J; the invariant of the h1 tower gives the α-family; the invariant of the h2 tower gives the β-family. Chromatically, the Mahowald invariant is explained by the chromatic spectral sequence and the telescope conjecture: the vn-periodic elements detected by M are precisely those that survive the K(n)-localization and are in the image of the Hurewicz map π*S → π*LK(n)S. The Mahowald invariant is not defined for all elements; it requires the existence of a finite type-n complex with a vn-self map of the correct degree. The Smith–Toda complexes V(n) provide the targets. For n=1, V(1) is the mod-2 Moore spectrum; for n=2, V(2) is a 4-cell complex built by Smith and Toda. The Mahowald invariant of an element x is the element M(x) ∈ π*S such that vnk M(x) corresponds to x under the chromatic convergence isomorphism. The periodicity theorem (Hopkins–Smith) guarantees that the vn-self maps exist on finite type-n spectra, making the invariant well-defined on the vn-periodic part of the Adams E2-term. In exams, students must distinguish the Mahowald invariant from the Adams–Novikov differential detection: the invariant produces an element in the stable stems, while the ANSS differential computes the E∞-term. The Mahowald invariant of h0k is the k-th element of the image of J; the invariant of h2 is the β-element in the 6-stem; the invariant of h2h4 is the β1 element in the 30-stem.

Worked Example: Computing the Mahowald Invariant of h2 at p=2
Show that the Mahowald invariant M(h2) ∈ π6S is the β-element, and explain why it is v2-periodic.
The element h2 ∈ ExtA1,4(𝔽2, 𝔽2) is a permanent cycle in the Adams spectral sequence (no differentials can hit it for degree reasons). Its Adams filtration is 1, stem degree 3 (t−s = 4−1 = 3). Wait: h2 has bidegree (s=1, t=4) so stem = 3. But the β-element is in π6S. The Mahowald invariant raises the stem by 3: M(h2) ∈ π6S. Construction: The Smith–Toda complex V(2) has cells in dimensions 0, 6, 14, 20. It admits a v2-self map v2 : Σ6V(2) → V(2) of degree 6 (since |v2| = 2p2−2 = 6 at p=2). The cofiber of v2 gives a map S6 → V(2) whose composition with the inclusion of the bottom cell S0 → V(2) yields an element in π6S0. This element is M(h2). It is detected by K(2) because v2 is a K(2)-equivalence. The v2-periodicity means that multiplication by v2k on V(2) corresponds to multiplication by the periodicity generator in π*LK(2)S. Hence M(h2) is v2-periodic and represents the β-family generator.
Exam Strategy: Mahowald Invariant and Chromatic Height
When asked to identify the chromatic height of a Mahowald invariant M(x), check the Adams filtration and the stem degree of x. If x is in the h0-tower → height 1 (image of J). If x involves h2 or h2h4 → height 2 (β-family). If x involves h3 or higher → height ≥3 (γ-family). Remember: the Mahowald invariant is defined only when a finite type-n complex with vn-self map exists. Cite the Smith–Toda complex V(n) and the degree of vn (2pn−2).

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Picard Group via Continuous Cohomology of the Morava Stabilizer Group

The K(n)-local Picard group PicK(n) classifies invertible objects in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n). By the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem, SpK(n) is equivalent to the category of modules over the E∞ ring spectrum En with continuous action of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n = Sn ⋊ Gal(𝔽pn/𝔽p), where Sn is the Morava stabilizer group of the Honda formal group law of height n. The Picard group fits into an exact sequence 0 → PicK(n)0 → PicK(n) → ℤ → 0 where the map to ℤ is the suspension (degree). The exotic part PicK(n)0 consists of invertible spectra with trivial determinant. The Hopkins–Mahowald–Sadofsky computation (for n=2, p=2) and the general Hovey–Sadofsky / Gross–Hopkins theory identify PicK(n)0 with the continuous cohomology group H1cts(𝔾n; (En)0×). More precisely, the K(n)-local Picard group is isomorphic to the group of continuous crossed homomorphisms 𝔾n → (En)0× modulo principal ones. The determinant map det : PicK(n) → H1cts(𝔾n; ℤp×) captures the exotic part. For n=1, PicK(1) ≅ ℤ × ℤp (no exotic part at odd primes; at p=2 there is a ℤ/2 exotic summand from the real K-theory KO). For n=2, p=2, PicK(2) ≅ ℤ × ℤ22 × (ℤ/2)2 (Hopkins–Mahowald–Sadofsky). The exotic elements are detected by the Gross–Hopkins duality: the Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere is IK(n) ≃ Σ−n2 Enh𝔾n, and the Picard group acts on it. The continuous cohomology computation uses the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence for 1 → Sn → 𝔾n → Gal → 1. The E2-term is Hs(Gal; Htcts(Sn; (En)0×)). The Morava stabilizer group Sn is a p-adic Lie group of dimension n2, and its cohomology is computed via the Lazard correspondence with its Lie algebra. The exotic Picard elements correspond to non-trivial characters of 𝔾n that do not come from the determinant of the adjoint representation. In the K(n)-local category, the smashing localizations are classified by the Bousfield lattice, and the exotic Picard elements give rise to exotic smashing localizations that are counterexamples to the telescope conjecture (via the chromatic fracture square and the residual Rn). For exams, the key computation is: PicK(n) ≅ ℤ × H1cts(𝔾n; (En)0×), and the exotic part is finite for all n (a theorem of Hovey–Sadofsky and Gross–Hopkins).

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Goodwillie Calculus and the Taylor Tower of the Identity Functor

Goodwillie calculus provides a chromatic filtration of the identity functor on spaces or spectra via the Taylor tower PnId → Pn−1Id → … → P0Id. The n-th layer DnId = hofib(PnId → Pn−1Id) is a homogeneous functor of degree n, classified by a spectrum with Σn-action (the n-th derivative ∂nId). Chromatically, the K(m)-localization of the Taylor tower yields a filtration where the n-th layer is K(m)-local for n ≤ m and K(m)-acyclic for n > m. This is the chromatic interpretation of Goodwillie calculus due to Arone–Mahowald and Kuhn. Specifically, for a K(m)-local spectrum X, the Goodwillie tower of the identity on SpK(m) converges and its layers are K(m)-local. The n-th derivative ∂nId is a K(m)-local spectrum with Σn-action, and its homotopy groups are related to the Morava stabilizer group cohomology. The chromatic height of ∂nId is exactly the p-adic valuation of n plus 1? No: the K(m)-localization of DnId is non-trivial iff n is a power of p times a number coprime to p? Actually, the Arone–Mahowald theorem states that the K(m)-localization of the n-th layer of the Goodwillie tower of the identity on spaces is non-zero iff n = pk for some k ≤ m. For spectra, the n-th derivative of the identity ∂nId is the Spanier–Whitehead dual of the partition complex |Πn| (the poset of partitions of an n-element set). The K(m)-local homotopy type of ∂nId is computed by Kuhn using the Morava K-theory of the partition complex. The result: K(m)*(∂nId) ≠ 0 iff n = pk for k ≤ m. Moreover, the K(m)-local layer DpkId is equivalent to the K(m)-localization of the T(k)-local sphere? Not exactly. The chromatic fracture for the Goodwillie tower gives a spectral sequence E1s,t = πtLK(m)DsId(X) ⇒ πtLK(m)X. This spectral sequence is the chromatic spectral sequence for the K(m)-local sphere when X = S. The Goodwillie derivatives ∂nId are related to the Lie operad and the Morava stabilizer group via the Kuhn–Morava theorem: the K(n)-localization of the n-th derivative of the identity on En-modules recovers the adjoint representation of 𝔾n. For examiners, the key points are: (1) The Goodwillie tower provides a chromatic filtration of the identity functor. (2) The n-th layer is K(m)-local iff n is a power of p up to pm. (3) The derivatives ∂pkId are K(k)-local spectra with Σpk-action. (4) The chromatic convergence theorem for the Goodwillie tower is equivalent to the chromatic convergence theorem for the chromatic tower.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the Bousfield Lattice and the Classification of Smashing Localizations Beyond the Telescope Conjecture

The Bousfield lattice BL of the stable homotopy category is the partially ordered set of Bousfield classes ⟨E⟩ = {X | E ∧ X ≃ *} ordered by ⟨E⟩ ≤ ⟨F⟩ ⇔ ⟨E⟩ ⊆ ⟨F⟩. The chromatic filtration gives a chain ⟨S⟩ = ⟨K(0)⟩ > ⟨K(1)⟩ > ⟨K(2)⟩ > … > ⟨K(n)⟩ > … > ⟨0⟩. The telescope conjecture asserts that the smashing localization LT(n) (localization with respect to a vn-self map on a finite type-n complex) equals LK(n). The failure of the telescope conjecture for n ≥ 2 (at many primes) implies that the Bousfield lattice contains smashing localizations strictly between LT(n) and LK(n). The classification of smashing localizations in the K(n)-local category is a theorem of Barthel–Heard–Valenzuela (building on Hovey–Strickland): every smashing localization of SpK(n) is of the form LE for some E ∈ SpK(n), and corresponds to a thick tensor-ideal in the compact objects SpK(n)ω. The Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)ω is homeomorphic to the Zariski spectrum of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n acting on Spec(π0En). The smashing localizations of the global stable homotopy category are more complicated. The chromatic fracture square shows that a smashing localization L is determined by its K(n)-localizations LK(n)L and its rationalization. The exotic smashing localizations constructed from exotic Picard elements (e.g., LEOn from higher real K-theory) sit strictly between LT(n) and LK(n). The Bousfield class of the telescope T(n) is ⟨T(n)⟩ = ⟨K(0)⟩ ∨ … ∨ ⟨K(n)⟩? No: ⟨T(n)⟩ = ⟨K(0)⟩ ∨ … ∨ ⟨K(n)⟩ is false; T(n) is K(n)-local but not K(m)-local for m < n. Actually LT(n) is the smashing localization generated by T(n), and ⟨T(n)⟩ = ⟨K(n)⟩ if the telescope conjecture holds. When it fails, ⟨T(n)⟩ < ⟨K(n)⟩. The residual Rn = LK(n)S / LT(n)S (in the K(n)-local category) is a non-zero K(n)-local spectrum that is T(n)-acyclic. The Bousfield lattice of the K(n)-local category is isomorphic to the lattice of closed subsets of the Balmer spectrum Spc(SpK(n)ω), which is a Noetherian topological space of dimension n2. The smashing localizations correspond to Thomason subsets (unions of closed subsets with quasi-compact complement). For exams, students must know: (1) The telescope conjecture is equivalent to ⟨T(n)⟩ = ⟨K(n)⟩. (2) The failure implies ⟨T(n)⟩ < ⟨K(n)⟩ and the existence of exotic smashing localizations. (3) The Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is classified by the Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)ω. (4) The chromatic fracture square reduces the global classification to the K(n)-local classification plus rational data.

  • Confusing the Mahowald invariant with the Adams–Novikov differential; the invariant produces a stable stem element, the differential computes the E∞-page.
  • Assuming the string orientation MString → tmf is an equivalence after K(2)-localization; it is not — the K(2)-localization of tmf is E2h𝔾2, while MString is not K(2)-local.
  • Treating the exotic Picard group PicK(n)0 as always trivial; it is non-trivial for n≥2 and detected by H1cts(𝔾n; (En)0×).
  • Believing the Goodwillie tower of the identity converges on all spectra without K(n)-localization; it converges only on K(n)-local spectra or after K(n)-localization.
  • Stating that the Bousfield class of T(n) equals the join of ⟨K(0)⟩ through ⟨K(n)⟩; T(n) is K(n)-local and K(m)-acyclic for m<n.</li>
  • Neglecting the role of the residual Rn in the chromatic fracture square; Rn is the fiber of LK(n)S → LT(n)S and is non-zero exactly when the telescope conjecture fails.
  • Confusing the chromatic height of ∂nId with n; the K(m)-localization of DnId is non-zero only when n is a power of p up to pm.

The Extended Morava Stabilizer Group and the Galois Action on Morava E-Theory

The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n = Aut(Γn) of the height n Honda formal group law Γn over Fpn acts on the Lubin–Tate deformation space and hence on the Morava E-theory spectrum En. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem lifts this action to an E∞-action, but the full automorphism group of the pair (En, Γn) is the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n ⋊ Gal(Fpn/Fp). The Galois group Gal(Fpn/Fp) ≅ ℤ/n acts on the coefficient ring π0En ≅ W(Fpn)[[u1, …, un-1]] by the Witt vector Frobenius on W(Fpn) and trivially on the deformation parameters ui. On the periodicity generator u ∈ π-2En, the Frobenius acts by u ↦ up (up to a unit). This semi-direct product structure is essential for the E∞-structure: the Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory computes obstructions in the continuous cohomology groups Hscts(𝔾n ⋊ Gal; πtEn), and the Galois action resolves the descent from En to the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S. The homotopy fixed point spectrum Enh𝔾n is equivalent to LK(n)S, while Enh(𝔾n ⋊ Gal) recovers the p-complete sphere Sp∧ after rationalization. For examiners, the key distinction is that 𝔾n is a profinite group of dimension n2, while the extended group adds a finite cyclic factor of order n. The continuous cohomology H*cts(𝔾n ⋊ Gal; (En)*) computes the E2-term of the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the extended action, which converges to π*LK(n)S after inverting the periodicity element. The Galois action is also crucial for the construction of higher real K-theories EOn as homotopy fixed points of finite subgroups of the extended group.

The Chromatic Fracture Cube and Iterated Localizations

The chromatic fracture square relates a spectrum X to its K(n)-localization LK(n)X, its E(n)-localization LE(n)X, and the localization LE(n-1)LK(n)X at the previous height. This square generalizes to a chromatic fracture cube when three or more consecutive heights are considered simultaneously. For heights n-1, n, n+1, the cube has vertices corresponding to the eight possible combinations of localizations: X, LK(n+1)X, LE(n)X, LE(n-1)LK(n+1)X, LK(n)X, LE(n-1)LK(n)X, LK(n-1)X, and LE(n-2)LK(n-1)X. Each face of the cube is a homotopy pullback square, and the entire cube expresses the fact that the chromatic tower X → … → LE(n)X → LE(n-1)X → … is a limit diagram in the K(n)-local category. The iterated fracture squares are used to compute the K(n)-local sphere by induction on n: the fiber of LE(n)S → LE(n-1)S is the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S, and the fracture square for LK(n)S involves LE(n-1)LK(n)S, which is the E(n-1)-localization of the K(n)-local sphere. This object is computed via the K(n)-local Adams–Novikov spectral sequence with E(n-1)-coefficients. The cube structure also appears in the study of the telescope conjecture: the failure of the telescope conjecture at height n implies that the map LT(n)S → LK(n)S is not an equivalence, and the residual Rn = fib(LK(n)S → LT(n)S) fits into a compatible system of fracture cubes across heights. For exams, students must be able to draw the fracture square for a given height, identify the four corners, and explain how the cube encodes the compatibility of the chromatic tower. The key formula is the homotopy pullback LE(n)X ≃ LK(n)X ×LE(n-1)LK(n)X LE(n-1)X.

Worked Example: Fracture Square for the Sphere at Height 1
Describe the chromatic fracture square for the p-complete sphere Sp∧ at height n = 1. Identify all four corners and the maps between them.
At height 1, E(1) is p-complete complex K-theory KUp∧ and K(1) is the first Morava K-theory. The fracture square is: Sp∧ → LK(1)Sp∧ ↓ ↓ LKUSp∧ → LKULK(1)Sp∧ Here LKUSp∧ ≃ KUp∧ (the KU-local sphere is KU itself), and LK(1)Sp∧ is the K(1)-local sphere, which is the fiber of the Adams operation ψq − 1 on KUp∧ for a topological generator q of ℤp×. The lower right corner LKULK(1)Sp∧ is the KU-localization of the K(1)-local sphere, which is KUp∧ again because LK(1)Sp∧ is already KU-local. The square expresses the fact that the p-complete sphere is the homotopy pullback of the K(1)-local sphere and KU over KU.
Exam Strategy: Chromatic Fracture Squares
Memorize the fracture square for a general height n and the specific identifications at heights 1 and 2. At height 1, E(1) = KUp∧ and K(1) is the p-completion of the K-theory of Fp. At height 2, E(2) relates to elliptic cohomology and K(2) to the supersingular elliptic curve. The fracture square is a homotopy pullback — always verify that the maps are the correct localization maps. For the cube, remember that each face is a fracture square for a different pair of heights. The iterated fracture squares are the computational engine for the chromatic spectral sequence.

The K(n)-Local Brauer Group and Azumaya Algebras

The Brauer group Br(C) of a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category C classifies Azumaya algebras up to Morita equivalence. For the K(n)-local category SpK(n), the Brauer group fits into an exact sequence relating it to the Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) and the cohomological Brauer group. The Picard group of SpK(n) is known to be ℤ × H1cts(𝔾n; (En)0×) × H2cts(𝔾n; (En)-1×) (with the ℤ factor generated by the suspension). The Brauer group Br(SpK(n)) was computed by Antieau–Mathew and others: it is isomorphic to the torsion subgroup of H2cts(𝔾n; (En)0×) ⊕ H1cts(𝔾n; (En)-1×) ⊕ H3cts(𝔾n; ℤp). For n = 1, Br(SpK(1)) ≅ ℚp/ℤp, generated by the K(1)-local analogues of the classical p-adic division algebras. For n = 2, the Brauer group is related to the Brauer group of the Lubin–Tate stack and has been computed at p = 2, 3. The Azumaya algebras in SpK(n) are E∞-ring spectra A such that A ∧ Aop ≃ End(A) ≃ LK(n)S ⊗ M for some matrix algebra M. The Morava E-theory En itself is an Azumaya algebra over the K(n)-local sphere in the K(n)-local category, and its Brauer class generates a subgroup of Br(SpK(n)). The relationship between the Brauer group and the Picard group is given by the exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → Brcoh(SpK(n)) → 0, where the cohomological Brauer group Brcoh is the torsion in H2cts(𝔾n; (En)0×). For examiners, the key points are: (1) The Brauer group classifies invertible E∞-algebras up to Morita equivalence, not just invertible modules. (2) It is computed via continuous group cohomology of 𝔾n with coefficients in the units of En. (3) The Brauer group is generally larger than the Picard group and detects higher chromatic phenomena. (4) At height 1, it recovers the classical p-adic Brauer group ℚp/ℤp.

The Chromatic Spectral Sequence for Morava E-Theory

The chromatic spectral sequence (CSS) is usually introduced as a spectral sequence converging to the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere, with E1-term given by the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group. However, there is also a chromatic spectral sequence for Morava E-theory itself, which computes the homotopy groups π*En from the cohomology of the extended Morava stabilizer group. The E2-term of this CSS is H*cts(𝔾n ⋊ Gal; (En)*), and it converges to π*Enh(𝔾n ⋊ Gal) ≅ π*LK(n)S after periodicity. But the CSS for En as a spectrum in its own right has E1-term given by the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower: E1s,t = Ht(ℳLT(s); ω⊗*), where ℳLT(s) is the s-th level of the Lubin–Tate tower (the moduli of deformations with Drinfeld level-ps structure) and ω is the line bundle of invariant differentials. This spectral sequence is a special case of the descent spectral sequence for the sheaf Otop on the Lubin–Tate stack. The differentials in this CSS are related to the action of the Hecke operators on the Lubin–Tate tower. For n = 1, the Lubin–Tate tower is the tower of modular curves for Γ(ps), and the CSS for E1 = KUp∧ collapses at E2 because KU is even periodic. For n = 2, the CSS for E2 is related to the cohomology of the moduli stack of supersingular elliptic curves with level structure, and the differentials are controlled by the action of the Hecke algebra. The CSS for En is also the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for En itself, which computes π*En from the Hopf algebroid (En)*(En). The Morava change-of-rings theorem identifies this with the continuous cohomology of 𝔾n. For exams, students must distinguish between: (1) The CSS for the sphere (computing ANSS E2). (2) The CSS for En (computing π*En via Lubin–Tate tower cohomology). (3) The homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for 𝔾n acting on En (computing π*LK(n)S). These three spectral sequences have the same E2-term but different convergence targets and differentials.

The Morava E-Theory of Classifying Spaces and Generalized Characters

The computation of En*(BG) for a finite group G is a central problem in chromatic homotopy theory, generalizing the classical character theory of finite groups (where KU*(BG) recovers the complex representation ring). The transchromatic character map of Hopkins–Kuhn–Ravenel provides an isomorphism En0(BG) ⊗En0 En0[u±1] ≅ Mapcts(Hom(ℤpn, G)/G, En0[u±1]) for p-groups, and a more general formula for arbitrary finite groups involving the fusion system. Here Hom(ℤpn, G) is the set of continuous homomorphisms from the p-adic rank-n free abelian group to G, modulo conjugation. The target is the ring of continuous functions on this conjugacy variety with values in the periodic En-cohomology of a point. This map is an isomorphism after completing at the augmentation ideal of the representation ring. For general finite groups, the character map involves the p-subgroups of G and their fusion data. The Morava E-theory of BG is also computed by the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of the Morava stabilizer group on En*(BG) ≅ (En)* ⊗ R(G)(p) (for n = 1) or its higher analogues. The E2-term is H*cts(𝔾n; En*(BG)), and the differentials are related to the power operations in En-theory. The generalized character theory has applications to the computation of the K(n)-local sphere: the K(n)-localization of the suspension spectrum of BG is a wedge of copies of LK(n)S indexed by the conjugacy classes of homomorphisms ℤpn → G. This is the Kuhn–Morava theorem for classifying spaces. For examiners, the key formulas are: (1) The character map for p-groups: En0(BG)∧ ≅ Mapcts(Hom(ℤpn, G)/G, En0). (2) The relationship to the Lubin–Tate stack: En0(BG) is the ring of functions on the scheme Hom(ℤpn, G)/G over the Lubin–Tate deformation space. (3) The K(n)-localization of Σ∞BG+ splits as a wedge of LK(n)S indexed by the conjugacy classes of p-subgroups of rank ≤ n.

  • Confusing the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n ⋊ Gal with 𝔾n alone; the Galois action is essential for the E∞-structure and for descent to the sphere.
  • Treating the chromatic fracture cube as merely three fracture squares; the cube encodes higher compatibility data (the tetrahedron of fibers) that is lost in pairwise squares.
  • Assuming the Brauer group of SpK(n) equals the Picard group; the Brauer group classifies Azumaya algebras up to Morita equivalence and is generally larger, with a non-trivial cohomological Brauer group.
  • Conflating the three chromatic spectral sequences: the CSS for the sphere (ANSS E2), the CSS for En (Lubin–Tate tower cohomology), and the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (computing π*LK(n)S). They share an E2-term but have different differentials and targets.
  • Believing the transchromatic character map is an isomorphism on the nose for all finite groups; it is an isomorphism after completion at the augmentation ideal and for p-groups, but for general groups it involves fusion data and is not a simple function ring.
  • Neglecting the role of Drinfeld level structures in the Lubin–Tate tower; the levels ℳLT(s) with Γ(ps)-structure are the correct covers for the CSS of En, not just the height filtration.
  • Stating that En*(BG) is always a free (En)*-module; it is free for p-groups after completion but can have torsion for groups with non-trivial fusion.

The Lubin–Tate Tower, Drinfeld’s Upper Half-Space, and the Local Langlands Correspondence

The Lubin–Tate tower is not merely a tool for computing Morava E-theory; it is the p-adic uniformization of Drinfeld’s upper half-space Ωⁿ = ℙⁿ⁻¹(ℂₚ) ∪K/ℚₚ, [K:ℚₚ]=n ℙⁿ⁻¹(K), the p-adic symmetric domain for GLₙ(ℚₚ). Drinfeld proved that the inverse limit of the Lubin–Tate tower ℳ_LT = lim_← ℳ_LT(s) (with full level p^s structure) is isomorphic to Ωⁿ ×ℚₚ ℚₚ^ab as a rigid analytic space over ℚₚ^ab, equipped with an action of GLₙ(ℚₚ) × D^× × Wℚₚ (where D is the central division algebra of invariant 1/n and Wℚₚ is the Weil group). The cohomology of this tower realizes the local Langlands correspondence for GLₙ(ℚₚ) and the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for D^×. Specifically, for an irreducible smooth representation π of GLₙ(ℚₚ), the π-isotypic component of H^*_c(Ωⁿ, ℚ_ℓ) (ℓ ≠ p) is non-zero exactly when π corresponds to an n-dimensional representation of Wℚₚ under the local Langlands correspondence. In chromatic homotopy theory, the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾ₙ = Aut(Γₙ) ⋊ Gal(𝔽pⁿ/𝔽ₚ) sits inside GLₙ(ℚₚ) × D^× via the Dieudonné module of the Honda formal group law. The Lubin–Tate tower with Drinfeld level structure provides a geometric model for the classifying space B𝔾ₙ, and the continuous cohomology H^*_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)*) computes the homotopy groups of the K(n)-local sphere via the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence. The connection to the local Langlands correspondence explains the appearance of supercuspidal representations in the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower and, by transchromatic descent, in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. For examiners, the key points are: (1) The Lubin–Tate tower uniformizes Drinfeld’s upper half-space Ωⁿ. (2) The action of GLₙ(ℚₚ) × D^× × Wℚₚ on the tower’s cohomology realizes the local Langlands and Jacquet–Langlands correspondences. (3) The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾ₙ embeds into this product, linking chromatic homotopy theory to the p-adic Langlands program. (4) The K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S is built from the homotopy fixed points of 𝔾ₙ acting on Eₙ, and its homotopy groups are computed by the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower.

The Morava E-Theory of Eilenberg–MacLane Spaces and the Ravenel–Wilson Hopf Ring

The computation of Eₙ*(K(ℤ/p, m)) for m ≥ 1 is a foundational result that reveals the algebraic structure of power operations in Morava E-theory. Ravenel and Wilson (1980) computed K(n)*(K(ℤ/p, m)) and showed it is a free K(n)*-module on generators corresponding to the Dyer–Lashof operations Q^I acting on the fundamental class. For Morava E-theory, the answer is more subtle: Eₙ⁰(K(ℤ/p, m)) is a completed tensor product of a polynomial algebra on certain power operations with the ring of functions on the scheme of p-torsion points of the universal deformation Γₙ. Specifically, let 𝔾ₙ[m] = Hom(ℤ/p^m, Γₙ) be the p^m-torsion subgroup scheme of the universal deformation over the Lubin–Tate ring Eₙ⁰. Then Eₙ⁰(K(ℤ/p, m)) ≅ Map_cts(𝔾ₙ[m](ℚₚ/ℤₚ), Eₙ⁰) as Eₙ⁰-algebras, where the right side is the ring of continuous functions on the p^m-torsion points of the formal group. The Hopf ring structure (a ring object in the category of coalgebras) encodes the Dyer–Lashof operations and the Kudo transgression. The Ravenel–Wilson Hopf ring for K(n)*(K(ℤ/p, *)) has generators e_i (dual to the Milnor basis of the Steenrod algebra) and the Dyer–Lashof operations Q^I. For Eₙ-theory, the Hopf ring is a deformation of this, with the same additive structure but a multiplication twisted by the formal group law. The power operations in Eₙ-theory are constructed via the action of the extended power construction on Eₙ, and they satisfy the Cartan formula, Adem relations, and the Nishida relations with the Steenrod algebra (at height 1). At higher heights, the power operations are governed by the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin–Tate tower. The computation of Eₙ*(K(ℤ/p, m)) is essential for the transchromatic character map, the construction of the K(n)-local sphere as a homotopy fixed point, and the analysis of the chromatic spectral sequence. For exams, students must know: (1) Eₙ⁰(K(ℤ/p, m)) is the ring of functions on the p^m-torsion of the universal formal group. (2) The Hopf ring structure encodes power operations. (3) The Ravenel–Wilson computation for K(n) is the associated graded of the Eₙ computation. (4) The power operations in Eₙ-theory are continuous with respect to the profinite topology on 𝔾ₙ.

Worked Example: Computing E₂⁰(K(ℤ/p, 1)) at p=2
Let p=2, n=2. The Honda formal group law Γ₂ over 𝔽₄ has height 2. Its universal deformation has Lubin–Tate ring E₂⁰ = W(𝔽₄)[[u₁]]. The 2-torsion subgroup scheme Γ₂[2] is a finite flat group scheme of rank 4 over E₂⁰. Compute E₂⁰(K(ℤ/2, 1)) as a completed E₂⁰-algebra and identify the power operation Q¹.
K(ℤ/2, 1) = ℝP^∞. The 2-torsion of the universal deformation Γ₂ is the kernel of the 2-series [2]_Γ₂(x) = u₁ x² + x⁴ (since p=2, the 2-series has no linear term). Over E₂⁰, [2]_Γ₂(x) = x⁴ + u₁ x². The scheme Γ₂[2] = Spec E₂⁰[x]/([2]_Γ₂(x)) is a finite flat scheme of rank 4. Its ℚ₂/ℤ₂-points (in the sense of the transchromatic character map) correspond to the 2-torsion points of the formal group over algebraically closed fields. The ring of continuous functions on Γ₂[2](ℚ₂/ℤ₂) is the completion of E₂⁰[x]/([2]_Γ₂(x)) at the augmentation ideal. Thus E₂⁰(K(ℤ/2, 1)) ≅ E₂⁰[[x]]/([2]_Γ₂(x))^∧. The power operation Q¹ on E₂⁰(ℝP^∞) corresponds to the map induced by the transfer ℝP^∞ → ℝP^∞ ∧ ℝP^∞ → ℝP^∞. In terms of the formal group, Q¹(x) = [2]_Γ₂(x)/x² = x² + u₁. This matches the formula for the power operation in Morava E-theory: Q¹(x) = xpⁿ + u₁ xpⁿ⁻¹ + ... for the coordinate x on the formal group. The Hopf ring structure has coproduct Δ(x) = x ⊗ 1 + 1 ⊗ x (since K(ℤ/2, 1) is an H-space) and the product is the ring multiplication in E₂⁰[[x]]/([2]_Γ₂(x)).
Exam Strategy: E-Theory of Classifying Spaces
When asked for Eₙ*(BG), first determine if G is a p-group. For p-groups, the Hopkins–Kuhn–Ravenel character map gives an isomorphism after completion: Eₙ⁰(BG)^∧ ≅ Map_cts(Hom(ℤₚⁿ, G)/G, Eₙ⁰). For Eilenberg–MacLane spaces K(ℤ/p, m), use the Ravenel–Wilson description: Eₙ⁰(K(ℤ/p, m)) is the ring of functions on the p^m-torsion of the universal formal group. Remember that the power operations Q^i act on the coordinate x of the formal group by Q¹(x) = [p]_Γₙ(x)/x^p. Do not confuse the Hopf ring structure (two products: * from the ring structure of Eₙ, ∘ from the H-space structure of K(ℤ/p, m)) with the ordinary ring structure. The Dyer–Lashof operations live in the ∘-product.

The Algebraic Chromatic Spectral Sequence and the Cohomology of the Moduli Stack of Formal Groups

The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence (ACSS) is the spectral sequence that computes the E₂-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS) for the sphere, i.e., ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*), from the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer groups. It arises from the chromatic filtration of the moduli stack of formal groups M_fg. The stack M_fg has a stratification by height: M_fg = ∪n≥0 M_fg≥n, where M_fg≥n is the closed substack of formal groups of height ≥ n. The quotient M_fg≥n/M_fg≥n+1 is the classifying stack B𝔾ₙ of the Morava stabilizer group. The sheaf of invariant differentials ω on M_fg restricts to a sheaf on each stratum, and the cohomology H^*(M_fg, ω^*) is the E₂-term of the ANSS. The ACSS has E₁-page E₁s,t = Ht-s(M_fg≥s/M_fg≥s+1, ω^*) = Ht-s_cts(𝔾ₛ; (Eₛ)*) and converges to H^*(M_fg, ω^*) = ExtMU*MU(MU*, MU*). The differentials in the ACSS are induced by the boundary maps in the long exact sequences of the pairs (M_fg≥s, M_fg≥s+1). This spectral sequence was introduced by Miller, Ravenel, and Wilson (1977) and is the algebraic avatar of the geometric chromatic spectral sequence for the sphere. The E₁-page is the direct sum over heights of the continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer groups with coefficients in the Morava E-theory coefficient rings. The ACSS collapses at E₂ for p ≥ 5 and n ≤ 2, but has non-trivial differentials at higher heights and for p=2,3. The most famous differential is the d₁ differential from the height 1 line to the height 2 line, which detects the image of J. The ACSS is also the E₂-page of the descent spectral sequence for the chromatic tower of the sphere. For examiners, the key distinctions are: (1) The ACSS computes the ANSS E₂-term (algebraic). (2) The geometric chromatic spectral sequence computes the homotopy groups of the chromatic tower (topological). (3) The homotopy fixed point spectral sequence computes π_*LK(n)S. All three have E₂-terms involving H^*_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)*) but different differentials and convergence targets. The ACSS is the algebraic input to the geometric chromatic spectral sequence via the Morava change-of-rings theorem.

The K(n)-Local Picard Group, Gross–Hopkins Duality, and the Determinant Map

The Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) of invertible objects in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category is a central invariant. By the work of Hopkins, Mahowald, Sadofsky, and others, Pic(SpK(n)) fits into a short exact sequence 0 → ℤₚ → Pic(SpK(n)) → H¹_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰) → 0, where the ℤₚ factor corresponds to suspensions of the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S, and the cohomology group classifies exotic invertible objects (those not equivalent to a suspension of the sphere). The Gross–Hopkins duality theorem identifies the Spanier–Whitehead dual of the K(n)-local sphere: IK(n)LK(n)S ≃ Σ-n²LK(n)S, where IK(n) is the Gross–Hopkins dualizing spectrum. This duality implies that the Picard group is self-dual under the pairing (X, Y) ↦ π₀LK(n)(X ∧ Y). The determinant map det: Pic(SpK(n)) → H¹_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰) sends an invertible K(n)-local spectrum X to the class of the 𝔾ₙ-equivariant line bundle Eₙ⁰(X) over the Lubin–Tate deformation space. The kernel of det is the subgroup of invertible objects that become trivial after smashing with Eₙ, which is precisely the suspensions ℤₚ. The image of det is the subgroup of H¹_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰) consisting of classes that are algebraic (i.e., come from the determinant of a representation of 𝔾ₙ). For n=1, Pic(SpK(1)) ≅ ℤₚ × ℤ/2 (the ℤ/2 is the sign representation of Gal(ℚₚ^ab/ℚₚ)). For n=2, the exotic Picard group is more complicated and related to the cohomology of the moduli stack of supersingular elliptic curves. The Gross–Hopkins dualizing spectrum IK(n) is itself an invertible object, corresponding to the determinant representation of 𝔾ₙ on the Lie algebra of the universal formal group. The duality pairing on the Picard group is given by the cup product in continuous cohomology followed by the trace map H²_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰) → ℤₚ induced by the Gross–Hopkins isomorphism. For exams, students must know: (1) The exact sequence for Pic(SpK(n)). (2) The Gross–Hopkins duality IK(n)S ≃ Σ-n²S. (3) The determinant map and its kernel/image. (4) The duality pairing on the Picard group. (5) The distinction between the Picard group (invertible objects up to equivalence) and the Brauer group (Azumaya algebras up to Morita equivalence), which is larger and fits into an exact sequence involving H²_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰×).

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category as a Module Category and the Classification of Localizing Subcategories

The K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category. A fundamental theorem of Barthel, Heard, and Valenzuela (building on work of Hovey, Strickland, and others) states that SpK(n) is equivalent to the ∞-category of modules over the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S in the ∞-category of Eₙ-module spectra with a compatible 𝔾ₙ-action. More concretely, the K(n)-local category is the category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid (Eₙ)₀(Eₙ) in the derived sense, or equivalently, the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the Lubin–Tate stack with a 𝔾ₙ-equivariant structure. The classification of localizing subcategories (closed under coproducts, not just finite colimits) of SpK(n) is a major open problem. The telescope conjecture for K(n)-local categories asserts that every smashing localization (localization functor that preserves coproducts and is given by smashing with a localizing object) is generated by a compact object. This is equivalent to the statement that the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is generated by the thick subcategories of compact objects. The thick subcategories of compact objects in SpK(n) are classified by the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins–Smith: they correspond to the heights 0 ≤ m ≤ n, with the m-th thick subcategory consisting of K(m)-acyclic compact spectra. However, there may be non-smashing localizing subcategories. The work of Barthel–Heard–Valenzuela shows that the localizing subcategories of SpK(n) that are generated by a single compact object are classified by the same height filtration. The classification of all localizing subcategories is related to the Balmer spectrum of the compact objects, which is the set of prime thick tensor ideals. For SpK(n)^c (the compact K(n)-local spectra), the Balmer spectrum is {0, 1, ..., n} with the Zariski topology, where the prime ideal at height m consists of K(m)-acyclic compact spectra. The tensor-triangular geometry of SpK(n)^c thus recovers the chromatic filtration. The non-compact localizing subcategories are more mysterious; they correspond to subsets of the Balmer spectrum that are not necessarily closed under specialization. The telescope conjecture is equivalent to the statement that every localizing subcategory is generated by the compact objects it contains. Recent counterexamples to the telescope conjecture in the p-local category (by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, Schlank) do not directly apply to the K(n)-local category, where the conjecture remains open for n ≥ 2. For examiners, the key points are: (1) SpK(n) is a module category over LK(n)S. (2) Thick subcategories of compact objects are classified by height. (3) The Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)^c is the chromatic filtration. (4) The telescope conjecture for K(n)-local categories is the statement that all smashing localizations are generated by compact objects. (5) The classification of all localizing subcategories is open and related to the structure of the Bousfield lattice.

  • Confusing the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence (computing ANSS E₂) with the geometric chromatic spectral sequence (computing π_* of the chromatic tower) and the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (computing π_*LK(n)S). They share E₂-terms but have different differentials and targets.
  • Assuming the Lubin–Tate tower is only a formal scheme; it is a rigid analytic space that uniformizes Drinfeld’s upper half-space, and its cohomology realizes the local Langlands correspondence.
  • Treating Eₙ⁰(K(ℤ/p, m)) as a polynomial algebra; it is a completed ring of functions on the p^m-torsion of the universal formal group, with a Hopf ring structure encoding power operations.
  • Neglecting the determinant map in the Picard group exact sequence; the exotic Picard group is the image of det in H¹_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰), not the whole cohomology group.
  • Believing the telescope conjecture is settled in the K(n)-local category; it is open for n ≥ 2, and the recent counterexamples are in the p-local category, not the K(n)-local category.
  • Conflating the Picard group (invertible objects) with the Brauer group (Azumaya algebras); the Brauer group is larger and involves H²_cts(𝔾ₙ; (Eₙ)⁰×).
  • Forgetting that the Gross–Hopkins duality shifts by n², not n; the dualizing spectrum is Σ-n²LK(n)S, and this shift is crucial for the duality pairing on the Picard group.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) admits a symmetric monoidal structure given by the K(n)-local smash product. A fundamental result of Hovey and Strickland identifies SpK(n) with the derived category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid (E_n)0(E_n) in the sense of derived algebraic geometry. More precisely, there is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories between SpK(n) and the ∞-category of modules over the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S in the ∞-category of E_n-module spectra with a compatible action of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n. This equivalence is realized by the functor X ↦ E_n ∧ X equipped with its natural 𝔾_n-action. The compact objects in SpK(n) are precisely the K(n)-localizations of finite spectra, and the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins–Smith classifies the thick tensor ideals of compact objects by the chromatic height filtration: for each 0 ≤ m ≤ n, the m-th thick subcategory consists of those compact spectra that are K(m)-acyclic. The Balmer spectrum of the tensor-triangulated category of compact objects SpK(n)^c is the set {0, 1, …, n} with the Zariski topology, where the prime ideal at height m corresponds to the thick subcategory of K(m)-acyclic compact spectra. This recovers the chromatic filtration purely from the tensor-triangular geometry of the compact objects. The classification of all localizing subcategories (closed under arbitrary coproducts) of SpK(n) remains a major open problem. The telescope conjecture for the K(n)-local category asserts that every smashing localization (a localization functor that preserves coproducts and is given by smashing with a localizing object) is generated by a compact object. This is equivalent to the statement that the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is generated by the thick subcategories of compact objects. Recent counterexamples to the telescope conjecture in the p-local category by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, and Schlank do not directly apply to the K(n)-local category, where the conjecture remains open for n ≥ 2. The work of Barthel, Heard, and Valenzuela shows that localizing subcategories generated by a single compact object are classified by the same height filtration, but the existence of non-smashing localizing subcategories is unknown. The chromatic fracture square provides a local-global principle relating the K(n)-local category to the E(n)-local and K(n-1)-local categories, and its iterated version, the chromatic fracture cube, encodes the full chromatic tower structure.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The Morava E-theory E_n is a complex-oriented, even-periodic, Landweber-exact cohomology theory of height n at a fixed prime p. Its coefficient ring is (E_n)0 ≅ W(𝔽p^n)[[u1, …, un-1]], where W(𝔽p^n) is the ring of Witt vectors, and the formal group law is the universal deformation of the Honda formal group law of height n over 𝔽p^n. The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n = Aut(Γ_n) ⋊ Gal(𝔽p^n/𝔽_p) acts continuously on E_n by E_∞ ring maps, where Γ_n is the Honda formal group law. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem constructs this action at the level of E_∞ ring spectra, making E_n a 𝔾_n-equivariant E_∞ ring spectrum. The homotopy fixed point spectrum E_nh𝔾_n is equivalent to the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S. The continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n with coefficients in the Morava module (E_n)_* computes the E2-term of the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (HFPSS) converging to π_*LK(n)S. The Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the Spanier–Whitehead dual of the K(n)-local sphere as Σ-n2 LK(n)S, inducing a perfect pairing on the K(n)-local Picard group Pic_n. The determinant map det: Pic_n → H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) detects exotic invertible objects, and its image is the exotic Picard group. The K(n)-local Brauer group Br_n classifies Azumaya algebras in SpK(n) and fits into an exact sequence involving H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×). Power operations in Morava E-theory, constructed by Rezk and Ando–Hopkins–Strickland, endow (E_n)0(BΣp^k) with a Hopf ring structure and are essential for the transchromatic character map. The Lubin–Tate tower {MLT(m)} of moduli spaces of p-divisible groups with level structure uniformizes Drinfeld's upper half-space and realizes the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(ℚ_p) in its étale cohomology. The Morava change-of-rings theorem identifies the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for a finite spectrum X with the continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n with coefficients in (E_n)_*(X), providing the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence computes the cohomology of the moduli stack Mfg of formal groups and abuts to the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the sphere.

Worked Example: Computing the K(1)-Local Picard Group at p=2
Compute the K(1)-local Picard group Pic1 at the prime 2 using the determinant map and the structure of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾1.
At height n=1 and p=2, the Morava stabilizer group is 𝔾1 ≅ ℤ2^× ≅ {±1} × (1+4ℤ2) ≅ C2 × ℤ2. The Morava E-theory is E1 ≅ KU2^∧, the 2-complete complex K-theory, with (E1)0 ≅ ℤ2. The determinant map det: Pic1 → H1cts(𝔾1; ℤ2^×) is an isomorphism onto its image. Since ℤ2^× ≅ C2 × ℤ2, we compute H1cts(𝔾1; ℤ2^×) ≅ Homcts(𝔾1, ℤ2^×) ≅ Homcts(C2 × ℤ2, C2 × ℤ2) ≅ (C2)2 × ℤ22. The image of det consists of the exotic Picard group. The full Picard group fits into the exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic1 → H1cts(𝔾1; ℤ2^×) → H2cts(𝔾1; ℤ2^×) → 0, where the first map sends 1 to the suspension ΣLK(1)S. The group H2cts(𝔾1; ℤ2^×) ≅ H2cts(ℤ2; ℤ2^×) ≅ ℤ2^×/(ℤ2^×)2 ≅ C2. The exotic Picard group is the kernel of the boundary map, which can be shown to be isomorphic to ℤ2 × C2. Thus Pic1 ≅ ℤ ⊕ ℤ2 ⊕ C2. The ℤ factor is generated by suspension, the ℤ2 factor by the determinant of the Lubin–Tate character, and the C2 factor by the exotic element detected by the non-trivial class in H1cts(C2; C2).
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Spectral Sequences
The Adams–Novikov spectral sequence (ANSS), the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence (HFPSS) for 𝔾_n acting on E_n, the chromatic spectral sequence (CSS) for the chromatic tower, and the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence (ACSS) for Mfg all share the same E2-term: continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n with coefficients in (E_n)_*. However, their differentials, convergence targets, and geometric origins differ. The ANSS computes π_*(X) for a p-local spectrum X. The HFPSS computes π_*LK(n)S. The CSS computes π_* of the chromatic tower layers. The ACSS computes the cohomology of Mfg. In exams, identify which spectral sequence is being referenced by its target and the nature of its differentials (e.g., the HFPSS has differentials induced by the 𝔾_n-action, while the ANSS differentials come from the cobar complex of the Hopf algebroid (BP_*BP)).

The Smith–Toda complexes V(m) are finite spectra of type m, meaning they are K(m)-local but K(m-1)-acyclic. Their existence for all m ≤ n at a given prime p is a deep theorem of Smith and Toda, constructed using the nilpotence theorem and the periodicity theorem. The V(m) spectra play a crucial role in detecting v_m-periodic families in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The v_m-self map v_m: Σd V(m) → V(m) induces a v_m-periodic family in π_*(S) via the composition S → V(m) → Σ-d V(m) → … . The Mahowald invariant provides a systematic way to relate v1-periodic families (image of J) to v2-periodic families (beta family) and higher. The Greek letter construction in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence produces elements α_i, β_i, γ_i, … corresponding to the v1, v2, v3, … periodic families. The beta family at height 2 is intimately connected to the theory of elliptic curves and the cohomology of the moduli stack Mell. The gamma family at height 3 involves the action of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾3 on E3 and the geometry of the Lubin–Tate tower. The periodicity theorem of Hopkins–Smith states that for any finite spectrum X of type n, there exists a v_n-self map v_n: Σ^d X → X. The nilpotence theorem of Devinatz–Hopkins–Smith asserts that a map of finite spectra is nilpotent if and only if it is K(n)-acyclic for all n. The X(n)-construction of Ravenel provides a geometric proof of the nilpotence theorem by building a ring spectrum X(n) with π_*X(n) = BP_*/I_n[v_n-1] and showing that the unit map S → X(n) is a monomorphism in the stable homotopy category. The telescope conjecture for the p-local category, which asserted that the localization with respect to a v_n-self map of a finite type n spectrum is smashing, was recently disproved by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, and Schlank using the theory of chromatic fracture and the non-existence of certain K(n)-local finite spectra.

Topological modular forms (tmf) is a connective, periodic cohomology theory of chromatic height 2 that interpolates between elliptic cohomology theories. It is constructed as the global sections of a sheaf of E_∞ ring spectra Otop on the compactified moduli stack Mell of elliptic curves. The string orientation of tmf is a map of E_∞ ring spectra MString → tmf, where MString is the Thom spectrum of the string group. This orientation refines the Witten genus and is central to the chromatic approach to the Stolz–Teichner program on elliptic cohomology and supersymmetric field theories. The homotopy groups of tmf are computed by the descent spectral sequence H^s(Mell; ω⊗t) ⇒ π2t-stmf, where ω is the line bundle of invariant differentials on the universal elliptic curve. At the prime 2, the homotopy groups of tmf exhibit a rich structure related to the beta family in the stable homotopy groups of spheres. The K(2)-localization of tmf is closely related to the Morava E-theory E2 at p=2,3. The chromatic splitting conjecture predicts a decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S into a wedge of suspensions of the K(n)-localizations of the monochromatic layers M_n. The chromatic convergence theorem of Ravenel states that the chromatic tower {L_n S} converges to the p-local sphere S_(p) in the sense that the natural map S_(p) → holim_n L_n S is an equivalence. The chromatic fracture square relates L_n S to Ln-1 S and LK(n) S via a pullback square of Bousfield localizations. The Bousfield–Kuhn functor Φ_n provides a chromatic approach to unstable homotopy theory by associating to a space X a K(n)-local spectrum Φ_n(X) that captures the v_n-periodic unstable homotopy groups. The Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor on spaces has a chromatic interpretation: the n-th derivative of the identity is related to the K(n)-local sphere. The algebraic K-theory of spheres exhibits chromatic redshift: K(S) has chromatic height one higher than S, and the cyclotomic trace K(S) → TC(S) detects this redshift. The Lichtenbaum–Quillen conjecture, now a theorem of Voevodsky–Rost–Weibel, relates the algebraic K-theory of rings of integers to étale cohomology, and its chromatic interpretation involves the K(1)-local sphere. The Baum–Connes conjecture for a group G relates the K-theory of the reduced C*-algebra C*_r(G) to the equivariant K-homology of the classifying space for proper actions EG, and its chromatic approach uses the assembly map in K(n)-local homotopy theory. Factorization homology ∫_M A of an E_n-algebra A over a manifold M provides a topological chiral homology theory that is deeply connected to the cobordism hypothesis and the classification of fully dualizable objects in K(n)-local categories.

The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n^ext = Aut(Γ_n) ⋊ Gal(𝔽p^n/𝔽_p) acts on the Lubin–Tate deformation space Spf (E_n)0, and the Galois group Gal(𝔽p^n/𝔽_p) acts on the coefficients (E_n)0 via the Frobenius automorphism. The chromatic fracture cube generalizes the chromatic fracture square to iterated localizations: for any sequence of heights n1 > n2 > … > n_k, there is a k-dimensional cube of Bousfield localizations whose total homotopy limit is the localization with respect to the wedge of the corresponding Morava K-theories. The classification of smashing localizations in the K(n)-local category is equivalent to the telescope conjecture for K(n)-local categories. The Bousfield lattice of the K(n)-local category is the lattice of Bousfield classes of objects in SpK(n). The thick subcategory theorem classifies the thick tensor ideals of compact objects, but the full Bousfield lattice includes non-smashing localizing subcategories. The work of Balmer on tensor-triangular geometry provides a framework for understanding the spectrum of prime tensor ideals. The Picard group Pic_n fits into an exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic_n → H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → Br_n → H3cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×), where the first map is suspension, the second is the determinant, and the third is the boundary map to the Brauer group. The exotic Picard group is the image of the determinant map. The transchromatic character map relates the Morava E-theory of classifying spaces of finite groups to the representation theory of the Morava stabilizer group and provides a bridge between different chromatic heights. The Honda formal group law Γ_n over 𝔽p^n is the unique (up to isomorphism) formal group law of height n, and its endomorphism ring is the maximal order in the division algebra of invariant 1/n over ℚ_p. The Lubin–Tate tower {MLT(m)} is a tower of rigid analytic spaces over ℚ_p that uniformizes Drinfeld's upper half-space Ω^n = ℙn-1 ∪H H, where the union is over all ℚ_p-rational hyperplanes. The étale cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower realizes the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(ℚ_p) and the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence for the division algebra D_n of invariant 1/n.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category as a Symmetric Monoidal Stable ∞-Category

The K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) carries a symmetric monoidal structure given by the K(n)-localized smash product X ∧K(n) Y := LK(n)(X ∧ Y). This makes SpK(n) a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category in the sense of Lurie, and the unit is the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S. The Morava E-theory E_n is an E_∞-ring spectrum in this category, and the Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem provides an action of the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next on E_n by E_∞-ring maps. The category of E_n-modules in SpK(n) is equivalent to the category of modules over the E_∞-ring E_n, and the homotopy fixed points (E_n)hH for closed subgroups H ≤ 𝔾_next yield the Lubin–Tate spectra E_nhH. The symmetric monoidal structure interacts with the chromatic fracture square: for any X ∈ SpK(n), the natural map X → LK(n-1)X ×LK(n-1)LK(n)X LK(n)X is an equivalence, and this square is a pullback in the symmetric monoidal ∞-category. The Picard group Pic_n classifies the invertible objects under ∧K(n), and the determinant map det: Pic_n → H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) is a homomorphism of abelian groups. The Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the dualizing object as I_n := Σ-n2LK(n)S, and the duality pairing Pic_n × Pic_n → ℤ given by (X, Y) ↦ π0(X ∧K(n) Y ∧K(n) I_n) is perfect. The Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is the lattice of Bousfield classes ⟨X⟩ = {Y | X ∧K(n) Y = 0}, and the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins–Smith classifies the thick tensor ideals of compact objects as {0} = C0 ⊂ C1 ⊂ … ⊂ C_n = SpK(n)^ω, where C_k consists of objects of type ≥ k.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of Ambidexterity

Ambidexterity in K(n)-local homotopy theory refers to the equivalence between left and right Kan extensions along certain functors, most notably the norm map N: colimBG X → limBG X for a finite group G acting on a K(n)-local spectrum X. The Hopkins–Lurie theorem on ambidexterity states that for a finite group G and a K(n)-local spectrum X with G-action, the norm map N: XhG → XhG is an equivalence after K(n)-localization if and only if the order of G is prime to p. When p divides |G|, the norm map is not an equivalence, and its fiber and cofiber are controlled by the Tate construction XtG := cofib(N). The Tate construction plays a central role in the chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture and in the theory of topological cyclic homology. For the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n, which is a profinite group, the continuous homotopy fixed points (E_n)hH for closed subgroups H are defined via the homotopy limit over the coset space 𝔾_n/H. The Tate construction for profinite groups is defined as the cofiber of the norm map from homotopy orbits to homotopy fixed points, and the Tate orbit lemma identifies the Tate construction of a induced spectrum with the spectrum itself. The ambidexterity theorem for K(n)-local categories implies that the K(n)-local category is "ambidextrous" for finite groups of order prime to p, and this property is used in the construction of the determinant map on the Picard group and in the proof of the Gross–Hopkins duality. The theory of ambidexterity also underlies the construction of the chromatic fracture cube and the iterated localizations LK(n1) ∨ … ∨ K(n_k).

Worked Example: Norm Map for Cyclic Group of Order p
Let G = C_p be the cyclic group of order p acting trivially on the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S. Compute the norm map N: (LK(n)S)hC_p → (LK(n)S)hC_p and its fiber.
Since the action is trivial, (LK(n)S)hC_p ≃ LK(n)S ∧ BC_p_+ and (LK(n)S)hC_p ≃ F(BC_p_+, LK(n)S). The norm map is the map induced by the transfer BC_p_+ → S0. In K(n)-local homotopy, the Tate construction (LK(n)S)tC_p is the cofiber of N. By the Tate orbit lemma and the fact that K(n)_*(BC_p) is a free K(n)_*-module of rank p, the norm map is not an equivalence. The fiber of N is Σ-1(LK(n)S)tC_p, and the cofiber is (LK(n)S)tC_p. The homotopy groups of the Tate construction are given by the Tate cohomology Ĥ^*(C_p; π_*LK(n)S). Since π_*LK(n)S is concentrated in degrees congruent to 0 mod 2(p^n-1) (by the periodicity theorem), the Tate cohomology is periodic of period 2. The fiber is therefore a non-trivial K(n)-local spectrum, demonstrating that ambidexterity fails for p-groups.
Exam Strategy: Ambidexterity and Tate Construction
Remember: ambidexterity holds for finite groups of order prime to p in K(n)-local categories. For p-groups, the norm map is not an equivalence; its failure is measured by the Tate construction. The Tate orbit lemma (Ind_H^G X)tG ≃ XtH is a key computational tool. In exams, identify whether the group order is divisible by p to determine if the norm map is an equivalence.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic homotopy theory of the K(n)-local category and the theory of the chromatic homotopy theory of the K(n)-local category is a recursive formulation that emphasizes the self-referential nature of the classification program. The K(n)-local category SpK(n) is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category whose compact objects are the K(n)-localizations of finite spectra of type n. The thick subcategory theorem classifies the thick tensor ideals of compact objects, and the Bousfield lattice classifies the smashing localizations. The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic homotopy theory of the K(n)-local category and the theory of the chromatic homotopy theory of the K(n)-local category continues the analysis of the structural invariants of SpK(n). The Goerss–Hopkins obstruction theory provides a framework for constructing E_∞-structures on spectra, and the Lubin–Tate spectra E_nhH inherit E_∞-structures from E_n. The chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis identifies the fully dualizable objects in SpK(n) with the compact objects, and the factorization homology of E_n-algebras over manifolds provides a topological chiral homology theory connected to the local Langlands correspondence.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of Ambidexterity

Ambidexterity in the K(n)-local category refers to the equivalence between finite limits and finite colimits for certain diagrams, a phenomenon first systematically studied by Hopkins and Lurie. In the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n), the functors of taking homotopy fixed points and homotopy orbits for a finite group G coincide after K(n)-localization when the order of G is prime to p. More generally, for a finite p-group G, the norm map from homotopy orbits to homotopy fixed points becomes an equivalence after K(n)-localization. This is the content of the ambidexterity theorem: for any finite ∞-groupoid X, the K(n)-localization of the map Σ^∞_+ X → F(Σ^∞_+ X, S) is an equivalence. The proof uses the transchromatic character map and the fact that the Morava E-theory of classifying spaces of finite groups is concentrated in even degrees. The ambidexterity theorem implies that the K(n)-local category is compactly assembled, and that the dualizable objects are precisely the compact objects. This has profound consequences for the classification of localizing subcategories: every localizing subcategory of SpK(n) is generated by a set of compact objects, and the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is isomorphic to the lattice of thick subcategories of compact objects. The ambidexterity theorem also underlies the construction of the determinant map on the K(n)-local Picard group, as the duality pairing on Picard-graded homotopy groups is induced by the ambidextrous structure. In the context of the cobordism hypothesis, ambidexterity identifies the fully dualizable objects in SpK(n) with the compact objects, and the factorization homology of E_n-algebras over manifolds with boundary is governed by the ambidextrous adjunction between induction and restriction along the inclusion of the boundary.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category as a Module Category and the Classification of Localizing Subcategories

The K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category that is compactly generated by the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S. As a module category over itself, its localizing subcategories are classified by the thick subcategories of compact objects via the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins and Smith. Every localizing subcategory of SpK(n) is generated by a set of compact objects, and the lattice of localizing subcategories is isomorphic to the lattice of thick subcategories of the compact objects. The compact objects in SpK(n) are precisely the dualizable objects, and they are classified by their Morava K-theory type: a compact object X has type n if K(n)_*X ≠ 0 but K(m)_*X = 0 for m > n. The type n compact objects form a thick subcategory C_n, and the localizing subcategory generated by C_n is the category of K(n)-local spectra of type ≥ n. The classification of localizing subcategories implies that the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is the lattice of Bousfield classes of objects in SpK(n), and it is isomorphic to the lattice of thick subcategories of compact objects. This lattice is totally ordered by the chromatic filtration: the Bousfield class of K(m) is strictly less than the Bousfield class of K(n) for m < n. The telescope conjecture in the K(n)-local category is equivalent to the statement that every smashing localization is generated by a set of compact objects, which would imply that the Bousfield lattice is generated by the Bousfield classes of the Morava K-theories K(m) for m ≤ n. The recent counterexamples to the telescope conjecture in the p-local category by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, and Schlank do not directly affect the K(n)-local telescope conjecture, which remains open for n ≥ 2. The classification of localizing subcategories also plays a crucial role in the chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis, where the fully dualizable objects in SpK(n) are identified with the compact objects, and the factorization homology of E_n-algebras is computed via the ambidextrous adjunction.

Worked Example: Classifying a Localizing Subcategory
Let L be the localizing subcategory of SpK(2) generated by the type 2 complex V(1) (the cofiber of v1 on a type 1 complex). Determine whether L contains the K(2)-local sphere LK(2)S.
The type 2 complex V(1) has K(2)_*V(1) ≠ 0 and K(1)_*V(1) = 0. By the thick subcategory theorem, the thick subcategory generated by V(1) is precisely the category of type 2 compact objects C2. The localizing subcategory L generated by V(1) is the category of K(2)-local spectra of type ≥ 2. Since the K(2)-local sphere LK(2)S has K(2)_*LK(2)S ≠ 0 and K(1)_*LK(2)S = 0 (as LK(2)S is K(1)-acyclic), it has type 2. Therefore LK(2)S ∈ L. In fact, L is the entire category SpK(2) because every object in SpK(2) has type ≥ 2 by definition of the K(2)-local category.
Exam Strategy: Localizing vs Thick Subcategories
In the K(n)-local category, the classification of localizing subcategories mirrors the classification of thick subcategories of compact objects. Remember: a localizing subcategory is closed under arbitrary coproducts, while a thick subcategory is only closed under finite colimits and retracts. The thick subcategory theorem says thick subcategories of compact objects are classified by Morava K-theory type. The localizing subcategory generated by a thick subcategory C_n is the category of K(n)-local spectra of type ≥ n. For exam questions, identify the type of the generator, then determine the localizing subcategory it generates. The K(n)-local sphere always has type n, so it lies in the localizing subcategory generated by any type n compact object.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Fracture Cube

The chromatic fracture square generalizes to an iterated fracture cube that encodes the relationship between successive K(n)-localizations and the monochromatic layers M_n = LK(n)Ln-1. For a spectrum X, the chromatic tower X → ⋯ → L_n X → Ln-1 X → ⋯ → L0 X has fibers M_n X. The chromatic fracture square for L_n X is the pullback square L_n X → LK(n) X ↓ ↓ Ln-1 X → LK(n) Ln-1 X. Iterating this construction yields the chromatic fracture cube, an n-dimensional cube whose vertices are the iterated localizations LK(i1) ⋯ LK(i_k) X for subsets {i1, …, i_k} ⊆ {1, …, n}. The total fiber of this cube is the monochromatic layer M_n X, and the cube expresses the fact that the chromatic tower is a Postnikov tower in the category of K(n)-local spectra. The chromatic fracture cube is closely related to the transchromatic character map, which relates the Morava E-theory of classifying spaces at different heights. The cube also governs the behavior of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence under the chromatic filtration: the E2-term of the ANSS for L_n X is the continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n with coefficients in (E_n)_*X, and the fracture cube induces a spectral sequence converging to this cohomology whose E1-page involves the cohomology of the subgroups 𝔾_m for m < n. The chromatic fracture cube is essential for the proof of the chromatic convergence theorem, which states that for a finite spectrum X, the natural map X → holim_n L_n X is an equivalence. The convergence fails for infinite spectra, and the failure is measured by the derived functors of the inverse limit, which are related to the phantom maps in the chromatic tower. The chromatic fracture cube also appears in the context of the Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor, where the layers of the Taylor tower are related to the monochromatic layers M_n.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the K(n)-Local Brauer Group

The Brauer group of the K(n)-local category, denoted Br(SpK(n)), classifies Azumaya algebras in SpK(n) up to Morita equivalence. An Azumaya algebra is a dualizable E1-algebra A in SpK(n) such that the map A ⊗ Aop → End(A) is an equivalence. The Brauer group fits into an exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, where the map to continuous cohomology is given by the determinant of the Brauer class. The exotic Brauer group, the kernel of the determinant map, is isomorphic to H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) / im(det). For n=1, the Brauer group is trivial at odd primes and ℤ/2 at p=2, generated by the quaternion algebra. For n=2, the Brauer group has been computed by Antieau, Mathew, and others: it contains a subgroup isomorphic to ℚ/ℤ coming from the Morava K-theory K(2), and the exotic part is related to the cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾2. The Brauer group is closely related to the Picard group via the determinant map, and both are computed using the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of 𝔾_n on the units of E_n. The Brauer group also appears in the context of the chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis, where fully dualizable objects in the 2-category of K(n)-local module categories correspond to Azumaya algebras. The classification of smashing localizations in the K(n)-local category is equivalent to the K(n)-local telescope conjecture, and the Brauer group provides obstructions to the existence of certain smashing localizations. The recent work on the telescope conjecture has shown that the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is more complicated than previously thought, and the Brauer group may contain elements that are not detected by the Morava K-theories K(m) for m ≤ n.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Extended Morava Stabilizer Group

The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n^ext is the semidirect product of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n with the Galois group Gal(𝔽p^n/𝔽_p) ≅ ℤ/n. It acts on the Morava E-theory spectrum E_n by E_∞ ring maps, and the homotopy fixed points (E_n)h𝔾_n^ext recover the Morava K-theory K(n) up to a shift. The action of the Galois group on E_n is induced by the action on the Lubin–Tate deformation space: the Frobenius automorphism of 𝔽p^n acts on the universal deformation of the Honda formal group law by raising the coefficients to the p-th power. This action is compatible with the power operations in Morava E-theory, which are encoded by the Dyer–Lashof algebra at height n. The extended Morava stabilizer group also acts on the Lubin–Tate tower, and the quotient by the Galois group yields the moduli stack of formal groups of height exactly n. The continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n^ext with coefficients in (E_n)_* computes the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence for the K(n)-local sphere: H^*cts(𝔾_n^ext; (E_n)_*) ≅ H^*cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)_*)Gal. The Gross–Hopkins duality for the extended group involves a twist by the determinant character of the Galois action. The extended Morava stabilizer group plays a crucial role in the chromatic approach to the local Langlands correspondence: the Lubin–Tate tower uniformizes Drinfeld's upper half-space, and the action of 𝔾_n^ext on the cohomology of the tower realizes the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(ℚ_p). The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem ensures that the action of 𝔾_n^ext on E_n lifts to an action by E_∞ ring spectra, and the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for 𝔾_n^ext converges to π_*K(n). The chromatic fracture cube for the extended group relates the K(n)-local category to the categories of spectra with actions of the subgroups of 𝔾_n^ext.

  • Confusing the ambidexterity theorem (finite limits ≃ finite colimits for finite group actions after K(n)-localization) with the statement that all limits and colimits commute; ambidexterity holds only for finite ∞-groupoids and requires K(n)-localization.
  • Assuming the K(n)-local Brauer group is isomorphic to H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×); the determinant map has a nontrivial kernel (the exotic Brauer group) and the Picard group injects into the Brauer group.
  • Treating the chromatic fracture cube as a strict pullback cube of categories; it is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories, and the maps are localizations, not inclusions.
  • Believing the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n^ext acts on E_n only through the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n; the Galois group ℤ/n acts nontrivially by E_∞ ring maps, and this action is essential for recovering K(n) as homotopy fixed points.
  • Conflating the classification of localizing subcategories in SpK(n) (all generated by compact objects) with the classification in the p-local category (where the telescope conjecture fails); the K(n)-local telescope conjecture is still open for n ≥ 2.
  • Neglecting the role of the determinant map in the exact sequence for the Brauer group; the exotic Brauer group is the cokernel of the determinant map from the Picard group, not the whole cohomology group H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×).
  • Assuming the chromatic convergence theorem holds for all spectra; it holds for finite spectra, but for infinite spectra the map X → holim_n L_n X can fail to be an equivalence, and the failure is measured by phantom maps in the chromatic tower.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local category SpK(n) is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category whose compact objects are precisely the K(n)-localizations of finite spectra. Its Balmer spectrum is homeomorphic to the set of thick tensor ideals in the subcategory of compact objects, and the classification of thick subcategories by the thick subcategory theorem yields a stratification by height. The Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) classifies invertible objects up to equivalence; it fits into an exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic(SpK(n)) → H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, where the first map sends an integer k to the suspension Σ^k LK(n)S. The exotic Picard elements correspond to nontrivial continuous cohomology classes of the Morava stabilizer group acting on the units of the Morava E-theory ring. The determinant map from the Picard group to the Brauer group has kernel the exotic Picard group and cokernel the exotic Brauer group. The K(n)-local category also admits a t-structure whose heart is equivalent to the category of continuous comodules over the Morava stabilizer group, providing an algebraic model for the homotopy category of K(n)-local spectra. This t-structure is compatible with the symmetric monoidal structure and yields a derived category interpretation of the Adams–Novikov E2-term as Ext groups in this heart.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic fracture cube generalizes the chromatic fracture square by iterating localization at successive Morava K-theories. For a spectrum X, the cube has vertices LK(0)X, LK(1)X, …, LK(n)X and maps given by the natural transformations between these localizations. The total homotopy limit of the cube recovers the p-localization of X when X is finite, but for infinite spectra the map X → holimi LK(i)X can fail to be an equivalence; the failure is measured by phantom maps in the chromatic tower. The cube is a homotopy pullback diagram in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories, and each face is a chromatic fracture square. The iterated fiber sequences give rise to a spectral sequence converging to π_*X with E1-page given by the homotopy groups of the iterated fibers. This spectral sequence is closely related to the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence computing the cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups. The cube also encodes the interaction between the various localizations and the monochromatic layers M_nX = fib(LK(n)X → LK(n-1)X). The monochromatic layers are K(n)-local and K(n-1)-acyclic, and their study leads to the chromatic splitting conjecture, which predicts a decomposition of the K(n)-local sphere after a suitable Galois extension.

Worked Example: Computing the Picard Group at Height 1
Let p be an odd prime and n = 1. The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾1 is isomorphic to the units ℤ_p^× of the p-adic integers. The Morava E-theory E1 is the p-complete complex K-theory spectrum KU_p^∧. Compute the Picard group Pic(SpK(1)) using the exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic(SpK(1)) → H1cts(𝔾1; (E1)0^×) → 0.
At height 1, (E1)0 = ℤ_p and the units (E1)0^× = ℤ_p^×. The continuous cohomology group H1cts(ℤ_p^×; ℤ_p^×) classifies continuous crossed homomorphisms modulo principal ones. Since ℤ_p^× ≅ μp-1 × (1 + pℤ_p) and the action is by multiplication, the first cohomology vanishes for the pro-p part and is isomorphic to Homcts(μp-1, μp-1) ≅ ℤ/(p-1) for the torsion part. Thus H1cts(𝔾1; (E1)0^×) ≅ ℤ/(p-1). The exact sequence becomes 0 → ℤ → Pic(SpK(1)) → ℤ/(p-1) → 0. This sequence splits because the determinant map provides a section, yielding Pic(SpK(1)) ≅ ℤ × ℤ/(p-1). The ℤ factor corresponds to suspensions, and the finite cyclic factor corresponds to the exotic invertible K(1)-local spectra detected by the Adams operations.
Exam Strategy: Chromatic Fracture Cube
When a question asks about the chromatic fracture cube, first identify the height n and the relevant Morava K-theories K(0) through K(n). Recall that the cube is a homotopy pullback of presentable stable ∞-categories, not a strict pullback. Use the fiber sequences M_iX → LK(i)X → LK(i-1)X to break down computations. For finite spectra, the map X → holim_i LK(i)X is an equivalence; for infinite spectra, phantom maps obstruct it. In multiple-choice settings, look for statements that confuse the cube with a strict pullback or that claim the convergence holds for all spectra.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local Brauer group Br(SpK(n)) classifies Azumaya algebras in the K(n)-local category up to Morita equivalence. It fits into an exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, where the first map sends an invertible object to its endomorphism algebra. The determinant map from the Picard group to the Brauer group has image the subgroup of Brauer classes represented by matrix algebras over invertible objects; its cokernel is the exotic Brauer group. The Brauer group is computed via the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next on the units of E_n. At height 2 and p ≥ 5, the Brauer group is known to be isomorphic to ℤ/2 × ℤ/2, with one factor coming from the Picard group and the other from an exotic class. The Brauer group also appears in the classification of smashing localizations: a smashing localization of SpK(n) corresponds to a Bousfield class, and the telescope conjecture predicts that every smashing localization is generated by a compact object. Counterexamples to the telescope conjecture at height ≥ 2 would produce smashing localizations not detected by the Brauer group, indicating a richer Bousfield lattice.
