The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The transchromatic character map generalizes the classical character map from the representation ring of a finite group to the Morava E-theory of its classifying space. For a finite group G, there is a natural transformation χ: E_n0(BG) → Mapcts(Homcts(G, 𝔾_n), (E_n)0) that becomes an isomorphism after inverting the Euler classes of the representations. This map is constructed using the theory of power operations in Morava E-theory and the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin–Tate deformation space. The target can be identified with the ring of functions on the scheme of continuous homomorphisms from G to the Morava stabilizer group, which is a finite scheme over the Lubin–Tate ring. The character map is compatible with the Adams operations and the Dyer–Lashof algebra, and it provides a computational tool for the Morava E-theory of classifying spaces. In the limit as n → ∞, the transchromatic character map relates to the classical character theory of compact Lie groups via the work of Hopkins–Kuhn–Ravenel. The map also plays a role in the chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture, where the completion of the Burnside ring at a prime p is recovered from the K(n)-localizations of the sphere spectrum.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis identifies fully dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal (∞,n)-category of K(n)-local module categories with Azumaya algebras in the K(n)-local category. The classification of such objects reduces to the computation of the Brauer group and the Picard group. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the Morava E-theory spectrum E_n by E_∞ ring maps, and the homotopy fixed points (E_n)h𝔾_next recover the Morava K-theory K(n) up to a shift. This action is essential for the Galois descent that underlies the chromatic fracture cube. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem guarantees that the action lifts to an action by E_∞ ring spectra, making the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence a spectral sequence of E_∞ ring spectra. The E2-page of this spectral sequence is the continuous cohomology H^*cts(𝔾_next; (E_n)_*), which computes the Adams–Novikov E2-term for the K(n)-local sphere. The Gross–Hopkins duality for the extended group involves a twist by the determinant character of the Galois action, and the dualizing object is the Gross–Hopkins dual of the sphere shifted by n2 - n. The chromatic splitting conjecture predicts that after a suitable Galois extension, the K(n)-local sphere splits as a wedge of suspensions of the Morava K-theory K(n), and the splitting is governed by the action of the extended Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin–Tate tower.

  • Confusing the chromatic fracture cube with a strict pullback square of categories; it is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories.
  • Assuming the chromatic convergence theorem holds for all spectra; it holds for finite spectra, but infinite spectra may have phantom maps obstructing the equivalence X ≃ holim_n L_n X.
  • Believing the K(n)-local Brauer group is simply H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×); the determinant map from the Picard group has a nontrivial kernel and cokernel, giving exotic Brauer classes.
  • Treating the transchromatic character map as an isomorphism on the nose; it becomes an isomorphism only after inverting Euler classes of representations.
  • Neglecting the role of the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next in recovering K(n) as homotopy fixed points; the Galois group ℤ/n acts nontrivially by E_∞ ring maps.
  • Conflating the classification of smashing localizations in SpK(n) (all generated by compact objects if the telescope conjecture holds) with the classification in the p-local category where the telescope conjecture fails.
  • Assuming the Picard group injects into the Brauer group via the determinant map; the determinant map has a kernel (the exotic Picard group) and its image is the subgroup of matrix algebras.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The classification of localizing subcategories in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) is governed by the tensor-triangular geometry of the category. By the work of Barthel, Heard, and Valenzuela, the Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)^c (the compact objects) is homeomorphic to the space of prime ideals in the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n, equipped with the Zariski topology. This identifies the lattice of localizing subcategories with the lattice of specialization-closed subsets of Spec(𝔾_n). The minimal primes correspond to the heights of formal subgroups, and the generic point corresponds to the trivial subcategory. The telescope conjecture for SpK(n) is equivalent to the statement that every smashing localization is generated by a compact object, which in turn is equivalent to the condition that every localizing subcategory is generated by the compact objects it contains. Recent counterexamples by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, and Schlank show that at height n ≥ 2 and prime p ≥ 5, there exist smashing localizations not generated by compact objects, implying the telescope conjecture fails in SpK(n). These exotic localizations are detected by the K(n)-local Brauer group, which classifies Azumaya algebras in SpK(n). The Brauer group fits into an exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H3cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, where the boundary map is the determinant map from the Picard group. The failure of the telescope conjecture produces nontrivial elements in the cokernel of the determinant map, giving exotic Brauer classes not represented by matrix algebras.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor on spaces provides a chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category via the Taylor tower of the identity. The n-th layer of the Taylor tower, D_n(X) = hofib(P_n X → Pn-1 X), is a homogeneous functor of degree n, classified by a spectrum with Σ_n-action. The chromatic height of D_n(S0) is precisely n, and the v_n-periodic homotopy groups of spheres are detected by the derivatives of the identity. The Arone–Mahowald theorem identifies the n-th derivative of the identity at the sphere with the Spanier–Whitehead dual of the partition complex, and its K(n)-localization is equivalent to a wedge of suspensions of the Morava K-theory K(n). The Taylor tower converges for finite complexes, and the chromatic convergence theorem L_n X ≃ holim_k L_k X is reflected in the convergence of the Taylor tower after K(n)-localization. The Goodwillie tower also interacts with the cyclotomic trace: the topological cyclic homology TC(X) computes the homotopy fixed points of the circle action on THH(X), and the filtration by polynomial degree corresponds to the chromatic filtration. The redshift conjecture predicts that the algebraic K-theory of a ring spectrum of chromatic height n has height n+1, and this is witnessed by the action of the Taylor tower on THH and TC. The derivatives of the identity also carry an action of the Morava stabilizer group, and the homotopy fixed points recover the v_n-periodic homotopy groups of spheres via the chromatic spectral sequence.

Worked Example: Derivative of the Identity at Height 2
Compute the K(2)-localization of the second derivative of the identity ∂2(Id)(S0) at the prime p=3, and identify its homotopy groups in terms of the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾2.
The second derivative ∂2(Id)(S0) is the Spanier–Whitehead dual of the partition complex |Part(2)|, which is S0. Its K(2)-localization is LK(2) S0. By the Gross–Hopkins duality, the homotopy groups π_* LK(2) S0 are isomorphic to the continuous cohomology H^*cts(𝔾2; (E2)_*) shifted by -2. At p=3, the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾2 is a profinite group of dimension 4, and its cohomology with coefficients in (E2)_* = W(𝔽9)[[u1]][u^±] (with |u| = -2) computes the K(2)-local sphere. The homotopy groups π-1 and π-2 are nontrivial and correspond to the Greek letter elements β1 and β2 in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The K(2)-local sphere is not equivalent to K(2) itself; the chromatic splitting conjecture predicts that after a Galois extension of degree 2, LK(2) S0 splits as a wedge of suspensions of K(2). The action of 𝔾2 on ∂2(Id)(S0) factors through the determinant map det: 𝔾2 → ℤ3^×, and the homotopy fixed points (∂2(Id)(S0))h𝔾2 recover the v2-periodic homotopy groups of the sphere.
Exam Strategy: Goodwillie Calculus and Chromatic Height
Remember that the n-th derivative of the identity has chromatic height exactly n. The Taylor tower of the identity provides a filtration of the stable homotopy category whose layers are classified by spectra with Σ_n-action. The K(n)-localization of the n-th layer is a wedge of suspensions of K(n). The redshift conjecture (K-theory increases height by 1) is witnessed by the cyclotomic trace and the action of the Taylor tower on THH/TC. For exam questions, focus on the relationship between the degree of the derivative and the chromatic height, and the role of the Morava stabilizer group action on the derivatives.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The Bousfield lattice of the K(n)-local category classifies all Bousfield localizations, not just the smashing ones. By the work of Barthel, Heard, and others, the Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is isomorphic to the lattice of localizing subcategories, which is in turn isomorphic to the lattice of specialization-closed subsets of the Balmer spectrum Spec(SpK(n)^c). The Balmer spectrum is homeomorphic to the space of prime ideals in the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n. The minimal primes correspond to the heights of formal subgroups, and the generic point corresponds to the trivial subcategory. The telescope conjecture for SpK(n) is the statement that every smashing localization is generated by a compact object, which is equivalent to the condition that the Bousfield lattice coincides with the lattice of smashing localizations. The recent counterexamples to the telescope conjecture at height n ≥ 2 show that the Bousfield lattice is strictly larger than the lattice of smashing localizations. The exotic Bousfield classes are detected by the K(n)-local Brauer group, which classifies Azumaya algebras in SpK(n). The Brauer group fits into an exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H3cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, where the boundary map is the determinant map from the Picard group. The failure of the telescope conjecture produces nontrivial elements in the cokernel of the determinant map, giving exotic Brauer classes not represented by matrix algebras. These exotic classes correspond to non-smashing Bousfield localizations that cannot be generated by compact objects.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic fracture cube generalizes the chromatic fracture square to an iterated localization diagram involving all heights simultaneously. For a spectrum X, the chromatic fracture cube is a homotopy limit diagram of the form X → ∏_n L_n X ⇉ ∏n<m</sub> L_m L_n X → ⋯, where the maps are the localization maps and their composites. The total homotopy limit of this cube is the chromatic completion of X, and the chromatic convergence theorem states that for finite spectra, the natural map X → holim_n L_n X is an equivalence. For infinite spectra, phantom maps can obstruct this equivalence. The chromatic fracture cube is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories, not a strict pullback square of categories. The layers of the cube are the monochromatic categories M_n = fib(L_n → Ln-1), which are equivalent to the K(n)-local categories. The cube encodes the local-global principle: a property of X can be checked on each L_n X and the compatibility data. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the Morava E-theory spectrum E_n by E_∞ ring maps, and the homotopy fixed points (E_n)h𝔾_next recover the Morava K-theory K(n) up to a shift. This action is essential for the Galois descent that underlies the chromatic fracture cube. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem guarantees that the action lifts to an action by E_∞ ring spectra, making the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence a spectral sequence of E_∞ ring spectra. The E2-page of this spectral sequence is the continuous cohomology H^*cts(𝔾_next; (E_n)_*), which computes the Adams–Novikov E2-term for the K(n)-local sphere. The Gross–Hopkins duality for the extended group involves a twist by the determinant character of the Galois action, and the dualizing object is the Gross–Hopkins dual of the sphere shifted by n2 - n.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) classifies invertible objects in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category. By the work of Hopkins, Mahowald, and Sadofsky, and later by Heard and others, the Picard group fits into a short exact sequence 0 → Picex(SpK(n)) → Pic(SpK(n)) → ℤ → 0, where the map to ℤ is the determinant map (or dimension map) and the kernel Picex is the exotic Picard group. The exotic Picard group is isomorphic to the continuous cohomology group H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×), which classifies 1-cocycles of the Morava stabilizer group with values in the units of the Morava E-theory ring. The determinant map sends an invertible object to its dimension, which is an integer. The image of the determinant map is the subgroup of matrix algebras in the Brauer group. The cokernel of the determinant map in the Brauer group exact sequence gives exotic Brauer classes. The Picard group also carries a natural topology as a profinite group, and its Pontryagin dual is the K(n)-local sphere. The Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere with a shift of the sphere itself, and this duality exchanges the Picard group with the Brauer group. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the Picard group, and the homotopy fixed points recover the Picard group of the K(n)-local category as a module over the K(n)-local sphere. The chromatic splitting conjecture predicts that after a suitable Galois extension, the K(n)-local sphere splits as a wedge of suspensions of the Morava K-theory K(n), and the splitting is governed by the action of the extended Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin–Tate tower.

  • Confusing the chromatic fracture cube with a strict pullback square of categories; it is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories.
  • Assuming the chromatic convergence theorem holds for all spectra; it holds for finite spectra, but infinite spectra may have phantom maps obstructing the equivalence X ≃ holim_n L_n X.
  • Believing the K(n)-local Brauer group is simply H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×); the determinant map from the Picard group has a nontrivial kernel and cokernel, giving exotic Brauer classes.
  • Treating the transchromatic character map as an isomorphism on the nose; it becomes an isomorphism only after inverting Euler classes of representations.
  • Neglecting the role of the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next in recovering K(n) as homotopy fixed points; the Galois group ℤ/n acts nontrivially by E_∞ ring maps.
  • Conflating the classification of smashing localizations in SpK(n) (all generated by compact objects if the telescope conjecture holds) with the classification in the p-local category where the telescope conjecture fails.
  • Assuming the Picard group injects into the Brauer group via the determinant map; the determinant map has a kernel (the exotic Picard group) and its image is the subgroup of matrix algebras.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category as a Module Category over the Lubin–Tate Stack

The equivalence between the K(n)-local stable homotopy category and the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the Lubin–Tate stack MLT^n is a cornerstone of derived algebraic geometry in chromatic homotopy theory. The Lubin–Tate stack classifies deformations of the height n formal group law over a perfect field of characteristic p, and its structure sheaf Otop is an E_∞-ring valued sheaf whose global sections recover the Morava E-theory spectrum E_n. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem endows Otop with an action of the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next, and the homotopy fixed points (Otop)h𝔾_next yield the Morava K-theory K(n). The category QCoh(MLT^n, Otop) of Otop-module sheaves is a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category, and the K(n)-localization functor LK(n) corresponds to the derived global sections functor RΓ. This equivalence is not merely categorical: it respects the symmetric monoidal structure, the t-structure, and the action of the Picard group. The compact objects in SpK(n) correspond to perfect complexes on MLT^n, and the Brown–Comenetz dual of the sphere corresponds to the dualizing sheaf ω⊗(n2-n) shifted by n2-n. The chromatic fracture cube is then the statement that the ∞-category of p-local spectra is the limit of the diagram of ∞-categories QCoh(MLT^n, Otop) over the chromatic tower, with transition functors given by pullback along the natural maps MLT^n → MLTn-1. This perspective makes the local-global principle manifest: a property of a p-local spectrum X can be checked on its images in each QCoh(MLT^n, Otop) together with the compatibility data encoded by the fracture cube. The derived stack point of view also clarifies the role of the Morava stabilizer group: the quotient stack [Spf E_n0 / 𝔾_n] presents MLT^n, and the continuous cohomology H^*cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)_*) computes the homotopy groups of the K(n)-local sphere via the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence arises from the cosimplicial resolution of the structure sheaf by the Amitsur complex of the cover Spf E_n0 → MLT^n, and its E2-page is the cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups Mfg with coefficients in the sheaf ω⊗*. This identifies the Adams–Novikov E2-term for the sphere with H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*), a purely algebro-geometric object.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of Ambidexterity

Ambidexterity in the K(n)-local category is the phenomenon that for a finite group G of order prime to p, the induction and coinduction functors between the K(n)-local category and the K(n)-local category with G-action are equivalent. More precisely, for a finite G-set X, the norm map N_X: (Σ^∞_+ X)hG → (Σ^∞_+ X)hG is an equivalence after K(n)-localization. This was proven by Hopkins and Lurie as a consequence of the fact that the K(n)-local category is an algebraic stable homotopy category in the sense of Hovey–Palmieri–Strickland, and the Morava E-theory E_n is a K(n)-local E_∞-ring spectrum with π0 E_n a complete local ring of characteristic 0. The key input is that the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n acts on E_n by E_∞-ring maps, and the homotopy fixed points E_nhG for finite subgroups G ⊂ 𝔾_n are again E_∞-ring spectra. The ambidexterity theorem implies that the genuine G-equivariant K(n)-local stable homotopy category is equivalent to the naive G-equivariant category, i.e., the category of G-objects in SpK(n). This collapses the distinction between homotopy orbits and homotopy fixed points for finite groups of order prime to p, and it has profound consequences for the chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture and the Burnside ring. The norm map for the trivial subgroup gives the equivalence between the K(n)-local sphere and its Spanier–Whitehead dual up to a shift, which is the Gross–Hopkins duality. For a general finite group G, the ambidexterity equivalence identifies the K(n)-local genuine G-spectra with the ∞-category of modules over the K(n)-local group ring SK(n)[G], and the Adams isomorphism in equivariant homotopy theory becomes an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories. The proof uses the transchromatic character map to reduce to the case of height 1, where ambidexterity follows from the fact that the p-complete complex K-theory spectrum KU_p is a Galois extension of the p-complete sphere with group ℤ_p^×, and the norm map for finite subgroups of ℤ_p^× is an equivalence by the classical theory of Lubin–Tate formal groups. The transchromatic character map then lifts this equivalence to height n by comparing the action of finite subgroups of 𝔾_n on E_n with their action on the height 1 theory via the determinant map det: 𝔾_n → ℤ_p^×. This determinant map is the key to the chromatic fracture cube: it encodes the compatibility between the K(n)-local categories at different heights and the action of the Galois group on the Lubin–Tate tower.

Worked Example: Norm Map for Cyclic Subgroups of 𝔾_n
Let G = C_p be a cyclic subgroup of order p in the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n at an odd prime p. Show that the norm map N: (E_n)hC_p → (E_n)hC_p is an equivalence after K(n)-localization, and compute the effect on homotopy groups π_*(E_n) ≅ W(k)[[u1,…,un-1]][u±1] where |u| = -2.
The ambidexterity theorem of Hopkins–Lurie states that for any finite group G of order prime to p, the norm map N_X: (X)hG → XhG is an equivalence in SpK(n) for any K(n)-local spectrum X. Here G = C_p has order p, which is not prime to p, so the theorem does not apply directly. However, the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n contains no elements of order p (it is a pro-p-group of finite virtual cohomological dimension), so C_p cannot be a subgroup of 𝔾_n. The correct statement is that for any finite subgroup G ⊂ 𝔾_n, the order of G is prime to p. Indeed, 𝔾_n ≅ Aut(Γ_n) ⋊ Gal(k/𝔽_p) where Γ_n is the height n formal group over k = 𝔽p^n, and Aut(Γ_n) is a compact p-adic Lie group of dimension n2 with no p-torsion. Thus any finite subgroup G ⊂ 𝔾_n has order prime to p. For such G, the norm map N: (E_n)hG → (E_n)hG is an equivalence in SpK(n). On homotopy groups, this means the Tate construction (E_n)tG vanishes, so the norm map induces an isomorphism on homotopy groups. The homotopy fixed point spectral sequence H^*(G; π_*(E_n)) ⇒ π_*(E_nhG) collapses at E2 because the Tate spectral sequence vanishes, and the norm map identifies the homotopy orbits spectral sequence H_*(G; π_*(E_n)) with the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence. Since G acts continuously on π_*(E_n) through the determinant character det: G → ℤ_p^×, the action on u is by multiplication by det(g), and on the u_i by the adjoint action. The invariants π_*(E_n)^G are the subring fixed by this action, and the norm map induces an isomorphism π_*(E_n)hG ≅ π_*(E_n)hG ≅ π_*(E_n)^G.
Exam Strategy: Ambidexterity and the Norm Map
When a question asks about the norm map in K(n)-local homotopy theory, first check whether the group order is prime to p. If yes, ambidexterity applies and the norm is an equivalence. If the group has p-torsion, the norm is generally not an equivalence; the Tate construction does not vanish. Remember that finite subgroups of 𝔾_n always have order prime to p because 𝔾_n is a p-adic Lie group without p-torsion. The determinant map det: 𝔾_n → ℤ_p^× controls the action on the periodicity generator u ∈ π-2E_n, and the transchromatic character map reduces height n ambidexterity to height 1 where it follows from the Galois theory of Lubin–Tate extensions.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Classification of Localizing Subcategories

The classification of localizing subcategories of the K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) is a theorem of Barthel–Heard–Valenzuela (building on work of Hovey–Palmieri–Strickland and Ohkawa) which states that the lattice of localizing subcategories is in bijection with the lattice of subsets of the Balmer spectrum Spc(SpK(n)^c) of the compact objects. The Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)^c is homeomorphic to the topological space underlying the Lubin–Tate stack MLT^n, which is a single point with a nontrivial profinite automorphism group 𝔾_n. However, the tensor triangular geometry of SpK(n)^c is richer: the prime ideals in the tensor triangular sense correspond to the irreducible closed subsets of MLT^n, which are just the point itself and the empty set. This would suggest only two localizing subcategories, but the correct statement uses the fact that SpK(n) is not compactly generated by the sphere alone; the compact objects are the K(n)-localizations of finite spectra, and the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins–Smith classifies thick subcategories of finite spectra by their Morava K-theory type. The localizing subcategories of SpK(n) are in bijection with subsets of {0,1,…,n} where a subset S corresponds to the localizing subcategory generated by {LK(n)V(i) | i ∈ S} where V(i) are the Smith–Toda complexes of type i. This classification uses the fact that the K(n)-local category is an algebraic stable homotopy category with a single invariant prime (the Morava K-theory K(n)), and the localizing subcategories are generated by the compact objects they contain. The telescope conjecture for SpK(n) is equivalent to the statement that every smashing localization is generated by a set of compact objects, which would imply that the lattice of smashing localizations is isomorphic to the lattice of localizing subcategories. The recent counterexamples to the telescope conjecture by Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank show that there exist smashing localizations not generated by compact objects, so the lattice of smashing localizations is strictly larger. The classification of localizing subcategories also has a derived algebraic geometry interpretation: localizing subcategories of QCoh(MLT^n, Otop) correspond to full subcategories closed under colimits and extensions, which by the work of Lurie and others are classified by subsets of the underlying topological space of MLT^n together with a choice of torsion theory on the category of quasi-coherent sheaves. The non-compactly generated smashing localizations correspond to non-closed subsets or to torsion theories not generated by perfect complexes.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Fracture Cube

The chromatic fracture cube is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories that encodes the local-global principle for p-local spectra. Its vertices are the ∞-categories SpL_n of L_n-local spectra for n ≥ 0 (with L0 = L_ℚ) and SpK(n) of K(n)-local spectra for n ≥ 1. The edges are the localization functors L_n → Ln-1 and L_n → K(n), and the faces are homotopy pullback squares. The cube is the limit of the diagram of ∞-categories SpL_n over the chromatic tower, and the natural map Sp_p → lim_n SpL_n is an equivalence on finite spectra but not on all spectra due to phantom maps. The layers of the cube are the monochromatic categories M_n = fib(L_n → Ln-1), which are equivalent to SpK(n) via the functor X ↦ LK(n)X. The fracture cube provides a recursive method to compute the homotopy groups of a p-local spectrum X: one computes the homotopy groups of each L_n X and the maps between them, and then reconstructs π_*(X) as the homotopy limit of the tower π_*(L_n X). The convergence of this tower is the chromatic convergence theorem, which holds for finite spectra but fails for infinite spectra in general. The obstruction is measured by the phantom maps in the tower, which are classified by the derived functors of the inverse limit lim1. The fracture cube also governs the behavior of the Picard group: the K(n)-local Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) fits into a pullback square with the Picard groups of the adjacent layers, and the determinant map det: Pic(SpK(n)) → ℤ is compatible with the fracture cube. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the entire fracture cube by E_∞-ring maps, and the homotopy fixed points under the Galois group ℤ/n recover the K(n)-local category from the E_n-local category. The Goerss–Hopkins–Miller theorem ensures that this action lifts to the level of E_∞-ring spectra, making the fracture cube a diagram of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories. The chromatic fracture cube is the categorical shadow of the arithmetic fracture square for the Lubin–Tate tower: the map of stacks MLT^n → MLTn-1 is a 𝔾_n-torsor, and the fracture cube is the ∞-categorical analogue of the Čech nerve of this cover. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence arises from the cosimplicial resolution of the structure sheaf on Mfg by the Amitsur complex of the cover ∐_n MLT^n → Mfg, and its E2-page is the cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups with coefficients in the sheaf ω⊗*. This identifies the Adams–Novikov E2-term for the sphere with H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*), and the fracture cube corresponds to the filtration of this cohomology by the height stratification of Mfg.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the K(n)-Local Brauer Group

The K(n)-local Brauer group Br(SpK(n)) classifies Azumaya algebras in the K(n)-local stable homotopy category up to Morita equivalence. An Azumaya algebra is an E_∞-ring spectrum A in SpK(n) such that A is invertible as a module over A ⊗ Aop and the map A ⊗ Aop → End(A) is an equivalence. The Brauer group fits into an exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0 where the map from the Picard group sends an invertible object to its endomorphism algebra, and the map to continuous cohomology is the obstruction to lifting a Brauer class to a Picard class. The continuous cohomology group H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) classifies the "exotic" Brauer classes that do not come from matrix algebras over invertible objects. The determinant map det: Pic(SpK(n)) → ℤ has kernel the exotic Picard group Picex(SpK(n)) ≅ H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×), and its image is the subgroup of matrix algebras in the Brauer group. The cokernel of the determinant map in the Brauer group exact sequence gives the exotic Brauer classes. The Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere with a shift of the sphere itself, and this duality exchanges the Picard group with the Brauer group via the map that sends an invertible object to its endomorphism algebra. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the Brauer group, and the homotopy fixed points recover the Brauer group of the K(n)-local category as a module over the K(n)-local sphere. The Brauer group also has a derived algebraic geometry interpretation: it classifies Azumaya algebras on the Lubin–Tate stack MLT^n, which are sheaves of algebras locally equivalent to matrix algebras. The cohomological Brauer group H2ét(MLT^n, 𝔾_m) injects into the K(n)-local Brauer group, and the cokernel is related to the exotic Picard group. The recent work of Antieau–Mathew and others has computed the Brauer group for n=1,2 at certain primes, showing that exotic Brauer classes exist and are detected by the Morava stabilizer group cohomology. The Brauer group is also related to the chromatic splitting conjecture: if the K(n)-local sphere splits as a wedge of suspensions of K(n) after a Galois extension, then the Brauer group would become trivial after that extension, and the exotic classes would be killed by the Galois action.

  • Confusing the classification of localizing subcategories in SpK(n) (generated by compact objects) with the classification of smashing localizations (which includes non-compactly generated ones after the telescope conjecture counterexamples).
  • Assuming the norm map is an equivalence for all finite groups; it only holds for groups of order prime to p, and finite subgroups of 𝔾_n always satisfy this condition.
  • Treating the chromatic fracture cube as a strict pullback square of categories; it is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories.
  • Believing the K(n)-local Brauer group is simply H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×); the determinant map from the Picard group has a nontrivial kernel and cokernel, giving exotic Brauer classes not captured by continuous cohomology alone.
  • Neglecting the role of the determinant map det: 𝔾_n → ℤ_p^× in the transchromatic character map; it is the key to reducing height n ambidexterity to height 1.
  • Assuming the chromatic convergence theorem holds for all spectra; it holds for finite spectra, but infinite spectra may have phantom maps obstructing the equivalence X ≃ holim_n L_n X.
  • Conflating the Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)^c (a single point) with the classification of localizing subcategories (which is by Morava K-theory type of finite spectra, not by tensor triangular primes).

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local stable homotopy category SpK(n) admits a symmetric monoidal structure whose unit is the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0. The Picard group Pic(SpK(n)) classifies invertible objects under this tensor product. By work of Hopkins–Mahowald–Sadofsky and later of Gross–Hopkins, there is a determinant homomorphism det: Pic(SpK(n)) → ℤ_p^× whose kernel is the exotic Picard group Picex(SpK(n)) ≅ H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×). The image of det is an open subgroup of ℤ_p^× of finite index, and its cokernel measures the failure of every invertible object to be a suspension of the sphere. The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_n acts continuously on the Morava E-theory spectrum E_n, and the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence H^*cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)_*) ⇒ π_*LK(n)S0 computes the homotopy groups of the K(n)-local sphere. The E2-page is the continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n with coefficients in the graded ring (E_n)_* ≅ W(𝔽p^n)[[u1,…,un-1]][u±1], where |u| = -2. The differentials in this spectral sequence are governed by the action of the extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next = 𝔾_n ⋊ Gal(𝔽p^n/𝔽_p). The Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the Anderson dual IK(n) of the K(n)-local sphere with a shift Σn2-nLK(n)S0, and this duality exchanges the Picard group with the Brauer group Br(SpK(n)) via the endomorphism algebra construction. The Brauer group fits into an exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, where the last term classifies exotic Brauer classes not coming from matrix algebras over invertible objects. Recent computations by Antieau–Mathew for n=1,2 at certain primes show that exotic Brauer classes exist and are detected by the continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n. The chromatic fracture cube provides a homotopy pullback diagram of ∞-categories relating SpK(n) to the categories of modules over the Lubin–Tate tower, and the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence arises from the cosimplicial resolution of the structure sheaf on the moduli stack Mfg by the Amitsur complex of the cover ∐_n MLT^n → Mfg. Its E2-page is H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*), which identifies with the Adams–Novikov E2-term for the sphere. The height stratification of Mfg induces a filtration on this cohomology that corresponds to the chromatic filtration of the stable homotopy category.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The transchromatic character map generalizes the classical character map from representation theory to chromatic homotopy theory. For a finite group G of order prime to p, the map K(n)^*(BG) → ⊕(H) K(n)^*(BH)W_G(H) where the sum runs over conjugacy classes of subgroups H ≤ G of p-power order, becomes an isomorphism after inverting the Euler classes of the relevant representations. This map is constructed using the norm map N: Σ^∞_+ BG → LK(n)S0 and the fact that the norm is an equivalence for groups of order prime to p. The determinant map det: 𝔾_n → ℤ_p^× plays a crucial role in reducing height n ambidexterity to height 1: the norm map for a finite subgroup H ≤ 𝔾_n factors through the determinant, and the resulting height 1 theory is the p-complete complex K-theory. The generalized Tate construction for a finite group G acting on a K(n)-local spectrum X is defined as the cofiber of the norm map XhG → XhG. When |G| is prime to p, this Tate construction vanishes, which is the essence of the ambidexterity theorem of Hopkins–Lurie. The chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture identifies the completion of the Burnside ring A(G) at the augmentation ideal with the zeroth stable homotopy group of the G-sphere after K(n)-localization. The Mahowald invariant provides a systematic way to detect v_n-periodic families in the stable homotopy groups of spheres by relating elements in the image of J to elements in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The beta family at height 2 arises from the elliptic curve formal group and is detected by the cohomology of the moduli stack of elliptic curves; the gamma family at height 3 involves the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the Lubin–Tate deformation space of a height 3 formal group. The chromatic redshift conjecture predicts that the algebraic K-theory of a ring spectrum of chromatic height n has chromatic height n+1. This has been verified for the sphere spectrum (where K(S) has height 1) and for certain Thom spectra. The cyclotomic trace map from algebraic K-theory to topological cyclic homology provides a computational bridge, and the work of Hesselholt–Madsen on the K-theory of truncated polynomial algebras gives explicit calculations at height 1. The motivic Adams–Novikov spectral sequence extends these ideas to motivic spectra over a field, with the motivic Steenrod algebra playing the role of the classical Steenrod algebra. The Lichtenbaum–Quillen conjecture, now a theorem of Voevodsky–Rost, relates étale K-theory to motivic cohomology and is reflected chromatically by the fact that the K(1)-localization of the algebraic K-theory of a number field agrees with its étale K-theory after p-completion.

Worked Example: Computing the Exotic Picard Group at Height 2
Let p=3, n=2. The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾2 is the semi-direct product of the units in the division algebra of invariant 1/2 over ℚ3 with the Galois group Gal(𝔽9/𝔽3). Its continuous cohomology H1cts(𝔾2; (E2)0^×) classifies the exotic Picard group. Using the known presentation of 𝔾2 and the fact that (E2)0^× ≅ W(𝔽9)^× × (1 + (u1)W(𝔽9)[[u1]]), compute the rank of the exotic Picard group as a ℤ3-module.
The group 𝔾2 has a pro-3 Sylow subgroup of rank 4. The coefficient module (E2)0^× is a ℤ3-module of rank 2 (from W(𝔽9)^× ≅ ℤ3 × ℤ/8) plus a pro-3 part from 1+(u1). By the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence for the extension 1 → S2 → 𝔾2 → Gal(𝔽9/𝔽3) → 1, the continuous cohomology H1cts(𝔾2; (E2)0^×) is a ℤ3-module of rank 2. Therefore Picex(SpK(2)) ≅ ℤ32 at p=3, n=2.
Exam Strategy: Recognizing Chromatic Patterns
When a question asks for the structure of Pic(SpK(n)) or Br(SpK(n)), first write down the determinant exact sequence and the Brauer exact sequence. Identify the continuous cohomology groups H1 and H2 of 𝔾_n with coefficients in (E_n)0^×. Remember that the determinant map lands in ℤ_p^×, not ℤ_p. For transchromatic character maps, check whether the finite group order is prime to p; if not, the norm map is not an equivalence and the character map need not be an isomorphism. In spectral sequence problems, note that the Adams–Novikov E2-term for the sphere is H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*) and the chromatic filtration corresponds to the height stratification. Use the Gross–Hopkins duality shift n2-n to translate between Anderson duals and sphere suspensions.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The Bousfield–Kuhn functor Φ: Sp → SpK(1) sends a spectrum X to the K(1)-localization of the mapping spectrum F(Σ^∞_+ Ω^∞ X, LK(1)S0). It provides a chromatic approach to unstable homotopy theory by approximating the unstable homotopy groups of a space through the K(1)-local stable homotopy groups of its suspension spectrum. The functor satisfies a chromatic fracture square relating Φ(X) to the K(1)-localizations of the layers of the Goodwillie tower of the identity functor on spaces. The Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor yields a Taylor tower whose layers are homogeneous functors classified by spectra with Σ_n-actions. The K(n)-localization of these layers is governed by the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the extended power constructions. The chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis identifies fully dualizable objects in the symmetric monoidal ∞-category SpK(n) with certain K(n)-local Thom spectra. The classification of smashing localizations in SpK(n) beyond the telescope conjecture involves the Bousfield lattice of the K(n)-local category; recent counterexamples to the telescope conjecture by Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank show that there exist smashing localizations not generated by compact objects. These are detected by the chromatic fracture cube, which iterates the fracture square across heights. The K(n)-local Brauer group classifies Azumaya algebras in SpK(n); an Azumaya algebra is an E_∞-ring spectrum A such that A is invertible as an A⊗Aop-module and the canonical map A⊗Aop → End(A) is an equivalence. The Brauer group fits into the exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, and the determinant map on Pic induces a map to the Brauer group whose cokernel captures exotic Brauer classes. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the Brauer group, and the homotopy fixed points recover the Brauer group of the K(n)-local category as a module over the K(n)-local sphere. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence for Morava E-theory has E2-page H^*cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)_*E_n) and converges to π_*LK(n)E_n. The Morava E-theory of classifying spaces E_n^*(BG) for finite groups G is computed by the generalized character theory of Hopkins–Kuhn–Ravenel, which expresses E_n^*(BG) as a ring of functions on the scheme of n-tuples of commuting elements in the formal group. The Lubin–Tate tower MLT^n → MLTn-1 is a 𝔾_n-torsor, and its Čech nerve gives the chromatic fracture cube. Drinfeld's upper half-space Ω^n = ℙn-1 ∪H H over ℚ_p provides a rigid analytic uniformization of the Lubin–Tate tower, linking it to the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(ℚ_p). The Morava E-theory of Eilenberg–MacLane spaces E_n^*(K(ℤ/p, m)) is described by the Ravenel–Wilson Hopf ring structure, which is a polynomial algebra on certain classes with coproduct given by the formal group law. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence for the moduli stack Mfg has E2-page H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*) and its differentials are controlled by the geometry of the height stratification. The K(n)-local Picard group, Gross–Hopkins duality, and the determinant map together give a complete description of the invertible objects and their duality properties in SpK(n).

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic homotopy theory of p-compact groups classifies K(n)-local spectra with actions of a p-compact group G. A p-compact group is a loop space BG such that H^*(BG; 𝔽_p) is finite and G is p-complete. The classification theorem of Dwyer–Wilkerson and Andersen–Grodal shows that connected p-compact groups are determined by their root data over the p-adic integers. The K(n)-local category of G-spectra is equivalent to the category of modules over the K(n)-localization of the group ring spectrum Σ^∞_+ G. The transchromatic character map for p-compact groups involves the generalized character theory of the formal group of height n. The chromatic approach to the Baum–Connes conjecture uses the assembly map in K(n)-local homotopy theory; the K(n)-localization of the group C*-algebra is related to the K(n)-local homotopy groups of the classifying space for proper actions. Factorization homology of n-manifolds with coefficients in an E_n-algebra provides a topological chiral homology theory; its K(n)-localization is governed by the action of the Morava stabilizer group on the E_n-algebra structure. Higher real K-theories EO_n provide a family of ring spectra interpolating between real K-theory (height 1) and Morava E-theory (height n). Their homotopy groups are computed by a homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for the action of a finite subgroup of 𝔾_n. Transchromatic duality relates the K(n)-local Picard group to the K(n-1)-local Picard group via the norm map along the Lubin–Tate tower. Chromatic nilpotence filtration via finite subgroup schemes of formal groups gives a filtration on the stable homotopy category whose associated graded pieces are detected by Morava K-theories. Derived algebraic geometry meets chromatic homotopy in the construction of the Lubin–Tate stack as a spectral Deligne–Mumford stack; its quasi-coherent sheaves model the K(n)-local category. Recent progress on the telescope conjecture includes the construction of a counterexample at height 2 and prime 2 by Burklund–Hahn–Levy–Schlank, using the chromatic fracture cube to produce a smashing localization not generated by a type n complex. Chromatic redshift in algebraic K-theory is now understood via the cyclotomic trace and the Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy theorem, which reduces the computation of K-theory of ring spectra to topological cyclic homology. The string orientation of tmf provides a map of ring spectra MString → tmf, and its effect on homotopy groups detects the beta family at height 2. The Mahowald invariant gives a systematic correspondence between elements in the image of J and v_n-periodic families in the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The K(n)-local Picard group computed via continuous cohomology of 𝔾_n yields explicit descriptions of exotic invertible objects. Goodwillie calculus and the Taylor tower of the identity functor on spaces have layers whose K(n)-localizations are classified by the action of the extended Morava stabilizer group on the partition complex. The Bousfield lattice of the K(n)-local category classifies smashing localizations; beyond the telescope conjecture, there exist non-compactly generated smashing localizations detected by the chromatic fracture cube. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next includes the Galois group of the base field and acts on Morava E-theory; its continuous cohomology computes the homotopy groups of the K(n)-local sphere. The chromatic fracture cube iterates the fracture square across heights, giving a homotopy limit diagram that reconstructs the stable homotopy category from its K(n)-local layers. The K(n)-local Brauer group classifies Azumaya algebras, and its exotic part is measured by H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) modulo the image of the determinant map from the Picard group.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic spectral sequence for Morava E-theory is a spectral sequence of algebras converging to π_*LK(n)E_n with E2-page the continuous cohomology H^*cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)_*E_n). The Morava E-theory of classifying spaces E_n^*(BG) for finite groups G is a completed tensor product of copies of E_n^*(BCp^k) indexed by the conjugacy classes of abelian p-subgroups of G. The Lubin–Tate tower MLT^n → MLTn-1 is a 𝔾_n-torsor in the category of formal stacks, and its rigid analytic generic fiber is Drinfeld's upper half-space Ω^n. The local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(ℚ_p) is realized geometrically by the cohomology of the Lubin–Tate tower with coefficients in the sheaf of Morava E-theory. The Morava E-theory of Eilenberg–MacLane spaces E_n^*(K(ℤ/p, m)) carries a Hopf ring structure described by Ravenel–Wilson; the primitives are generated by certain classes vm,i with coproduct given by the formal group law. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence for the moduli stack Mfg has E2-page H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*) and its differentials are induced by the geometry of the height stratification; the E_∞-page gives the associated graded of the Adams–Novikov filtration on π_*S0. The K(n)-local Picard group, Gross–Hopkins duality, and the determinant map together give a complete description of the invertible objects in SpK(n): Pic(SpK(n)) ≅ ℤ × Picex(SpK(n)), where the ℤ factor records the suspension and Picex ≅ H1cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×). The determinant map det: Pic(SpK(n)) → ℤ_p^× has kernel Picex and image an open subgroup of finite index. The Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere is IK(n) ≅ Σn2-nLK(n)S0, and Gross–Hopkins duality identifies the functor X ↦ FK(n)(X, IK(n)) with the functor X ↦ Σn2-nX. This duality exchanges the Picard group with the Brauer group via the endomorphism algebra construction. The extended Morava stabilizer group 𝔾_next acts on the Brauer group, and the homotopy fixed points recover the Brauer group of the K(n)-local category as a module over the K(n)-local sphere. The chromatic fracture cube is a homotopy pullback cube of presentable stable ∞-categories whose vertices are the K(m)-local categories for m ≤ n and whose edges are the localization functors. The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence arises from the cosimplicial resolution of the structure sheaf on Mfg by the Amitsur complex of the cover ∐_m MLT^m → Mfg. Its E2-page is H^*(Mfg; ω⊗*), and the height filtration on Mfg induces the chromatic filtration on the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence. The K(n)-local category as a module category over the Lubin–Tate stack is equivalent to the category of quasi-coherent sheaves on the spectral Lubin–Tate stack. The classification of localizing subcategories in SpK(n) is given by the tensor triangular geometry of the compact objects: the Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)^c is a single point, but the lattice of localizing subcategories is classified by the Morava K-theory type of finite spectra. The K(n)-local Brauer group fits into the exact sequence 0 → Pic(SpK(n)) → Br(SpK(n)) → H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×) → 0, and the exotic Brauer classes are those not in the image of the Picard group.

  • Confusing the determinant map landing in ℤ_p^× with a map to ℤ_p; the target is the group of p-adic units.
  • Assuming the norm map is an equivalence for all finite groups; it only holds for groups of order prime to p.
  • Treating the chromatic fracture cube as a strict pullback square of categories; it is a homotopy pullback cube in the ∞-category of presentable stable ∞-categories.
  • Believing the K(n)-local Brauer group is simply H2cts(𝔾_n; (E_n)0^×); the determinant map from the Picard group has a nontrivial kernel and cokernel, giving exotic Brauer classes not captured by continuous cohomology alone.
  • Neglecting the role of the determinant map det: 𝔾_n → ℤ_p^× in the transchromatic character map; it is the key to reducing height n ambidexterity to height 1.
  • Assuming the chromatic convergence theorem holds for all spectra; it holds for finite spectra, but infinite spectra may have phantom maps obstructing the equivalence X ≃ holim_n L_n X.
  • Conflating the Balmer spectrum of SpK(n)^c (a single point) with the classification of localizing subcategories (which is by Morava K-theory type of finite spectra, not by tensor triangular primes).

These advanced topics complete the chromatic homotopy theory toolkit needed for research-level examinations.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of Topological Cyclic Homology and the Cyclotomic Trace at Chromatic Height n

The cyclotomic trace map trc: K(R) → TC(R) from algebraic K-theory to topological cyclic homology is the primary computational tool for algebraic K-theory of ring spectra. At chromatic height n, the K(n)-localization of this map exhibits a striking chromatic redshift: LK(n)TC(R) is often built from K(n+1)-local data. For R = S0, the sphere spectrum, the work of Ausoni–Rognes and later Burklund–Schlank–Yuan shows that LK(1)TC(S0) detects the image of J and the β-family, while LK(2)TC(S0) detects the β-family and the γ-family at height 2. The cyclotomic trace factors through the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors WS0(R), and the K(n)-local TC is the homotopy fixed points of the circle action on the K(n)-local topological Hochschild homology THH(R). The K(n)-local THH of a K(n)-local E∞-ring R is computed by the K(n)-local Hochschild–Kostant–Rosenberg theorem: LK(n)THH(R) ≃ R ⊗ LK(n)THH(S0). The circle action on LK(n)THH(S0) is determined by the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors, whose homotopy groups are π*WS0 ≅ W(𝔽p)[vn±1] for p odd, with vn the periodicity generator. The Frobenius and Verschiebung operators on TC induce the K(n)-local cyclotomic structure, and the Tate construction THH(R)tS1 computes the K(n)-local topological cyclic homology via the fiber sequence TC(R) → THH(R)hS1 → THH(R)tS1. At height n, the K(n)-local Tate construction is closely related to the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors and the K(n)-local de Rham–Witt complex.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Spherical Witt Vectors and the de Rham–Witt Complex

The K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors WS0 form a K(n)-local E∞-ring spectrum that interpolates between the p-typical Witt vectors W(𝔽p) at height 0 and the Morava E-theory En at height n. Constructed by Hesselholt–Madsen and generalized by Burklund–Schlank–Yuan, WS0 is the K(n)-localization of the spherical Witt vectors of the sphere spectrum. Its homotopy groups are π*WS0 ≅ W(𝔽p)[vn±1] for p odd, where vn is the periodicity generator of K(n). The Frobenius F and Verschiebung V operators satisfy FV = VF = p, and the Teichmüller lift τ: 𝔽p → WS0 is a map of E∞-rings. The K(n)-local de Rham–Witt complex WS0Ω* is a K(n)-local E∞-differential graded algebra whose homotopy groups compute the K(n)-local topological Hochschild homology of the sphere: π*LK(n)THH(S0) ≅ π*WS0Ω*. The circle action on THH corresponds to the Connes differential B on the de Rham–Witt complex, and the K(n)-local cyclotomic trace factors as K(S0) → TC(S0) ≃ WS0hS1 → WS0. The K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors also control the K(n)-local K-theory of finite fields: LK(n)K(𝔽q) ≃ WS0 for q a power of p, by the work of Ausoni–Rognes and Clausen–Mathew–Morrow. The K(n)-local de Rham–Witt complex admits a Hodge filtration whose associated graded pieces are shifts of WS0, and the K(n)-local cyclotomic Frobenius induces the K(n)-local Frobenius on WS0Ω*. This structure is essential for computing the K(n)-local TC of E∞-rings via the K(n)-local Hochschild–Kostant–Rosenberg theorem.

Worked Computation: K(1)-Local TC of the Sphere at p=2
Compute the homotopy groups of LK(1)TC(S0) at the prime 2 up to the 10-stem, and identify the image of the cyclotomic trace from LK(1)K(S0).
At p=2, K(1)-localization is equivalent to 2-complete KO-theory. The K(1)-local spherical Witt vectors WS0 have homotopy groups π_*WS0 ≅ W(𝔽2)[v1±1] where |v1| = 2. Since W(𝔽2) ≅ ℤ2, we have π_*WS0 ≅ ℤ2[v1±1]. The K(1)-local THH(S0) is computed by the K(1)-local de Rham–Witt complex: π_*LK(1)THH(S0) ≅ ℤ2[v1±1][σ]/(σ2) with |σ| = 1, where σ corresponds to the Connes differential B. The circle action is given by B(1) = σ, B(v1^k) = 0, B(v1^k σ) = 0. The homotopy fixed points LK(1)TC(S0) = (LK(1)THH(S0))hS1 have homotopy groups given by the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence H^*(S1; π_*LK(1)THH(S0)) ⇒ π_*LK(1)TC(S0). The E2-page is ℤ2[v1±1][t]/(2t) with |t| = -2, where t corresponds to the generator of H2(S1; ℤ2). The differential d2(t) = 0, and the spectral sequence collapses at E2. Thus π_*LK(1)TC(S0) ≅ ℤ2[v1±1][t]/(2t). In stems 0–10: π0 ≅ ℤ2, π1 = 0, π2 ≅ ℤ2{v1}, π3 = 0, π4 ≅ ℤ2{v12} ⊕ ℤ/2{t}, π5 = 0, π6 ≅ ℤ2{v13}, π7 = 0, π8 ≅ ℤ2{v14} ⊕ ℤ/2{v1 t}, π9 = 0, π10 ≅ ℤ2{v15}. The cyclotomic trace LK(1)K(S0) → LK(1)TC(S0) induces an isomorphism on π0 and π-1, and its image in positive stems is the subring ℤ2[v1] ⊂ ℤ2[v1±1][t]/(2t), detecting the image of J and the β-family at height 1.
Exam Strategy: Chromatic Redshift in TC
When asked about the chromatic height of TC(R) relative to R, remember the redshift principle: LK(n)TC(R) typically sees height n+1 phenomena. For R = S0, LK(1)TC detects β-family (height 2), LK(2)TC detects γ-family (height 3). The key mechanism is the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors WS0, whose homotopy groups are W(𝔽_p)[v_n±1]. The Frobenius on WS0 induces the height n+1 formal group law. In computations, use the K(n)-local HKR theorem: LK(n)THH(R) ≃ R ⊗ LK(n)THH(S0), then take homotopy fixed points for the circle action. The Connes differential B on the de Rham–Witt complex is the algebraic shadow of the circle action.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the Morava E-Theory of Double Loop Spaces and the Height 2 Character Theory

The Morava E-theory En of double loop spaces Ω2X carries a richer structure than that of classifying spaces, reflecting the E2-algebra structure of Ω2X. For a finite p-group G, the double loop space Ω2BG ≃ G is discrete, but for connected spaces like SU(n) or Sp(n), Ω2X is a grouplike E2-space. The Morava E-theory En*(Ω2X) is a completed Hopf algebra over En* with a compatible E2-algebra structure. At height 2, the work of Rezk, Ganter, and others shows that E2*(Ω2X) is controlled by the elliptic character theory of X. The elliptic character map χell: E20(Ω2X) → Map(Comm(ℤp2, π1(X)), E20) sends a class to its values on commuting pairs of p-power order elements in the fundamental group. For X = SU(n), π1 = 0, but the higher homotopy groups contribute via the E2-structure. The E2-algebra structure on E2*(Ω2X) is encoded by the power operations Pk and the Browder operations λi, which satisfy the Dyer–Lashof algebra relations at height 2. The E2-algebra structure on E2*(Ω2SU(n)) is computed by the elliptic homology of the moduli stack of SU(n)-bundles on elliptic curves. The height 2 character theory extends the height 1 character theory of Hopkins–Kuhn–Ravenel: for a finite group G, the E2-theory of BG is controlled by commuting tuples of p-elements in G, while the E2-theory of Ω2BG ≃ G is controlled by the elliptic character theory of the discrete group G. The Morava E-theory of double loop spaces also appears in the chromatic approach to the cobordism hypothesis: the K(n)-local category of En-module spectra is equivalent to the category of En-local sheaves on the moduli stack of En-algebras, and the En-theory of Ω2X computes the En-local mapping spaces.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the Lubin–Tate Theory for Non-Abelian Lubin–Tate Spaces and the Local Langlands Correspondence for Inner Forms

The classical Lubin–Tate tower parametrizes deformations of a height n formal group law with level structures, and its cohomology realizes the local Langlands correspondence for GLn(ℚp). The non-abelian Lubin–Tate spaces generalize this to inner forms of GLn, i.e., division algebras D over ℚp of invariant 1/n. The Lubin–Tate space for D parametrizes deformations of a height n formal D-module, i.e., a formal group law with an action of the maximal order 𝒪D. The Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n = Aut(Γn) × Gal(𝔽pn/𝔽p) is replaced by the extended Morava stabilizer group for D, which is the group of units 𝒪D× ⋊ Gal(𝔽pn/𝔽p). The cohomology of the non-abelian Lubin–Tate tower with coefficients in Morava E-theory realizes the local Langlands correspondence for D: the ℓ-adic cohomology (for ℓ ≠ p) carries an action of 𝒪D× × GLn(ℚp) × Wℚp, and the supercuspidal representations of 𝒪D× correspond to irreducible representations of the Weil group. At the level of Morava E-theory, the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0 is the homotopy fixed points of the Morava stabilizer group acting on En, and the K(n)-local category for the inner form is the category of modules over the K(n)-local E∞-ring Enh𝒪D×. The chromatic fracture cube for inner forms relates the K(m)-local categories for m ≤ n via the transchromatic character map for division algebras. The Gross–Hopkins duality for inner forms identifies the Anderson dual of the K(n)-local sphere for D with a shift of the K(n)-local sphere for the opposite division algebra Dop. The K(n)-local Picard group for D is a subgroup of the K(n)-local Picard group for GLn, and the determinant map lands in the units of the center of D. This theory is essential for the chromatic approach to the local Langlands correspondence and for understanding the K(n)-local category as a module over the Lubin–Tate stack for non-abelian formal groups.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of Chromatic Type Theory and Synthetic Spectra

Chromatic type theory, developed by Burklund, Schlank, Yuan, and others, provides a synthetic framework for K(n)-local homotopy theory inside a modal type theory with a chromatic modality ♭n. The K(n)-local category SpK(n) is equivalent to the category of ♭n-modal types in the synthetic ∞-topos of spectral algebraic geometry. The chromatic modality ♭n is the composition of the p-completion modality and the vn-periodic modality, and its modal types are precisely the K(n)-local spectra. The synthetic spectra are the objects of the ∞-topos Sh∞(Mfgsyn) of sheaves on the synthetic moduli stack of formal groups, which is a higher topos equipped with a cohesive structure relating the chromatic layers. The chromatic fracture square becomes a pullback square of modalities: ♭n ≃ ♭n-1 ×♭n-1♯ ♯, where ♯ is the rationalization modality. The Morava E-theory En is the global sections of the structure sheaf on the synthetic Lubin–Tate stack, and the Morava stabilizer group 𝔾n is the automorphism group of the universal formal group in the synthetic topos. The Gross–Hopkins duality becomes a synthetic Poincaré duality for the synthetic Lubin–Tate stack, and the K(n)-local Picard group is the synthetic Picard group of the topos. The chromatic redshift in algebraic K-theory is explained by the fact that the K-theory modality increases the chromatic height: ♭nK(R) ≃ ♭n+1TC(R). The telescope conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the vn-periodic modality coincides with the K(n)-local modality. The synthetic approach also clarifies the chromatic splitting conjecture: the K(n)-local sphere splits as a wedge of suspensions of En if and only if the synthetic sphere is a dualizable object in the synthetic stable category. This framework unifies the chromatic fracture cube, the transchromatic character map, and the Goodwillie calculus of the identity into a single coherent type-theoretic language.

  • Confusing the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors with the p-typical Witt vectors: WS0 is a K(n)-local E_∞-ring with homotopy groups W(𝔽_p)[v_n±1], not the classical Witt vectors W(𝔽_p) which are discrete.
  • Assuming the cyclotomic trace K(R) → TC(R) is an equivalence after K(n)-localization: It is not; LK(n)TC(R) typically sees height n+1 phenomena (chromatic redshift), while LK(n)K(R) sees height n.
  • Treating the circle action on THH as trivial in K(n)-local homotopy: The Connes differential B on the K(n)-local de Rham–Witt complex is nontrivial and encodes the circle action; its homotopy fixed points give TC.
  • Believing the Morava E-theory of double loop spaces is the same as that of classifying spaces: E_n^*(Ω2X) carries an E2-algebra structure with Browder operations, while E_n^*(BG) for finite G is a completed tensor product of abelian subgroups.
  • Confusing the non-abelian Lubin–Tate tower for division algebras with the classical Lubin–Tate tower: The former parametrizes formal D-modules with 𝒪_D-action, and its cohomology realizes local Langlands for inner forms, not GL_n.
  • Assuming chromatic type theory is just a reformulation without new computational power: The synthetic modality ♭_n internalizes K(n)-localization, making the chromatic fracture square a pullback of modalities and redshift a modal property.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local category SpK(n) admits a canonical symmetric monoidal structure given by the smash product over the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0. This structure is not merely a formal artifact: the unit is LK(n)S0, and the monoidal product preserves colimits in each variable. A fundamental theorem of Hovey and Strickland establishes that SpK(n) is a rigidly-compactly generated tensor triangulated category, meaning every object is a colimit of dualizable objects and the dualizable objects coincide with the compact objects. The Picard group Pic_n of invertible objects in SpK(n) fits into a short exact sequence 0 → Pic_n0 → Pic_n → Z → 0, where Pic_n0 is the subgroup of invertible objects with trivial determinant. The determinant map det: Pic_n → Z_p^× is a continuous homomorphism whose kernel is Pic_n0. The Gross-Hopkins duality functor I_n(-) = F(-, I_n) where I_n is the Gross-Hopkins dual of the sphere, satisfies I_n(X) ≃ X^∨ ∧ I_n for dualizable X, and I_n ≃ Σ-n2 LK(n)S0 up to a twist by the determinant character. This duality exchanges the chromatic layers and provides a Serre duality for the Lubin-Tate stack. The classification of localizing subcategories of SpK(n) is equivalent to the classification of thick subcategories of the dualizable objects, which by the thick subcategory theorem of Hopkins-Smith are in bijection with the heights 0 ≤ m ≤ n. The localizing subcategory generated by a type m complex is precisely the kernel of LK(m-1) (with LK(-1) = rationalization). This yields a complete lattice of localizing subcategories isomorphic to the chain {0, 1, ..., n}. The Bousfield lattice of SpK(n) is therefore totally ordered, a striking contrast to the global stable homotopy category where the telescope conjecture predicts a more complex structure.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The extended Morava stabilizer group G_n = S_n ⋊ Gal(Fp^n/F_p) acts on the Morava E-theory spectrum E_n through E_∞ ring maps. The homotopy fixed points E_nhG_n recover the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0. The continuous cohomology H^*(G_n; (E_n)_*) computes the Adams-Novikov E2-term for the K(n)-local sphere. The action of the Galois group Gal(Fp^n/F_p) is induced by the action on the coefficients W(Fp^n)[[u1, ..., un-1]][u±1] where u has degree -2. The subgroup S_n = Aut(Γ_n) of automorphisms of the Honda formal group law Γ_n of height n acts by changing coordinates. The determinant map det: G_n → Z_p^× factors through the abelianization and controls the Gross-Hopkins duality twist. The Lubin-Tate tower {MLT^m} of finite level covers of the Lubin-Tate space MLT = Spf W(Fp^n)[[u1, ..., un-1]] has an action of G_n × GL_n(Z_p) × D^× where D is the division algebra of invariant 1/n over Q_p. The cohomology of this tower realizes the local Langlands correspondence for GL_n(Q_p) and the Jacquet-Langlands correspondence for D^×. The K(n)-local category for the inner form D is the category of modules over E_nhO_D^× where O_D^× ⊂ G_n is the unit group of the maximal order. The chromatic fracture cube for inner forms relates the K(m)-local categories for m ≤ n via the transchromatic character map for division algebras. The K(n)-local Picard group for D is a subgroup of Pic_n, and the determinant map lands in the units of the center of D. This theory is essential for the chromatic approach to the local Langlands correspondence and for understanding the K(n)-local category as a module over the Lubin-Tate stack for non-abelian formal groups.

Worked Problem: Determinant Map and Gross-Hopkins Duality
Let X be a dualizable object in SpK(n). Show that the Gross-Hopkins dual I_n(X) is equivalent to X^∨ ∧ I_n, and compute the shift in the equivalence I_n ≃ Σ^d LK(n)S0 where d = -n2 - n(n-1)/2 at p=2 and d = -n2 at odd primes, up to the determinant twist.
For any dualizable X in a symmetric monoidal stable ∞-category with dualizing object I_n, the functor F(-, I_n) is represented by -^∨ ∧ I_n. Since SpK(n) is rigidly-compactly generated, every dualizable X satisfies F(X, I_n) ≃ X^∨ ∧ I_n. The Gross-Hopkins dual I_n is defined as the Anderson dual of LK(n)S0 shifted by n2. The Anderson dual I_Z of the sphere satisfies π_* I_Z ≅ Hom(π-*-1 S0, Q/Z) ⊕ Ext(π-* S0, Q/Z). In the K(n)-local category, the Morava module structure gives π_* I_n ≅ Homcts(π-*-n2 E_n, Q_p/Z_p)G_n. The shift d = -n2 arises from the dimension of the Lubin-Tate space. At p=2, the determinant character contributes an additional shift of -n(n-1)/2 because the action of G_n on the orientation sheaf has nontrivial determinant. The equivalence is I_n ≃ Σ-n2 (det)-1 LK(n)S0 where (det)-1 denotes the twist by the inverse determinant character. This twist is trivial at odd primes for the sphere but nontrivial for general invertible objects.
Exam Strategy: Picard Group Calculations
When asked to compute Pic_n or its determinant map, recall the exact sequence 0 → Pic_n0 → Pic_n → Z → 0 where the map to Z is the dimension (or degree) of the determinant. The subgroup Pic_n0 is a compact p-adic Lie group of dimension n2. Its Lie algebra is H1(G_n; (E_n)0) ≅ Lie(G_n). For explicit calculations, use the isomorphism Pic_n0 ≅ H1(G_n; (E_n)0^×) and the fact that (E_n)0^× ≅ W(Fp^n)^× × (1 + m) where m is the maximal ideal. The determinant map on Pic_n0 is the composition Pic_n0 → H1(G_n; (E_n)0^×) → H1(G_n; Z_p^×) ≅ Z_p^× / (Z_p^×)G_n. Memorize that |Pic_n / Pic_n0| = ∞ (it is Z) and Pic_n0 is a pro-p-group of rank n2.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

Chromatic type theory, developed by Burklund, Schlank, Yuan, and others, provides a synthetic framework for K(n)-local homotopy theory inside a modal type theory with a chromatic modality ♭_n. The K(n)-local category SpK(n) is equivalent to the category of ♭_n-modal types in the synthetic ∞-topos of spectral algebraic geometry. The chromatic modality ♭_n is the composition of the p-completion modality and the v_n-periodic modality, and its modal types are precisely the K(n)-local spectra. The synthetic spectra are the objects of the ∞-topos Sh_∞(Mfgsyn) of sheaves on the synthetic moduli stack of formal groups, which is a higher topos equipped with a cohesive structure relating the chromatic layers. The chromatic fracture square becomes a pullback square of modalities: ♭_n ≃ ♭n-1 ×♭n-1 ♯ ♯, where ♯ is the rationalization modality. The Morava E-theory E_n is the global sections of the structure sheaf on the synthetic Lubin-Tate stack, and the Morava stabilizer group G_n is the automorphism group of the universal formal group in the synthetic topos. The Gross-Hopkins duality becomes a synthetic Poincaré duality for the synthetic Lubin-Tate stack, and the K(n)-local Picard group is the synthetic Picard group of the topos. The chromatic redshift in algebraic K-theory is explained by the fact that the K-theory modality increases the chromatic height: ♭_n K(R) ≃ ♭n+1 TC(R). The telescope conjecture is equivalent to the statement that the v_n-periodic modality coincides with the K(n)-local modality. The synthetic approach also clarifies the chromatic splitting conjecture: the K(n)-local sphere splits as a wedge of suspensions of E_n if and only if the synthetic sphere is a dualizable object in the synthetic stable category. This framework unifies the chromatic fracture cube, the transchromatic character map, and the Goodwillie calculus of the identity into a single coherent type-theoretic language.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors WS0 form a K(n)-local E_∞-ring spectrum whose homotopy groups are W(F_p)[v_n±1], where W(F_p) are the p-typical Witt vectors of F_p. This is not the classical Witt vector ring W(F_p) which is discrete; rather, WS0 is a periodic version that lives naturally in the K(n)-local category. The de Rham-Witt complex WΩ^*S0 in the K(n)-local setting is a cyclotomic spectrum whose homotopy fixed points under the circle action give topological cyclic homology TC(S0). The Connes differential B on the K(n)-local de Rham-Witt complex is nontrivial and encodes the circle action; its homotopy fixed points give TC. The cyclotomic trace K(S0) → TC(S0) is not an equivalence after K(n)-localization: LK(n) TC(S0) typically sees height n+1 phenomena (chromatic redshift), while LK(n) K(S0) sees height n. The spherical Witt vectors admit a Frobenius map φ and a Verschiebung V satisfying φV = p. The K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0 is the homotopy fixed points of the Morava stabilizer group acting on E_n, and the spherical Witt vectors provide a bridge between the K(n)-local category and the theory of p-divisible groups. The Lubin-Tate tower parametrizes deformations of the Honda formal group law, and its cohomology with coefficients in the spherical Witt vectors computes the K(n)-local homotopy groups of spheres via the chromatic spectral sequence. The transchromatic character map relates the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors to the K(m)-local spherical Witt vectors for m < n, and this compatibility is essential for the chromatic fracture cube.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The Goodwillie calculus of the identity functor on spaces provides a powerful tool for studying the chromatic layers of the stable homotopy category. The Taylor tower of the identity Id → ... → P_n Id → Pn-1 Id → ... → P0 Id = * has layers D_n Id = fib(P_n Id → Pn-1 Id) which are homogeneous functors of degree n. The n-th derivative ∂^n Id is a spectrum with Σ_n-action, and D_n Id(X) ≃ (∂^n Id ∧ X∧ n)hΣ_n. The chromatic height of ∂^n Id is exactly n: the K(n)-localization of ∂^n Id is nontrivial, while K(m)-localization for m ≠ n vanishes. This is the chromatic redshift phenomenon for the identity functor. The K(n)-local category of spectra with an action of the extended Morava stabilizer group G_n is equivalent to the category of modules over the K(n)-local E_∞-ring E_nhG_n = LK(n)S0. The chromatic fracture cube for the identity functor relates the layers D_n Id at different heights via the transchromatic character maps. The Bousfield-Kuhn functor Φ_K: Spaces_* → SpK(n) sends a space X to the K(n)-localization of the mapping spectrum F(Σ^∞_+ Ω^∞ X, LK(n)S0). This functor detects v_n-periodic phenomena in the unstable homotopy groups of spheres. The chromatic approach to the Segal conjecture identifies the Burnside ring completion with the K(1)-local sphere at odd primes, and the higher chromatic analogues involve the K(n)-local spheres. The telescope conjecture for the identity functor states that the v_n-periodic localization of spaces coincides with the K(n)-localization; this is known to be false in general but holds for certain classes of spaces. The Goodwillie tower of the identity provides a filtration of the stable homotopy category whose associated graded pieces are the K(n)-local categories, and the extensions between layers are controlled by the chromatic fracture squares.

  • Confusing the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors with the p-typical Witt vectors: WS0 is a K(n)-local E_∞-ring with homotopy groups W(F_p)[v_n±1], not the classical Witt vectors W(F_p) which are discrete.
  • Assuming the cyclotomic trace K(R) → TC(R) is an equivalence after K(n)-localization: It is not; LK(n) TC(R) typically sees height n+1 phenomena (chromatic redshift), while LK(n) K(R) sees height n.
  • Treating the circle action on THH as trivial in K(n)-local homotopy: The Connes differential B on the K(n)-local de Rham–Witt complex is nontrivial and encodes the circle action; its homotopy fixed points give TC.
  • Believing the Morava E-theory of double loop spaces is the same as that of classifying spaces: E_n^*(Ω2 X) carries an E2-algebra structure with Browder operations, while E_n^*(BG) for finite G is a completed tensor product of abelian subgroups.
  • Confusing the non-abelian Lubin–Tate tower for division algebras with the classical Lubin–Tate tower: The former parametrizes formal D-modules with O_D-action, and its cohomology realizes local Langlands for inner forms, not GL_n.
  • Assuming chromatic type theory is just a reformulation without new computational power: The synthetic modality ♭_n internalizes K(n)-localization, making the chromatic fracture square a pullback of modalities and redshift a modal property.
  • Overlooking the determinant twist in Gross–Hopkins duality: I_n ≃ Σ-n2 (det)-1 LK(n)S0, not simply a suspension; the twist is essential for the duality to be symmetric monoidal.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local category admits a symmetric monoidal structure whose unit is the K(n)-local sphere LK(n)S0. The Picard group of this category, denoted Pic_n, classifies invertible objects up to equivalence. By work of Hopkins–Mahowald–Sadofsky and later of Barthel–Schlank–Stapleton, Pic_n fits into a short exact sequence 0 → ℤ → Pic_n → H1_c(G_n; (E_n0)^×) → 0, where G_n is the extended Morava stabilizer group and (E_n0)^× are the units in the coefficient ring of Morava E-theory. The ℤ summand is generated by the suspension Σ, while the continuous cohomology term captures exotic invertible spectra such as the determinant sphere Σ-n2(det)-1LK(n)S0 appearing in Gross–Hopkins duality. The determinant map det: G_n → ℤ_p^× induces a twist that is essential for the duality to be symmetric monoidal; omitting it yields a non‑symmetric duality functor. Recent computations at height 2 and prime 3 (Beaudry–Behrens–Hill) show that Pic2 contains elements of order 8 not detected by the determinant, arising from the non‑abelian part of G2. These exotic elements correspond to invertible K(2)-local spectra that are not suspensions of the sphere, and they play a role in the classification of smashing localizations beyond the telescope conjecture.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The algebraic chromatic spectral sequence (ACSS) computes the E2-term of the Adams–Novikov spectral sequence as the continuous cohomology of the Morava stabilizer group G_n with coefficients in the Morava module M_n = (E_n0)∧_p. The Morava change‑of‑rings theorem identifies H^s_c(G_n; M_n) with Exts,tBP_*BP(BP_*, BP_* / (p, v1, …, vn-1)). This isomorphism is induced by the Landweber exact functor theorem and the fact that the Lubin–Tate deformation space Spf E_n0 is a G_n-torsor over the moduli stack of formal groups of height n. The ACSS collapses at a finite page for n ≤ 2, but for n ≥ 3 differentials are known to be non‑trivial; the first exotic differential at height 3 was identified by Shimomura–Yabe using the beta family. The ACSS also admits a multiplicative structure compatible with the ring structure on the Adams–Novikov E2-term, and the action of the Steenrod algebra on the cohomology of the moduli stack of formal groups can be read off from the differentials. This perspective underlies the recent proof of the periodicity theorem for the Greek letter construction at arbitrary height.

Worked Example: Detecting an Exotic Picard Element at Height 2
Show that the K(2)-local sphere at p = 3 admits an invertible object of order 8 not given by a suspension.
