The extended Morava stabilizer group G2 at p = 3 fits into 1 → S2 → G2 → Gal(F9/F3) → 1, where S2 is the Morava stabilizer group of the Honda formal group law of height 2. The continuous cohomology H1_c(G2; (E20)^×) contains a class represented by the determinant character det: G2 → ℤ3^× composed with the Teichmüller lift of a primitive 8th root of unity in F9^×. This class has order 8 because the image of det on the Galois quotient is the full unit group F9^× ≅ C8. The corresponding invertible K(2)-local spectrum is Σ-4(det)-1LK(2)S0, which is not a pure suspension since the determinant twist is non‑trivial. Its order in Pic2 is exactly 8, as verified by the computation of the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence for G2 acting on E2 (Beaudry–Behrens–Hill, 2020).
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the structure of Pic_n, write the short exact sequence, identify the ℤ summand as suspensions, and explain the role of the determinant twist in Gross–Hopkins duality. For explicit orders at small heights, recall the Galois quotient of G_n and the Teichmüller lift of roots of unity in the residue field.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The transchromatic character map relates the K(n)-local spherical Witt vectors WK(n)S0 to the K(m)-local spherical Witt vectors for m < n. Concretely, for a finite group G, the map E_n^*(BG) → ∏(H) E_m^*(BH) (product over conjugacy classes of subgroups H of G of rank ≤ m) is induced by the inclusion of the height‑m stratum in the Lubin–Tate tower. This map is a ring homomorphism after K(n)-localization and becomes an equivalence after completing at the ideal (p, u1, …, um-1) in the coefficient ring. The character map is the key input in the proof of the generalized character theory of Hopkins–Kuhn–Ravenel, which identifies the K(n)-local homology of classifying spaces with the ring of class functions on the set of commuting n‑tuples of elements of G valued in the Morava E-theory of a point. The transchromatic compatibility of power operations (Dyer–Lashof at height n) with the character map was established by Rezk and later refined by Barthel–Schlank using the language of spectral algebraic geometry. This compatibility implies that the Frobenius on the K(n)-local de Rham–Witt complex corresponds under the character map to the Adams operation ψ^p on the K(m)-local side, providing a bridge between chromatic heights.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The K(n)-local Brauer group Br_n classifies Azumaya algebras in the K(n)-local category up to Morita equivalence. By work of Antieau–Gepner–Heller and later of Mathew–Naumann–Noel, there is an exact sequence 0 → H2_c(G_n; (E_n0)^×) → Br_n → H1_c(G_n; Pic(E_n)) → 0, where Pic(E_n) is the Picard group of the E_n-module category. The first term is the cohomological Brauer group, which at height 1 recovers the classical p‑primary Brauer group of ℚ_p. At height 2 and p = 2, the Brauer group contains a non‑trivial element of order 2 detected by the quaternionic Azumaya algebra constructed from the action of the quaternion group Q8 on the Lubin–Tate space. This element maps to the unique non‑trivial class in H1_c(G2; Pic(E2)) ≅ ℤ/2, showing that the Brauer group is not purely cohomological. The K(n)-local Brauer group also controls the classification of K(n)-local TQFTs via the cobordism hypothesis: fully dualizable objects in the K(n)-local category correspond to separable K(n)-local algebras, and their Morita classes are precisely the elements of Br_n.

The Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category and the Theory of the Chromatic Homotopy Theory of the K(n)-Local Category

The chromatic fracture cube generalizes the chromatic fracture square by iterating localization at the Morava K-theories K(0), K(1), …, K(n). For a spectrum X, the cube has vertices LK(0)∨…∨K(i)X for 0 ≤ i ≤ n, with edges given by the natural maps between successive localizations. The total homotopy limit of the cube is equivalent to the harmonic localization LK(0)∨…∨K(n)X, and the cube is a limit diagram in the ∞-category of spectra. The fracture cube encodes the transchromatic relations among the layers: the fiber of LK(0)∨…∨K(i)X → LK(0)∨…∨K(i-1)X is the K(i)-localization of the K(i-1)-acyclic part of X. This structure underlies the proof of the chromatic convergence theorem for finite spectra, where the tower of localizations LK(0)∨…∨K(i)S0 converges to the p‑completion of the sphere. The cube also appears in the work of Clausen–Mathew on the K(n)-local category as a module over the Lubin–Tate stack: the stack of formal groups of height ≤ n admits a stratification by height, and the fracture cube is the sheaf‑theoretic incarnation of the resulting recollement of quasi‑coherent sheaves.

  • Confusing the determinant twist in Gross–Hopkins duality with a mere suspension shift; the twist is a non‑trivial G_n‑action on the sphere.
  • Assuming the algebraic chromatic spectral sequence collapses at E2 for all heights; it only collapses for n ≤ 2.
  • Treating the transchromatic character map as an isomorphism on homotopy groups without completing at the appropriate ideal.
  • Believing the K(n)-local Brauer group is always isomorphic to H2_c(G_n; (E_n0)^×); the Pic(E_n) term can contribute non‑trivially.
  • Overlooking that the chromatic fracture cube is a limit diagram only after harmonic localization; the plain tower of K(i)-localizations does not converge without the K(0) factor.
  • Misidentifying the Galois quotient of G_n as the full unit group of the residue field at all primes; the quotient is Gal(Fp^n/F_p) and its action on roots of unity depends on p and n.
  • Neglecting the role of the Dyer–Lashof operations in the compatibility of power operations across the character map; they are essential for the Frobenius correspondence.
